DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE:
July 18,
4:30 P.X.
SUBJECT:
Australian Whaling Proposal
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. H. W. Bullock, Second Secretary, Australian Bmbassy
Mr. Fearey, NA
Files
Central
RM/R
This Document Must Be Regurned to
√A-H
COPIES TO:
FE
IR
JK
628/9-1547
Mr. Bullock called at his request on instruotions from
Ambassador Makin to present "at the working level" certain
comments by his Government on the U.S. aide-mémoire delivered
to the Australian Embassy on July 9 rejecting the Australian
Government's proposal to operate a Japanese whaling unit on
behalf of Japan.
Mr. Bullock called attention to the statement 1n the U.s.
note that
"The United States Government would have been ready
CS/E
fully and promptly to state its views on this proposal
at any time during the previous six weeks, but its
position by June 18, when the proposal was placed before
the responsible ofricers of the Department, had reached
a definitive form in the statement already prepared
and approved for reading by General McCoy to the Far
Eastern Commission on June 19."
Mr. Bullock stated that his Government found this statement
inconsistent with that made by General McCoy to the FEo on
June 26 that
"I am informed that the expedition does not leave
Japan until October, so that there is plenty of
An4.
time
Rev
Cat.

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.98)
time for the diplomatio protests and negotiations now
reported to me to go on for a period,"
and that the Australian Government beeause of this statement had
failed to press its proposal in the FEC as etrongly as it othemwisi
might before receiving the U.s. rejeotion..
Mr. Fearey replied that he maw nothing inconsistent in the
two statements. The responsible ofricers or the Department re-
ceived the Australian proposal on June 18 after the U.s. had
taken a definitive position in the matter and communicated that
position to General McCoy. On receiving the Australian proposal
the highest officers of the Department had nevertheless re-
examined the entire matter in the light of the proposal and had
transmitted the proposal to SCAP for his opinion.While the
matter was being reconsidered, and while the British and New
Zealand protests supporting the Australian proposal remained
unanswered by this Government, General McCoy was entirely right
1n stating on June 26 that the matter was still a subjeot of
discussion and negotiations among the concerned Governments and
that action by the FEC could well wait, and indeed must wait,
until these negotiations had been completed, which might not be
for some time. The Australian member was in no way placed at
a disadvantage by the statement as the Australian proposal was
at that very time receiving full consideration by the U.S.
Government and nothing the Australian Ambassador or Government
could have done could have increased the measure of that consideration.
Mr. Fearey pointed out that the Australian Government soarcely
had cause for complaint in the fact that the U.S. Government had
entirely reconsidered its "definitive position", as announoed by
General McCoy on June 19, after receiving the Australian proposal.
The above quoted passage from our aide-memoire of July 9 meant
simply, as stated, that the Government would have been happy to
consider the Australian proposal at any time during the six weeks
before it was actually submitted, while the last two sentences
of the aide-mémoire were intended to make it clear that the proposal
would have had to have been rejected for the reasons there stated
no matter when received. The record seemed to show that the U.B.
position had been entirely consistent, and that we had given the
Australian proposal full consideration, notwithstanding the late
date at which it had been received, by reviewing our entire position
in the light of the proposal.
Mr. Bullock appeared personally to accept ths reasoning and
stated he would convey the reply to Ambassador Makin and to his
Government. He stated that having made the above point his
Government planned no further notes or protests regarding the
expedition, but would take any further steps it had in mind in
the FEC.
NA:RAFearey/pm
RAP
Tiortheast asnan

Enolosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 1291 dated September

subjeot "Japanese Antarotic Whaling".
COPY
United Kingdom Liaison Missiom
in Japan,
British Rabassy,
CONFIDENTIAL
Tokyo.
Ref. No: 82/542/47
lst September 1947
: General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
(Attention: Natural Resources Section,
Fisheries Division.
Through:
Diplomatic Section.)
SUBJECT
Antarctic whaling.
Please refer to General Headquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, Directive No. 1737 addressed to the
Japanese Government.
1. In earlier directives the Japanese authorities were
given details of the various whaling conventions. His
Majesty's Government have not, however, seen any direotive
calling the attention of the Japanese Government to the
Protocol governing the 1947-48 season whioh was drawn up at
Washington on 2nd September, 1946, and would be grateful
for an assurance that this has or will be done.
2. The ships are merely instructed in Directive 1737
to make a daily report on catch to the Japanese Bureau of
risheries. His Majesty's Government trust that separate
arrangements have been made for the prompt communication
of these reports to the International Bureau for Whaling
Statistics at Sandefjord, Norway.
3. The ships are authorized by Directive 1737 to fly
the international code flag E and in certain areas the
Japanese merchant marine flag in addition. His Majesty's
Government strongly suggest that the SCAJAP flag be flown,
as was originally arranged for the last expedition, in
order that it may be made plain that the expedition is
under the control of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers.
4. For a number of seasons the "Discovury Committee"
of His Majesty's Colonial Ofrice has carried on whale-
marking experiments and has issued instructions about the
return of these marks. It would be greatly appreciated by
His Majesty's Government if these instructions, a copy of
which will shortly be available to this Mission, could be
brought to the attention of the Japanese authorities.
8/
A. Gasooigne
Head of Mission

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
Enolesure No. 2 to Despatch No. 129l dated September
1947 from the Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on
subject "Japanese Antarotic Whaling".
COPY
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Seotion
MEMORANDUM
Tokyo, September 16, 1947
Reference is made to United Kingdom Liaison Mission's
memorandum No. 82/542/47, September 1, 1947, subject
Antarctio Whaling.
1. It is believed that the first numbered paragraph
of the memorandum has reference to the protocol signed in
Washington on December 2, 1946, which was brought to the
attention of the Japanese Government, and a copy made
available in June l947 for study. A memorandum has been
prepared and is in process of formal transmission to inform
the Japanese Government of the extension of the pretocol
and to instruct the Japanese whaling industry to conform to
its terms.
2. The Japanese whaling expedition to the Antarotio
being under the suprevision of the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers, responsibility for sending to the
International Bureau for Whaling Statistics in Norway re-
ports required by the International whaling conventions
rests with the Supreme Commander, and during the 1946-1947
season all reports were promptly forwarded. The same pro-
cedure will be followed during the 1947-1948 season.
3. The flag to be flown by the Japanese whaling ex-
pedition to the Antarctic will be the same as that flown
during the latter part of the 1946-1947 expedition (the
international code flag E modified by cutting a triangle
from the end, internationally promulgated for use by
Japanese merchant vessels during the present period).
4. The Japanese were instructed to search for whale
marks of the initial Discovery Committee during the 1946-
1947 season and a careful search was made but no marks were
found. The Japanese Government has been requested to in-
struct their whaling inspectors to search for the marks in
all whales taken during the 1947-1948 seaso
5. Reference memorandum of August 16, 1947 from the
United Kingdom Liaison Mission No. 70/542/47 and paragraph
2 of the reply by the Natural Resources Section regarding
vessels which it is proposed to send on the 1947-1948 ex-
pedition, the additional transport vessel referred to in
paragraph 2 of the reply has been determined upon. It is a
vessel of transport type to be used to carry salted whale
meat and blubber, gross tonnage 9670, name Settsu Maru.
To the
United Kingdom Liaison Mission,
Tokyo.

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.97)
TO
: Signing and initialing officers.
DATB: September 25, 194
SUBJECT:
894.628/9-2547
On September 23, Assistant Secretary Armour asked me to call
Senator Knowland (California) who had expressed to him concern
over the plans of the Department for hearing the views of the West
Coast fishing industry concerning the fishery provision of the
Japanese peace treaty.
I called Senator Knowland who made the following points:
(1) The fishing industry of the West Coast should be heard.
I indicated that I had discussed the problem with representa-
tives of California fishing industry at various times, that the
Pacific Fisheries Conference in San Francisco on September 9
agreed to send a five-man committee to Washington to express the
views of the industry, and that Congresaman Tollefson held a
Congressional hearing in San Francisco on September 10 on the
fishery provisions of the Japanese peace treaty. I informed the
Senator that the officers of the Department would meet with any
representatives of the industry, either singly or in group, to
hear its views.
(2) He feels it essential that the industry be given an opportunity
to comment on the Department's proposals before the treaty is signed
and presented to the American people as an accomplished fact.
S
I said (a) It had been the practice of the Department in other
fisheries matters to consult with the industry as frankly as condition
permitted; (b) No U.S. proposals had been crystallized as yet;
(c) No aefinite plans had been finally formulated as to consultation
with private inaustry on the Japanese treaty; (d) Full consultation
might not be possible, especially in the case of information which
could be used by other delegations to the disadvantage of the U.S.
894.628
delegation.
(3) He feels that in international affairs, the Congress should
stand firmly behind the Administration. However, since the fishery
provisions of the Japanese peace treaty are of such great importance
to the West Coast States, the Senators from those States should be
consulted.
MAY 12 1948
wfs
I said
FILED
ar
m

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
I said that the Department would be glad to discuss the fishery
problem with him at any time he desired, and I offered to call at
his office at his convenience.
He said that he 1s leaving for Europe next week, and that be
wants to be assured that, in his absence, (a) any fishery people
would be freely received by the Department and there would be as
full discussion as appropriate, and (b) he receive a memorandum
from the vepartment outlining the alternative suggestions for
fishery provisions. I assured him of the former and the attached
letter and memorandum fulfill the latter obligation.
In view of the interest on the part of the West Coast fishing industry
and its political power, it would seem advisable to consult the interested
Senators and also Congressman Tollefson (Chairman, Salt Fish Suboommittee,
House Committee on Merchant Marine and Fisheries, who held the San Francisoo
hearing on this subject) on the U.S. proposals with respect to fish before
entering into negotiation of the Japanese treaty. If no other office in
the Department inaicates a desire to bear this in mind, IR will propose
consultation at the appropriate time.
Of possible interest are attached some newspaper items on the
inaustry meeting anu the Congressional hearing on the fishery provisions
of the Japanese treaty.

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
UNCLASSIFIED
Enclosure to Despatch No. 2276 dated October 9, 1947,
from American Embassy, London, England.
Source: The Spectator
Ootober 3, 1947
WHALE-HUNTING FOLLY
By George Godwin
Factory ships and catcher boats of seventeen whal-
ing expeditions will soon be setting forth for the Ant-
arctic. There they will hunt the blue, fin and sperm
whale from Decembar 8th until April 7th, the date upon
which the hunting season closes. This date was fixed
by international agreement embodied in a protooal of
X
November 26th, l945, following the International Whaling
Conference in London and its subsequent ratification in
Washington in the following year. The quota of 16,000
blue whale units was fixed by the protocol as the limit,
to go beyond which would be to endanger the speoies.
(A blue whale unit equals two fin whales or six sei
whales.) The figure of 16,O00 units is not, as has
been suggested, an arbitrary figure, but one arrived
at after careful estimates of the whale population trend
in Antarctic waters. It was fixed by scientists with
the necessary knowledge, and it had, also, the backing
of both Norway and our own Ministry of Agriculture and
Fisheries.
Because of the world's dire need of the products
of the whale for new sources of fats and meat, it was
recently advocated that the protocol restriction should
be suspended. Very little meat is coming to us now
from the Antarctic, perhaps, at most, fifteen to twenty
thousand tons; whereas the oil deliveries are in the
neighbourhood of 300,O00 tons. Whaling experts, British
and Norwegian--and these are the countries chiefly con-
cerned--regard the existing restrictions as essential
for the preservation of the species. Indeed, according
to a statement made quite recently by the Ministry of
Agriculture and Fisheries, scientists in this and other
countries fear that the present limit of 16,O0o blue
whale units is too high. Nor is it estimated that the
oils and fats position in 195l will be better than it
is now. The issue, one of increasing importance for
the worla's depleted larder, is simple.  The destruction
of whale life beyond the fertility rate involves an
inevitable progressive depopulation of the hunting-
grounds, and, perhaps, as in the case of the "right"
or Greenland whale and the humpback of the Antarotio,
in virtual extinction. In this matter there is no
confliot of interests between man and this great sea
mammal. If the whale, obedient to universal law,
struggles to survive, the whale-hunters, those cattle-
men of the great sea ranges, no less desire to preserve
and even to increase the sea herds hunted by them. The
fate of the whale, consequently, is a matter of inoreasing/
importance/

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
UNCLASSIFIED
Page 2.
Encl. to Despatch 2276, October 9, 1947, from London.
importance in a worla of widespread dearth. What,
then, are the facts?
During the war there was virtually no whaling,
and it was anticipated that this respite would result
in a considerable increase in numbers. This increment
did not materialise. On the contrary, both fertility
rate and average adult size showed decreases. The
official pregnancy figures for the season 1934-5 were
1,476 pregnant females in a total catoh of 5,135. Ten
years later there were 253 pregnant females (blue whales)
in a total catch of 1,224. Similar aeclines in the
fertility rate of the fin whales were recorded; for the
season 1934-5 it was 1,509 pregnant females in a total
catch of 4,557, and, a decade later, 800 pregnant fe-
males in a total catch of 3,44l. Between these two
dates the fertility figure showed a decrease varying
from 20.7 per cent to 43.8 per cent. These figures
suggest very strongly that what befell in the Arotio
is now befalling in the Antarctic, namely, that the
decline of the species is being unnecessarily hastened
by man. Not only is there a known and measurable deorease
in fertility, but in quality also. The average size of
a mature blue whale in 1931-2 was eighty-four feet; of
a mature fin whale seventy feet. Those figures are
reduced today to seventy-eight and sixty-seven feet
respectively. There is, then, a known twofold decline--
in fertility and in size--so that it may be said that
the decline of the whale population of the Antarctic has
already begun; had, indeed begun already before the
war, when a hundred per cent increase in the whaling
fleets, with improved methods and equipment, produced
the altogether disproportionate increase in the catch
of only ll per cent.
There appears to be no known cause of the failure
of the species to make some sort of biological recovery
during the respite of the war years. It has been sug-.
gested that the whale's staple food, the krill has been
adversely affected by the sinking of oil tankers during
the war. In Englana the "Discovery" Committee and in
Horway Det Norske Hvalrad are concerned with this and
other problems of whale life in the Antarctic. Writing
in these columns before the war, the present writer
said: "Quite obviously, any restrictive measures
designed to preserve the whale from the extinction which
now threatens it must be aaherea to by all those taking
part in the industry, for complete unanimity is essential
if the present destruction of the whales of the worla
is to be halted." The case for restrictions, irksome
as it may be to a hungry world, is overwhelming, and
opinion in the beat-informed circles is unanimous. To
lift the present limit woula be to invite the same kind
of catastrophe as overtook the cash-croppers of the
Albertan prairie. Nature is not robbed with impunity.
But restrictions to be effeotive must be respeoted
or enforced by legal sanctions, and unfortunately the
Japanese/

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
Pago 3.
UNCLASSIFIED
Encl. to Despatoh 2276, October 9, 1947, from London
Japanese, now again permitted to hunt in the Antarotio
whaling grounds, have in the past always ignored them,
in the same way as, so long as they were able, they
ciroumvented the restrictions on fishing in the great
salmon rivers of the American and British Columbian
Pacific seaboards. Mr. F.F. Anderson, Director of
Fisheries for Australia, after visiting the whaling
fleets last year, reported that the Japanese expedition
was detrimental to the industry since, while ignoring
regulations made by international agreement, it also
operated uneconomically and inefficiently. The Japan-
ese operate their whaling ships with crews of boys and
youths, who lack the necessary experience to identify
males from females or to judge maturity, and laok,
also, the technical knowledge necessary for efficient
factory economy. Mr. Anderson quotes the case of the
" Hashidate Maru" which left Japan for the Antarotio
hunting grounds in November last year. There she
hunted for seventy-two days, securing a total catch of
490 whales--297 blue, 189 finback, four sperm. From
this catch her crew secured an average yield, per blue
whale unit, of only 9.57 tons of oil, compared with 19
tons obtained by whalers of other nations.
The Japanese, lacking proper equipment and the
essential technical knowledge, are mainly concerned
with whale meat, for this has long been a stapla article
of Japanese diet, and unrestricted whale-hunting in
the Japanese Pacific waters has so much reduced the
whale stocks there that Japan has been driven to send
its whalers to the great Antarctic hunting grounds.
Japan had five fleets in the 1938-9 season. They soo-.
ured 857,625 barrels at a time when the catches of
the British and Norwegian expeditions were falling. 
How, it may be asked, with inferior equipment and lack
of technical knowledge and sound experience, was it
aone? The answer is: By hunting indiscriminate!y
mature whales and the immature and females with suck-.
lings or calves. Only the experienced whaler knows,
from the blow and the dimensions of the whale des-
cribing a swift curve as it rises and dives, sex and
approximte size. The Japanese methods obviously
hasten the decline of the species; yet experience has
shown in this fielu, as in other branches of industry,
the Japanese will neither cooperate nor conform to
accepted practice. For these adequate reasons it may
well be deplored that Japan has been granted permits
to hunt again in the last remaining whale preserve
left in the seas of the earth.
To sum up, the preservation of the whale population
of the Antarctic is essential for the maintenance of
the oil and meat supplies now needed to replenish the
world's empty larder. The retention of restrictions
then, is a matter of international concern and interest.
Last, the strengthening of legal sanctions against
those who disregard, through ignorance or bad faith,
international agreements designed to preserve the species
babngs to the practical problem of restriction en-
forcement.

The whaling companies thus

October 17, 1947
Mr. W. E. S. Flory
Assistant Chief
International Resources Division
State Department
Washington, D.C.
Dear Sir:
The writer recently returned from Japan, having proceeded there under military permit
with the first group of business representatives from the United States.
The preliminary reason for the trip was to try to arrange a source of supply for
frozen Albacore for our cannery in the Pacific Northwest. The present sources of
supply are not sufficiently dependable to warrant year around operations. Un-
fortunately, no immediate business resulted as there were no supplies of the
particular type of fish we wanted, nor was it the right season for Albacore. How-
ever, whilst in Japan we had many conferences with owners and operators of the tuna
fishing vessels and some of the crews, and some interesting suggestions wara discussed
at that time. The operators do not seem to have sufficient nor modern up  hatet
receiving stations. They are obsolete or not available. Of course, fishing areas
are restricted. There is some possibility of these being extended but the Japanese
told us that even though they were extended to the north they would not be too
happy about going very far in that direction. Another point brought to our attention,
which was of much more interest, was the report of rich fishing areas quite adjac-
ent to the mandated islands, Saipan in particular. We would be very much interested
in this. Of course, to establish the industry in Saipan it would necessitate a
CS/A
substantial investment in shore facilities, such as, refrigeration stations, cold
storage holding facilities and the like.
We are quite interested in such a proposition and would like to kmow the proper party
in the International Resources Division with whom we could disuuss this matter. We
hope we are correct in addressing you on the subject and in such event we would like
your assistance in suggesting just how we should go about this. You will no doubt
want considerable detailed information as to our firm and our ideas and consider-
able other data, and if you would be so kind as to let us have your comments and
suggestions we would like to have them. We realize that perhaps under present
conditions and maybe until such time as a peace treaty is completed, nothing could
actually be done but we could probably do considerable preliminary work.
Yours truly,
OCT 2 11947

DEL MAR CANNING CO.

SUBJECT:
Expansion of Japanese Fishing Area.
PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Herrington, SCAP.
Mr. Castleman, In - Fisheries and Wildlife Branch.
894.628/10-1747

IR - Mr. Kennedy and ITP - Mr. Schaetzel
Mr. Herrington visited the Branch on October 17, 1947 in order to
discuss possible modifications of FEC-095. Mr. Herrington indicated
his belief that the Supreme Commander would dislike any modification
whicn would deprive him of any of the powers granted under FEC-035.
Herrington, however, stated that ne would personally support either
of the two following modifications if they were considered important
An
in bettering international relations:
(1) expansion of present area to one which SCAP will soon propose
to U.S. Navy, no further expansion towards the coast of any country
without prior permission of that country; or
(2) uniform (exclusion) line off all land masses in Pacific not
exceeding 200 miles - SCAP not to expand beyond such line without
prior permission of country concerned.
No indication was given Herrington of line to be followed in FEC.
He was, however, asked if it would be possible for him to informally
discuss possible modification of FEC-035 with the Diplomatic section and
inform us of their views. He said he would do so when he returned to
Japan.

DEPARTMENT OF THE AR
November
NOV 5 1947
Assistant Secretary of State for Occupted
Room 4033, New State Department Building,"
Washington 25, D. C.
Dear Mr. Saltsman:
Recently we had the privilege of conferring personally
with Mr. W. C. Herrington, Chief of the Fisheries Division,
Natural Resources Section, GHQ, SCAP. Although he emphasised
that the principal limitations on Japanese fish production
are nets, twine, and cordage, he did point out that the over-
894.628/11-
all limitation on production is the supply of fish in the
authorized area.
Mr. Herrington claims that the best information avail-
able indicates that the maximum production which can be obtaimed
from the present authorized area is about 3,300,000 motrio tons
annually. It would be very advantageous to extend the area
open to Japanese fishing in order to make the most officient
use of present gear supplies and to provide for expanded opera-
tions as additional supplies of gear become available.
It appears that there are three principal possibilities
for expansion of Japanese fishing operations: First, the area
to the north along the Kuril Islands, Kamchatka, Okhotsk Bea,
and East Coast of Siberia, where the Japanese fished prior to
World War II; second, the areas in the Yellow and East China
Sea; and, third, the high seas areas east and south of Japan
where tuna was taken by the Japanese in pre-war years.
Mr. Heringtontells us that as to the first area referred
to above, a staff study was completed by his Fisheries Division
sometime ago, and that a proposal based on this study has been
referred to the Diplomatic Section of SCAP and the State Dopart-
ment for discussion with the Russians. To date there has Deen
no determination and it is anticipated that there willbe no
favorable Russian reaction to this proposal.
CSA
The second area referred to above has been thoroughly
studied and discussed informally with Korea. It is reported
that the Korean fishing industry, Government officials, and
the U. S. advisors to the Korean government, are strongly
opposed to any extension of Japanese fishing in this direction.
Apparently this opposition is based on the contention that
Korea desperately needs these supplies of fish and that if
3B
35/2

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
fishing equipment is available for such fishing eperatiems
because of the tense political situation in Kerea, further
action on this matter has been suspended pemding the results
of efforts to enable the Koreans to make use of Yellew Sea
supplies. Mr. Herrington's latest information was that this
subject would be reviewed sometime early in 194s, with the
understanding that if the Koreans do not make use of these
fisheries, action will be resumed to opem this area to the
Japanese.
The expansion of Japanese fishing in the third area,
namely, to the east toward Midway and south toward the equator
would reopen areas fished by the Japanese prior to the war and
not presently utilized by other nations. It is estimated that
this area would yield about 100,000,000 pounds ef albacose and
tuna, much of it available for export at high prices. We
gather that the primary considerations bearing on favorable
action to this proposal are security and international com-
plications.
Both from the point of view of increasing the Japamese
food supply, by way of providing more fish, and making it possible
to increase Japanese exports, it is evident that some defimite
action should be taken in the matter of getting these fishing
areas extended for the benefit of the Japanese. The first
proposal no doubt is the most unlikely because of the anticipated
unfavorable reaction of the Soviets. However, that should mot
stop us from actively and energetically pursuing the second
and third proposals.

your attention and let us have your views as to what the possi-
bilities are of obtaining favorable action at an early date.

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DalR
DATE: Novembef 20, 1947
SUBJECT:
Aide-Memoire on Japanese Whaling
894.628/11-2047
PARTICIPANTS:
Norwegian Embassy - Mr. Lykke
IR - Mr. Flory
IR - Mr. Castleman
COPIES TO:
IR - Mr. lory
IR - Mr. Himbert
Mr. Lykke, Counsellor of the Norwegian Embassy visited the
Branch on November 20, 1947 in order to present the Department with
an aide-memoire on Japanese whaling. Mr. Lykke axplained that the
aide-memoire was concerned with only the following two points:
(1) an analysis by the International Bureau for Whaling Statistics of the
Japanese whale oil production (indicating Japanese oil production was
very low despite the retention of meat and blubber), and (2) bringing
to the attention of American authorities certain violations charged
against one of the Japanese 1946/47 Antarctic expeditions by the Australian
observer. Mr. Lykke did not discuss the above points in any detail since
his Government had apparently only instructed him to bring them to the
attention of this Government.
CS/V
Mr. Lykke,in passing, mentioned the bi-zonal negotiation for surplus
Norwegian fish and said that after meeting with Mr. Acheson he had
cabled his Government that any initiative at this time would apparently
have to come from Norway.
Mr. Lykke asked the status of the December 2, 1946 Protocol and was
894.628/11
informed that Australia, France, and the USSR had not as yet accepted.
FEB 26 1913

A number of Japanese newspapers, specifically, the
Nippon Times, Tokyc Times and others, published on 2
October 1947 a communication to the effect that permission
was given the Japanese by General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander, Allied Fowers, Fecific, to install radar equip-
ment on whale-boats sailing on whale-fisning trips to the
Antarctic. The colmunication also pointed out that the
radar equipment to be installed on the whale-boats will
be removed fron the ships of the former Jepanese Navy.
I shall greatly appreciate it if I be informed as to
whether tne above mentioned communication is true, and if
it is, then why did General Headquarters, Supreme Commonder,
grant the Japanese permission tc use radar equipment
despite the decision of the Far Lastern Commission,
written into the document FLC-084/21 dated 14 august 1947,
according to which rader is classified under combat equip-
ment (sub-article "a"(2), article 2 of the above mentioned
document) and its possession by the Jepanese 1s prohibited
on the basis of sub-article "a"(1), article 9 of the same
document.

Sirs
Reference is made to your letter of October 20, 1947, re-
questing certain information concerning the installation of
radar equipment aboard Japanese whaling vessels, and refer-
ring to thet part of a policy statement of the Far Eastern
Commission which relates to the use of combat-equipment
products.
In the case under discussion, possession of radar equip-
ment by Japanese (in the sense of ownership) is not con-
templated. The simple navigational type of Japanese rader
•quipment installed aboard the whaling vessels under refer-
ence is similar in every respect to that used aboard Japanese
repatriation ships and will be used under the same conditions
of continuous supervision by representatives of General
Headquarters, Supreme Commender for the Allied Fowers. The
Commander Naval Forces, Far Last, will supervise the eventu-
al disposal of equipwent installed in whaling vessels.
Furtnermore, the Japanese Government has been informed
that the instaliation of such equipment will not be con-
siiered a basis for so equipping other Japanese ships nor
for any research, development, or manufacture of radar
equipment.
Vory truly yours,
W. J. Sebald

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
UNCLASSIFIED
Enclosure to Despatch No. 2737 dated December 1. 1047,
from American Embassy, London, England.
Source: The Observer
November 30,1947
HUNTING THE WHALE
Py David Gunston
The third post-war whaling season, opening in the
Antarctic on December 8, is noteworthy both for the number
and variety of ships engaged in it, and for the contro-
versy it has stirred over the future of whale stooks.
The campaign is the largest seen in the South Polar seas
since 1939, and the hopes of food-starved Europe will
anxiously follow the reat fleet of factory-ships from
five nations and the 175 fast catchers in attendance.
The expected yield is 3,000,000 barrels of oil, and a
great deal of whale meat.
There are 17 separate but co-ordinated expeditions;
nine are from Norway, as the leading whaling nation; four
from Britain, two from Japan and one each from Holland
and Russia. Our fleet has a new vessel equipped with the
latest refrigeration plant in which to bring home whale
meat.
Three land stations are to be used to organise the
hunting, and up-to-date developments in marine radar,
long-range radio, and spotting by aircraft will assist
the vast armada. It includes several modern factory-
ships completed since the war, which took heavy toli of
these specialised vessels.
EXPERTS DIFFER
The 13,000 whalemen have until April 7 to secure
their maximum quota of 16,000 blue whale "units"--one
blue whale equalling for this purpose 2 fin or 6 Sei
whales. This maximum figure, which applied also to last
season's catch, was fixed by international agreement in
London in 1945. It was not reached in 1946, but may well
be reached this time.
Mr. Strachey, replying to questions in the Commons
on November 10, said that "a catch of such dimensions
should not, in the opinion of experts, endanger future
supplies." He admitted, however, that experts differ
on the question, as indeed they do. Some even hold that
a catch of 16,O00 units will be fatal to the future main-
tenance of a worthwhile whale population in the Antarotic.
It was/

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
UNCLASSIFIED
Page 2.
Encl. to Desp. 2737, December 1, 1947, from London
It was thought that the suspension of whaling
during the war would enable the whales to inorease their
numbers sufficiently to make cood the reckless ravages
of the immediate pre-war years. But nature is not
always obedient to man's wishes, and the respite has
proved to bo insufficient. A blue whale, the largest
and most important species, can breed at the age of two,
but each young whale means a full year's gestation, and
abnormally severe weather during several war-time winters
sorely reduced the expected rate of recovery.
Ever since 1934, indeed, there has been a marked
decline in fertility and aduit size, and the decline 1s
still coing on. The evidence strongly suggests that to-
day, under the stimulus of a world food shortage, man is
invading these potentially rich waters with his ships and
his explosive harpoons too soon. The general stock pos-
ition has been grcwing steadily worse for many years,
and long before the war skilled whalemen gave warning
that to continue on such a scale would bring the whole
international industry to a speedy end, ruined by the
rush for easy profits. But little heed was taken.
TUE JAPANESE
There is anxiety also over the role of the Japanese
in this campaign. In the face of international disapproval,
Japanese whalers have American permission to employ their
two fleets within the main armada, apparently without any
shipboard control. In the past, when whaling was a general
free-for-all, Japanese whalers earned a very bad name, and
there seems to be no reason to assume that their methods
have improved. Their catching-ships are manned largely
by boys and youths, who may be efficient enough at killing
whales but lack the experience and knowledge needed to
distinguish between males and females, or to recognise
pregnant and immature specimens. They cannot, for example,
be trusted to observe the rule that young whales under 40
feet in length should be laft alone. Since the average
yield from their factory-ships is well below half that of
other countries, the agreed quota could easily be obtained
without their participation.
Whale oil makes up about 10 per cent of the total
world production of edible oils, so the present campaign
is obviously of great importance for the world's fat
supplies. But a far-sighted attitude towards this vital
ocean harvest is no less essential. We cannot afford to
risk losing the harvest altogether by reckless hunting,
however urgent our immediate needs.

Referring to the first question, r. Flory explained that it is a rule
of international law, recognized by the United States, that, in the abeenoe
of treaty or agreement, American fishing vessels have the right to operato
without interference in any portion of the high seas off the coasts of my

the exercise of that right offthe coasts of the Union of Socialist Soviet
Republics. The right, however, was a right of the United States of Amerio
and not of its inaiviaual citizens, and it is exercised by an individual
citizen only by virtue of the tacit approval of this Government. The
question of how far tnis Government will go to assert or protect such a right
as exercised by a citizen was, therefore, not only a matter of law but also
one of policy. Mr. Flory referred to the Fresidential Proclamation of

Proclamation, he pointed out, conceded the right of any nation to establish
conservation zones oif its coasts providing that recognition is given to
interest of nationals of the United States which may exist in such areas.

November 22nd, 1947
General George C. Marshall
Secretary of State
State Department
Washington, D. C.
My Dear Sir:
uring my recent trip to Japan, negotiations were entered
into and temporary qualified agreements reached between a large
Japanese firm ana ourselves.
Prior to the recent War, the Japanese were not only the largest
producers of canned crab, but also produced the best quality. The catch-
ing and canning of crab was cone on floating canneries; practically
all of whicn were sunk auring the War by American Naval action.
The Japanese fished tne waters off northern Japan, as well
as the waters off tne Kurile Islanas, Siberia and Kamchatka. Today
the Japanese are restricted to a very narrow belt of water close to
their Northernmost Island of Hokkaido. The uifference this restricted
fishing area will make is best indicated by comparing their prewar
production of close to one million cases of canned crab with their
estimated present production of 30,000 cases of crab annually.
Even tuough the Japanese were able to fish where they pleased
today, their economic situation is such that they ao not have financial
resources with whicn to replace the large floating canneries.
Our firm has proposed to the Japanese firm, which before the
War was the largest in Japan connected with the canning of crab, that
we furnish up to five large floating canneries completely equipped.
The ships would remain American owned, fly the American flag and be
operated by American officers. The Crew would be Japanese. The Japmese
woula furnish the experience and "know now" and the operating capital.
Our firm and the Japanese firm would benefit from any profits on an
agreed basis.
The American State epartment comes into the pictture because
it is our uesire to fish in those waters now denied to Japanese ships.
It goes without saying that all business arrangements between the
Japanese firm and ourselves would be subject to the approval of
General McArthur
With all the above as background, we therefore place before
you the following questions, to wnich we earnestly request early, blunt
and clear answers.

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.98)
George C. Marshall
-2-
May our ships fish for crab anywhere in the world that w
desire providing our ships remain on the high seas and do not
the territorial waters of any Nation where we do not have a permls
to enter? In the event tnat the answer to our first question is that
it is the policy of the State Department that American ships may
anywhere they so aesire on the nigh seas, we then propose the follow
ing question.
In the event our floating ca. neries, remaining out of terri-
torial waters of the Soviet Union but fishing in Northwest Pacifie waters
adjacent to anu nearby such territorial waters were stopped, or bomrded.
or hindered in any manner, or forced to proceed to a Russian Port, what
then would be the State epartment's reaction, and could we feel free
to uemand American Naval protection to prevent the above mentioned
actions? It should be clearly understood by you that these queetions
are prefaced by our understanuing that inside Russian territorial
waters we are unuer lussian control, and that it would be our most
rigid policy to remain outside a three or twelve mile limit, whichever
aistance is declared to be the width of territorial waters.
It is our earnest and urgent request that our questions be
answered at the very earliest possible moment.
I should personally like to inform you that there is at
least one American businessman who understands some of the probloms with

what you do some will praise you anu some will damn you. Simply do

December 26th, 1947
Mr. Alexei P. Korobochkin
Counselor of Embassy
Embassy of USSR
Washington, D. C.
My Dear Sir:
We have been referred to you by our State Department
concerning a matter which we discussed with them.
It is our desire to fish for crab in the waters of the
Bering Sea, the Sea of Okhotsk and the waters East of Sakhalin.
In all cases our ships would remain beyond territorial waters
(twelve miles) of the USSR.
Our ships would be American owned and registered, would fly
the American flag and would have American officers and operating crow.
However, by reason of tneir past experience, the canning personnel
would be Japanese. The ships themselves would be floating canneries.
The overwhelming portion of the canned crab would be sent to the U.S.
The State Department has informed us that so long as we
remain outside of the-territorial waters of the USSR, we would be
entitled to use the freedom of the seas and in the event our froedom
were violated by individuals wno were citizens of another nation
or by official personnel of another nation, we would be entitled
to the full diplomatic and legal protection of our own government.
Because the UsSR and U.S.A. seem to have plenty of excuses
at the present time for friction and argument, it is the desire of
our Company to aud no further reasons for friction by causing an
intemational incident. Witn the full knowledge of our State Dep
we have audressed this letter to you to aetermine the answors to the
following two questions:
1. What would be the attitude of your government relative to
our fishing the waters above described provided we remmin out-
side the territorial waters of the USSk?
2: Would the government of the UsSR be favorably inelined to
grant us permission to fish inside its territorial waters in
the general areas above mentioned?
It shoulu be aistinctly understood that our operations would
be purely commercial, would have no government connestion whatsoever

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.98)
and our actions could have no military or naval signifi
would have no objection to a representative of the UsSR
board each of our ships during the period when our ships would
be in the above mentioned waters.
May we have your answers at the very earliest possible date.
Sincerely,

THE HUBERT D. LONG COMPANY

the Japanese could fish anywhere they wished as long as they

verify this. Mr. Castleman told Mr. Karp that this was not
correct and that any permission for Japanese fishing operations
outside of the above defined area would have to come from the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers. Mr. Karp said that he
was pleased to have his erroneous impression corrected.
Mr. Karp asked if there were any indication that the
Supreme Commander intended to expand the above defined area in
the near future. Mr. Castleman replied that the Department had
CS/A
received no word which would indicate that such action was being
taken by the Supreme Commander.

SECRET
TO
Mr. Saltzman
DATE: January 4, 1948
H. W. Moseley, o Mky
FROM
SUBJECT:
Status of U.S. Policy for Japanese Fishing.
During your absence the controversy over the extension of
Japanese fishing rights was temporarily settled by agreement to:
1. Advise SCAP within 30 days (on Jan. 15) or upon prior
approval by the FEC that he can permit the extension of the
present Japanese fishing area eastward and south as far as the
Trust territory (the limits last proposed by the Army).
6
2. Not later than Feb. 15, 1949, SANACC in consultation
h
with Interior will consider approval by the U.S. to expansion
of Japanese fishing into the entire southern area (The Trust
Territory) recommended by SCAP.
Castleman (U/FW) advises me that the above proposed
This Docu!
action is meeting opposition in the FEC only from the Chinese
(who want the present line pushed back from their area) and
the Filipinos who strongly object because the above proposed
extension would bring the Japanese within 30 miles of the Luzon
coast. Castleman feels that the Filipinos have a good case and
-448
that probably we should agree to modify the proposed extension
to satisfy them. This could be done by obtaining SANACC agree-
ment to a minor modification to the agreed extension. Castleman
ed
says that this would involve only a minor change and that Army
should not object. It is probable that he will prepare such a
proposal for you to consider for SANACC introduction within the
next few days.
Considerable difficulty is also being encountered in the FEC over
that part of the proposed policy which states that if such expansion
will involve fishing in an area in which any country has "a direct
concern," arrangements shall be made between SCAP and such country.
The approved SANACC paper provides that the question of a definition
of direct concern can be referred to SANACC if the over-all policy
is held up by FEC as a result of controversy over this term (p. 59
of SANACC 99/11).
5

SANACC 99/11.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
NATURAL RESOURCES SECTION
HGS/WCH/CMA/ss
NR 442 (22 Jan 48)F1
22 Janmary 1948
MEMORANDUM FOR: Record
SUBJECT:
Letter from Lt. Col. Winston Concern-
ing Attitude of Allied Observers Ac-
companying the Japanese Antarctic
Whaling Expedition.
The following letter was received from Lt. Col.
Winston accompanying the Japanese Antarctic Whaling Ex-
pedition forwarded to Tokyo aboard the Banshu Maru and
received 20 January 1948 by Fisheries Division, Natural
Resources Section:
"Aboard the Hashidate Maru
In the Antarctic
18 December 1947
Lat. 64028is, Long.155016'm
"Have just learned from Terry that one of the
Banshu transports will leave on the 19th or 20th for
Tokyo. will outline a few points of interest as the
time allows. I hope the office has not been too eriti-
cal of activities as reported in our interchange of in-
formation to date. To avoid unpleasantness, I have
referred one or two matters to you, that I could normal-
ly handle, to indicate to you something of the situation.
Have to live with these people for a few more months
(you know). On the whole our associations have been
most enjoyable, and the whole operation absorbs my
interest from time of awakening until I retire. Here
are (my) comments:
"The Australian: His 71 days in the Antarctic in
1946-47 have made him such an expert that the Norwegian
doesn't care to become engaged in discussion with him on
technical matters, except to remark to me afterwards,
'Of what possible interest can that fellow have in this
expedition. As he says of your SCaP representative last
year--he is rarely out on the deck.' I haven't always
fared so well with Coonan--hence my official WImm. If
it didn't sound sensible, it was because we were ep-
posite Australia, and I didn't want to tip off what
our

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
No. 61, Janmary 27, 1948.
-2 -
our friend may have in mind. Witness the following
dialogue on 24 Nov.
"We were at lunch when the Australian inquired if
it would be possible to communicate directly with his
wife in Australia. We were then about l20 miles offshore
I replied, 'It is necessary for all communication to go
through Tokyo and our respective missions.' 'Well,' he
said meaningly, 'I knew that before I left Australia,
but I wanted to get it from you. It seems a bloody
shame,' he muttered, 'to be so close home and not be able
to send a message to my wife.' Then he became surly,
'Just why can't I communicate direct since it can be
done without changing wave lengths,' and quoting mocking-
ly, 'Impossible to grant your request, the frequencies
already have been assigned!'
":Well, we're here as representatives of our re-
spective governments,' I explained, 'and every release
must go /through/ them.'
"'You mean through your own,' was his reply, 'You
censor every message that goes off this ship.
didn't know then that he had tried to get Capt. Miyata
to connect him with the mainland the day before and was
referred to me.)
"'There is no censorship,' I assured him. 'Not a
word is changed on any message sent from this ship or
any handled by my section.'
"About this point Comdr. Sundt interrupts to inquire,
'Why, is there a war on?'
"'Aw, I knew it would be this way before I left
Australia,' continued Coonan.
"Somewhat nettled by his bantering attitude, I in-
quired, 'Then what's the point of asking me?'
"'I just wanted to get it from you,' was his sullen
reply. 'Just give me one good reason why I can't com-
municate direct with my wife. I don't want to communi-
cate with the bloody government.
"I was sitting back of the table on one of those
corner seats where getting out isn't too easy and besides
I had decided to have the last word on the subject,
'There is no reason why I should explain the logic of
the thing when instructions have been issued.'
"'They'll answer calls from land stations, won't
they?' he demanded.
"'of course, they've had their instructions on such
matters.'
"iTbey
SECRET

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclesure to Tokye's
No.61, Jammary 27, 1948.
"'They bloddy well better reply or by God . . .
he lapsed into silence, leaving the sentence incomplet
and as an afterthought, 'They're glad enough to receive
the weather reports!!'
"Here Sundt mentioned that Norwegian gunners en
Russian catcher boats last year got the catcher beat
captains to send messages direct to Morwegian factery
ships in the area who in turn relayed them home.
"All this is background for my official WINE. End
of dialogue, except that he remained sullen and unsocia-
ble toward me until we had passed Australia. Sundt
seemed concerned over the matter too and was cool toward
me for several days.
"Several days ago I made a mental note that Coonan
hadn't forgotten about his failure to plug the Australian
other. In fact he let the cat out of the bag. I had
loaned him my copy of National Geographic describing
Amiral Byrd's Expedition, 'High Jump' to the Antaretic
and Little America last year. 'That Byrd is certaimly
a publicity hound,' Coonan commented, 'and I'm down here
and can't even get a word on the radio or a line in the
pper. It all helps a fellow get along.'
"I learned that Coonan had wangled a deal through
Capt. Miyata and Mr. Ogino, general manager, to have a
subordinate in the business section keep his catch log
book for him. I instructed Ogino to have the book
returned to Coonan and informed him that he would be
held responsible for authenticating any reports issued
for the ships, and they were to be released only to me.
Coonan did this same thing last year which may account
for some of his left-handed statistics. Ogino said he
thought it was 'OK' because Coonan did it last year.
"This deal really 'smoked' 'ole' Coonan out because
up to then neither he nor Sundt were willing to let me
see their log books.
"The Japanese have many forms similar to those in
the catch log book, but they are not complete or easy to
use in all instances for statistical purposes. This was
the second deliberate run-around Coonan has attempted.
I'm trying to keep my mouth shut and my eyes and ears
open, but I think you get the general idea.
"At dinner the next day after the log book episode,
I expressed my regret because a certain production re-
port requested by Coonan had been delayed. 'What was
the matter,' he inquired, 'wasn't the production high
enough to suit you?' Ignoring the innuendo, I patiently
(?) explained that, through clerical inadvertence, ton-
nage figures had been entered for the number of boiler
fillings.
"Have
SECRET

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tekyo's
No. 61, Janmary 27, 1948.
"Have I made my point?
"The Morwegian: Sundt is a fine gentleman and,
with one exception, has been very cooperative and
generous with suggestions. His comments have helped
me on several occasions to head off violations or mis-
takes in interpretations. Once I inquired if I might
examine his catch log book, but he was very unresponsive,
saying, 'we didn't come here to tell these people how
we do things but to observe how they do the job!' For
all this, I feel that he is honest, straightforward
and will submit a report based on fact, of course,
from a Norwegian interpretation of the conventions. I
did not warn you ahead of time about his code message
because your official SIX stated 'No intention of
censorship.' Besides it was a sort of defensive measure;
I knew that if it were incorrect, you would catch it and
let me know. I don't believe in passing the buck but
sitting across the table from one fellow who is delib-
erately unsociable--won't meet your eye for as much as
eight days at a time--isn't to be encouraged when one
is on a floating island for six months. By working on
the problem of indirection, Sundt is opening his code
book to me and showing me some parts of his catch log
book.
"Please excuse some of my inconsistencies. There
may be a reason for them down here.
"The Japanese: According to them and to the way
they started out, they have not in the past marked the
tails of whales according to conventions. No mark to
indicate number of whales killed by catcher boat on day
killed--only to know total killed for the season.
"They have assigned serial numbers when processed
and not when killed. Sundt started making inquiries
about wireless log the first day, and I caught on. Re-
viewing paragraph 13, Schedule, Conventions, 2 December
1947, I had the Japanese change their previous practice.
I am satisfied that Sundt's report would have been un-
favorable on these two points because there is a slight
chance for manipulation under the other methods. Sundt
is not unreasonable in his inquiries or suggestions, but
it is obvious that he is a man of experience as an
inspector.
"The Japanese always have some damn custom to fall
back on or to introduce. They keep Tokyo time on the
ship but wanted me to decide when the whaling season
would start. I informed them that GMT for the Antarctic
would govern, which is about one hour earlier than Tokyo
time.
"Rations: It is slightly amusing that Kobe sent
us rations for four instead of two until one figures up
the value which is about $2223.00 for the two of us!!
They say they computed at 1t rations per man. That's
right!! Then when they were told to separate the ship-
ping documents in order to bill the Norwegian Govern-
ment, but the dumb dodos just duplicated the previous
order
SECRET

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
No. 61, January 27, 1948.
order. (Must have saved paper work.) If I had beef
two meals a day, there is enough for 226 days; there
is enough frozen pork sausage, alone, at two meals a
day for 154 days; chicken, turkey and spareribs com-
bined to last l66 days; or good 'ole' frozen ham for
118 days. All of this for one man. Hells bells, I
estimate that there are enough perishables aboard to
last one man, based on two meals a day, for 726 days!
This does not include eggs, frozen vegetables, and
fresh fruit. I would not complain except that Kobe
Base stated, so I recall, that no perishables would be
accepted on a 'turn in'. This means that 400-500
pounds of fresh frozen meat will be condemned because
of their own inadvertence. This can be explained,
perhaps, to our authorities but not to the Morwegian
Government.
"Photography: We're getting some good pictures,
I believe, but I find that my l6 mm kodachrome which
was packed for me has an expiration date of 1946! Also
176 rolls of 35 mm (B&W) has an expiration date of 1946.
Maybe it will turn out 'OK'. However, for still pix-
kodachrome, I am using my own, which is fresh, with an
expiration date of August 1948."
Winston
PERSONAL PART OF FOREGOING MESSAGE
"Actually, the days are too short, and I'm really
having a most interesting time. When we first started
on the trip, I had Mr. Ogino and Mr. Susuki in for a
conference. Trying out my Japanese on Mr. Susuki, I
asked, 'Ikaga desuka?' He replied seriously and
courteously, 'I no speak English.'
"Told Mr. Saito to inform the cook that I am very
fond of biscuits--to please make some. Nothing happened.
When I asked Saito why we didn't get biscuits, he re-
ported the cook was afraid the flour would not last,
that there was only a little more than 250 pounds left!
'Why,' I replied, greatly surprised, 'we have 300 pounds
and that's more than the usual allowance for the same
period.' 'Yes,' he agreed, quite puszled, 'but the cook
says it will take 30 pounds for the biscuits you wanted.
I was a trapped gourmand! What could be the reason for
such extraordinary production of biscuits? It developed
that the cook was referring to some quartermaster recipes
for l00 men for one (1) day!
"I haven't started counting days yet. If I get
tired I 'jest natcher'ly goes to sleep'
"I'll
SECRET

Enclesure to Tekyo's
No: 61, Janmary 27, 1948.
-6-
"I'll start signing off now and add more later
before this is mailed. Best wishes to the whole office.
Sincerely,
18/
win
19 Dec 47
"P.S. Actually, the Japanese are making my life
quite pleasant. All officials are cooperative, and I
hope for a successful season in spite of a couple of
short whales. Life in general is very interesting and
exciting, and I consider the instances cited as just
passing events. The situation is well in hand.
"Regards to Col. Schenck and the office."

Representative Tollefson,

Dear Sir,
I have operated this firm since 1911, that 1s 37 yeers
and I have fished a Master of my own vessels there for
nine years. During that time in 1937 I had the pleasure
to drive the Jap fishermen out single handed.
I want information as to the part of Bering Sea where
the Japs fished codfish in Bering Sea, and with what
success. One year along about 1935 or 1936 the Union Fish
Co of San Francisco, contracted with the Japs to catch
their fish for them and the Power schooner Golden State
was sent from San Francisco, and was loaded by the Japs.
This was contrary to U S law and the Govt seized the
Golden State when she got back, but there was much pull
and they got someone in Washington to take the rap and
say he had given permission.
I want to know where that fish was caught, this
information is available to the occupation forces in
Japan, and at least should be on file in Washington,
although I doubt if it is, Anyway I want that information,
and demand is hereby made for it.
Yours very truly,
/s/ J. E. Shields

1. At present NR has under study three proposals affecting
Japanese fishing operations which will be of interest to other
nations:
a. A proposal was made to axtend the authorised Jay
fishing area to the east and south. This proposal was sak
mitted to DS in check sheet, file NR 432(9 Jan 48)Fi, mabje
"Extension of Authorised Fishing Aree to the East and South
Pacific," dated 9 January 1948.
(1) Reports have been received from CINCPAC imdi-
cating opposition to extension which would include
waters in the Trust Territory of the Pacifie Islands.
However, CINCPAC would entertain no objection to exter
sion of the eastern boundary to the l80o meridian with
southern and northern boundaries at 240 and 450 north
latitude respectively. Action on this proposal now
awaits reply from Department of Army.
b. A proposal will be made extending the northern boy
ary of the authorized fishing area from 450 30' north latis
to approximately 450 45' north latitude and extending 
east as 1450 35' east longitude. Thence south fol)-wing a
mid-channel course between Hokkaido and the ialards wader
Russian occupation to a point midway between Nosappu Misaki
and Kaigara Jima at approximately 430 23' north latitude 
145° 51' east longitude.
c. A staff study is being prepared concerning a 1948.
1949 SCAP-authorized Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expeditiom.
The possibility of authorising three fleets is being studie
in order to maintain the Japanese share of production to th
obtained in the 1946-47 and 1947-48 seasons.
2. Proposals on these three projects will be submitted as
soon as adequate studies have been completed.

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.98)
SOPY
AUSTRALIAN
Washingten, D.G.
4 May, 1948.
The Australian Government is increasingly concerned at a number of resems
developments concerning the occupation and control of Japan which seem to suggest
that certain matters which clearly belong to the jurisdiction of the Far Eastern
Commission are being resolved unilaterally by the Occupation authorities.
Australia proposes to bring this tendency to the attention of the Far Eastern
Commission.
Japanese Whaling
The Supreme Commander's action in proposing another whaling expedition is in
itself a unilateral action and the Australian Government considers it a matter of
principle which should be decided in FEC.
Supreme Commander's Press Release.
It will be recalled that a statement was made recently by the Australian
Representative at the Far Eastern Commission on the subject of a prese release
issued by the Supreme Commander in Japan. The Australian Government interprets
this to mean that the Supreme Commander was advocating unilateral action on the
part of the United States Government.
Japanese Coastal Patrol.
This question was the subject of discussion at the last meeting of the Far
Eastern Commission. The manner in which the FEC was ignored was a direct
challenge to the authority of the Far Eastern Commission.

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.98)
C.Ω P I (Cont.)
2
Japenese_seing abroad.
The Australian Government is also most concerned at the tendemey tewards
unilateral action on the part of the Supreme Commander on the question of Japanes
going abroad.
There are three proposals before the Far Eastern Commission on the question of
Japanese going abroad. The first relates to Japanese going abroad for cultural
purposes, the second relates to Japanese business men going abroad and the third
to Japanese officials accompanying SCAP officials to international conferences as
technical advisers. In all the aforementioned categories Australia can only support
the last mentioned. The fact that the United States submitted three proposals to
the Far Eastern Commission for its approval indicates that in the view of the
United States Government these questions must be approved by the Far Eastern Dom-
mission before Japanese can be permitted to go abroad and enjoy the same privileges
as nationals of the Allied Governments. In spite of this the Far Eastern Commission
has been informed that pending a decision by the Far Eastern Commission the
Supreme Commander will go ahead and allow the Japanese to make visits abroad. The
Australian Government would be prepared to agree to visits abroad by Japanese,
subject to the concurrence not only of the country concerned but also of the Far
Eastern Commission. There are numerous reports in the press, confirmed by
informationirom the Australian Mission in Tokyo, which indicate that the Supreme
Commander is about to take unilateral action before a decision has been reached by
the Far Eastern Commission in these questions.
Fishing.
On the question of fishing the Australian Government would be prepared to
agree to Japanese fishing outside of Japanese waters only with the concurrence
of the country particularly concerned and of the Far Eastern Commission. While
there might be a possible case for Japanese fishing outside Japanese waters from

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.98)
C2I(Cont.)
-3
There are reports of Japanese expeditions being in proximity to the Amstrelimm
mandated territories. One Japanese fishing vessel has already been discevered
south of the area authorized by the Supreme Commander and within Australian terri-
torial waters. Australia is concerned also about reports that the Japanese may be
permitted to fishin the vicinity of the former Japanese mandates. These mandated
islands are being administered by the United States in trust for the security
of Australia and all other members of the United Nations. Australia does not
consider that the Japanese should have access to these areas.
Bauxite
Another example of unilateral action is the recent permission given by the
Supreme Commander for the Japanese to import 100,000 tons of bauxite. Under the
existing interim reparations removals programme there is a decision of the Far
Eastern Commission, the spirit of which is that the Japanese shall not be permitted
to have an aluminum industry. The importation of bauxite at the present time for
the purpose of reviving the Japanese aluminum industry is also contrary to the
spirit of the FEC decision on the reduction of Japan's industrial war potential.
It is true that a paper "Policy towards Japanese industry" which is at present
under discussion in the SteeringCommittee does contemplate a change in the axisting
policy decision but this is far from being decided and the Supreme Commander is not
justified in taking unilateral action which would in fact pre-judge a decision which
is still under consideration in the Commission.

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.96)
CQEI
AUSTRALIAN EMBASSY
Washington, D.c.
ATOE MEMOIRE
The Australian Government has given careful consideration to the State
Department's aide memoire of 13 April, 1948, concerning the proposed Japanese
Antarctic whaling expedition for the 1948/49 season and desires to remind the
M
United States Government that the attitude of the Australian Government towards
the resumption by Japan of Antarctic whaling so soon after the cessation of
hostilities has been made clearly known throughout the past two years. The
Australian Government sees no reason to modify its attitude and wishes to re-
state its contention that Japanese participation in Antarctic whaling is
properly a matter of principle which should be decided by the Far Eastern
Commbsion. The Australian Government is particularly opposed to the proposal
that a third factory ship be included in the projected 1948/49 expodition.
Employment of a third factory ship would not increase the total amount of whale
3
oil taken from Antarctic waters since the seasonal limit of 16,000 BWU as
UV
prescribed by the International Convention for the Regulation of Whaling was
reached during the 1947/48 season. It would however have the effect of reducing
the amount of whale oil, meat and other products taken by other Allied expeditions
whose countries are badly in need of fats, oils and meat. To allow Japan a third
factory ship would clearly amount to giving equal treatment to an enemy country to
the detriment of former allies.
The Australian Government notes that "Allied observers will be welcomed on
the expedition to observe compliance with the regulations." In this connection the
Australian Government desires to point out that in its view the treatment accorded
the Australian observer during the last expedition could not be regarded as satis-
factory. The Australian observer states that he was discouraged from seeking

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
technical information from Japanese members of the expedition and that is
general he was made to feel that his presence was not welcomed by the represemta-
tive of the Supreme Commander.

SUBJECT:
Area Authorized for Japanese Fishing and Whaling.
References:
(a) FLTLOSCAP Serial No. 80 of 27 Sept mber 1945.
(b) SCAJAP Serial No. 42 of 13 October 1945.
(c) SCAJAP Serial No. 587 of 3 Novber 1945.
1. The provisions of references (a) and (b), and paragraphs
1 and 3 of reference (c) in so far as they relate to authorisation
of Japanese fishing areas, are rescinded.
2. Effective this date and until further notice Japanese fish-
ing, whaling and similar operations are authorised within the area
bounded as follows: From a point midway between Nosappm Misaki and
Kaigara Jima at approximately 43023' North Latitude, 145051' East
Longitude; to 430 North Latitude, 146o30' East Longitude; thenee to
450 North Latitude, 1650 East Longitude; thence south along 165th
Meridian to 24o North Latitude; west along the 24th Parallol to 1230
East Longitude; thence north to 26o North Latitude, 123o East Longi-
tude; thence to 32030' North Latitude, 1250 East Longitude; thence
to 330 North Latitude, 127040' East Longitude; thence to 400 North
Latitude, 1350 East Longitude; to 45030' North Latitude, 1400 East
Longitude; thence east to 45030' North Latitude, 1450 East Longitude:
rounding Soya Misaki at a distance of three (3) miles from shore;
south along 145th Meridian to a point three (3) miles off the coast
of Hokkaido; thence along a line three (3) miles off the coast of
Hokkaido rounding Shiretoko Saki and passing through Nemuro Kaikyo
to the starting point midway between Nosappu Misaki and Kaigara Jima.
3. Authorization in paragraph 2 above is subject to the follow
ing provisions:
(a) Japanese vessels will not approach closer than twelve
(12) miles to any island wit hin the authorized area which lies south
of 3oo North Latitude with the exception of Sofu Gan. Personnel
from such vessels will not land on islands lying south of 30° North
Latitude, except Sofu Gan, nor have contact with any inhabitants
thereof.
(b) Japanese vessels or personnel thereof will not ap-
proach closer than twelve (12) miles to Takeshima (37015' North
Latitude, 131o53' East Longitude) nor have any contact with said
island.

The Chief,

TOKYO.
Infringement of Limits on Fishing Area at Present Authorised
to the Japanese.
With further reference to Mr. Shaw's letter of 10th December
I wish to inform you that the Department of the Navy has now given
us a fuller report concerning the vessel responsible for the in-
fringement. The vessel was named KOSEI MARU and registered in
Kiserazu. No SCAP markings or orders were carried. Displacement
of vessel was about 250 tons and was marked "fishing vessel." An
examination of charts carried on board indicated that vessel had
proceeded from Tokyo Bay area with no diversions en route. Two
W.T. receivers and two transmitters were carried, one of about 150
watts output and the other about l0. No international code books
could be seen or signal flags found.
Two similar vessels had been observed north of equator at 1920
on previous day, i.e. 2nd December. Approximate positions were 2
degrees 02 minutes north 141 degrees 20 minutes east and 1 degree 50
minutes north 14l degrees 16 minutes east.
I hope that this further information will be of some assistance
in identifying the Japanese responsible for this disregard of the
limits of the fishing area.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
Tokyo, February 4, 1948.
MEMORANDUM
Reference is made to the Australian Mission's memoranda of
December 10, 1947 and January 3, 1948, concerning the reported
infringement of fishing limits by a Japanese fishing vessel on
December 3, 1947.
There is now enclosed for the information of the Mission a
copy of a report prepared by the Natural Resources Section of this
Headquarters, which gives details of the investigation made and
the action taken to date in this matter. It will be noted from
the enclosed report that the offending .vessel has been identified
as the Kosei Maru No. 2, owned by the Kosei Suisan Company, home
port Kisaratsu (Kiserazu), Chiba Prefecture, and that the captain
of the vessel was one Kazo Matsushima. It will also be noted that
the case was expected to be brought to trial by the Japanese Govern-
ment, probably in the Chiba Prefectural Court, Chiba City. It will
further be noted that a representative of the Fisheries Division
of Natural Resources Section addressed a meeting of the members of
the Tuna and Bonito Fisheries Association, at which he stressed the
seriousness of the violation in que stion and instructed the associ-
ation to work out a plan for disciplining its members to prevent a
recurrence of such violations and to report the action taken.
When further information is received in the premises, includ-
ing the judgment of the Japanese Court, the Australian Mission will
be advised.
The Australian Mission's cooperation in reporting violations
by Japanese fishing vessels is appreciated, and the Mission is
assured that every step will be taken to prevent a recurrence of
such violations.
Enclosure:
Copy of Report.
To the
Australian Mission in Japan,
Tokyo.

MEMORANDUM
Reference is made to the Australian Mission's memoranda of
December 10, 1947 and January 3, 1948 and to this Section's memo-
randum of February 4, 1948, concerning the reported infringement
by a Japanese fishing vessel on December 3, 1947 of a Headquarters'
directive defining fishing areas.
The following information has been received concerning the
disposition of this case by the Japanese court:
1. The captain of the offending vessel, the Kosei Mar
has been sentenced to eight months' penal servitude and fined
¥20,000.
2. The tuna and bonito license for this vessel will be
revoked; the owners will thereby be required to dispose of
the vessel by sale. The vessel will be required to remain in
port until its ownership is changed.
3. The company owning the vessel was required to hold
.the vessel in port for the duration of the court action, and
it is estimat ed that a loss of over ¥2,400,000 was suffered.
The Australian Mission's cooperation in reporting violations
by Japanese fishing vessels and in supplying complete information
is appreciated.
To the
Australian Mission in Japan,

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
Office of the United States Political Adviser at Tokye
subject of: "Australian and Chinese Representatiens to
