Memorandun for Record, Prepared by
Natural Resources Section, GHQ, SCAP
January 12, 1948
1. In a memorandum dated 10 December 1947 fron the Australian
Mission to Diplomatic Section on subject topic, it was reported
that "on 3rd December H.M.A.S. Australia, whilst on passage from
Hongkong to Manus Island, intercept ed and boarded a Japanese fish-
ing vessel, Kojee Maru, at a position 00 degrees 25 minutes South,
145 degrees 10 minutes East (approximately 60 miles north of Hermit
Island). The vessel, which was flying the SCAJAP burgee, was en-
gaged in deep line fishing and had about 300 fish, tunny variety,
on board. Her crew number 30, none of whom could speak English."
2. Action to date on this case is as follows:
a. On 22 December 1947, NR instructed the Japanese Bureau.
of Fisheries to investigate immediat ely the reported violation and
to have the offending parties prosecuted and punished in accordance
with the seriousness of the violation.
b. On 29 December 1947, Mr. N. Sogawa, Chief of the Pelagic
Fisheries Section of the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries, reported to
NR/Fi the results of the investigation as of that date. The offend-
ing vessel had been identi fied as the Kosei Maru No. 2 owmed by the
Kosei Suisan Co. The vessel had sailed on 10 November 1947 from
Kisaratsu, Chiba, and on 12 November 1947 had sailed from Misaki,
Kamagawa Prefecture, from where she proceeded on a southerly course
crossing the southern boundary (24° Latitude) of the authorised area
on 16 November 1947 at O800. Tuna fishing operations were confined
principally to the area from about 2o north of the equator to 40
minutes south of the equator and at or near 1450 east longitude. On
3 December 1947, the Japanese vessel sighted "a foreign cruiser (pre-
sumably the H.M.A.S. Australia) at a position O degrees 25 minutes
south 1450, 10 minutes east." The Japanese vessel was boarded by a
detachment from the Australian vessel. The boarding party obtained
some information from the captain, but this was very limited because
the boarding party did not have an interpreter and the Japanese
captain and members of his crew could not speak English. The board-
ing party obtained evidence of fish aboard the Koai Mari, and iden-
tified the vessel through the name of a life preserver. Upon re-
turn of the boarding party to the Australian war vessel, the latter
proceeded on an easterly course. The Kosei Maru continued fishing
in the general vicinity until 14 December, at which time she de-
parted for a Japanese port of landing, crossing the boundary of the
authorized area on 23 December 1947 and arriving at the home port
of Kisaratsu, Chiba, on 27 December where she unloaded a catch con-
sisting of 1,188 tuna, and 353 shark, or a total catch, by weight,
of approximately 12,000 kan.
c. On 6 January 1948, in accordance with instructions
from Chief of the Fisheries Divi sion, NRS, the following persons
called at the Fisheries Division office to report further on the
incident: Messrs. M. Sogawa md Kato, of the Japanese Bureau of
Fisheries, Captain Kaso Matsushima of the Kosei Maru No. 2, Mr.
Kuniichi Kubo, representing the Kosei Suisan Co., owmer of the of-
fending vessel. By int eview with the captain, the above information

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclomure No. 5 to Tokyo's
No. 234, April 19, 1948.
- 2 -
on details of the trip was confirmed. The captain also added the
information that the principal reasons for his fishing outside of
the authorized area were:
(1) This was his first assignment as captain, and the
first trip of the Kosei Maru, and he was anxious to make a good
showing.
(2) On the basis of past experience in the tuna fisheries
he was aware that the region near the equator had produced good
catches prior to World War II.
(3) Within the authorized area, catches of tuna are rela-
tively poor at this time of year, and he had not found temperatures
suitable for tuna until he reached the area fished. Records of
water temperatures had been taken every four hours, but no attempts
to catch fish had been made within the authorized area on the trip
in question. In other words, after leaving his port of departure
in Japan, he had proceeded directly south to the preferred tuna
fishing area in the region of the equator.
d. Captain Matsushima admitted that he was aware of the order
issued by General MacArthur setting up authorized fishing areas. Mr.
Sogawa, of the Bureau of Fisheries, stated that information on the
authorized area had been issued to the prefectural governments and
to the bonita and tuna Fishermen's Association at the time licenses
were issued for boats to engage in the tuna fisheries, and by a
direct communication to the boat owner. Captain Matsushima stated
that he had not seen other Japanese fishing vessels operating out-
side of the area during the trip in which his violation occurred.
e. Japanese Bureau of Fisheries has reported the case to the
judicial section of the Japanese Government and Mr. Taketada
Matsuzugumi has been assigned as a government prosecutor. The case
is expected to go to court about the middle of January and will
probably be tried at the Chiba Prefectural Court, Chiba City. The
presiding judge has not yet been named.
f. On 9 January 1948, Mr. Herrington, Chief of NR Fisherios
Division, addressed a meeting of the members of the Tuna and Bonito
Fishermen's Association. He stressed the seriousness of the sub-
ject violation, and the effect that it may have upon the decisions
of Allied Nations regarding exten sion of area, and the conditions
respecting Japanese fi sheries to be included in the forthcoming
Peace Treaty conferences. In addition, he instructed the Associa-
tion to work out some plan for self-disciplining its members to
prevent recurrence of violations and to report to him on the action
taken. Such action appears as the most practical solution because
of the wide extent of the authorized area and the lack of surveil-
lance by patrol vessels.
3. Future action on the case, including the penalty asigned by
the Japanese court, will be reported in the form of a supplement to
this memorandum for record as soon as such information is available.

CHINESE MISSION IN JAPAN
MEMORANDUM
Reference is made to the memoranda of the Diplomatic Section,
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
dated August 6, October 2 and 22, 1947, on the subject of fishing
by Japanese in unauthorized waters off the coast of China.
The Chinese Mission appreciates GHQ's action in having in-
structed the Japanese Government to investigate into the reported
cases of Japanese fishing boats operating outside authorised areas.
It is requested that the Mission be kept informed of the results
of such investigations, particularly the prosecution of the owmers
of the Japanese vessel AKASHI MARU by the Japanese court. The Mi
sion is also gratified to note the plans in preparation to patrol
the area in the East China Sea in an effort to reduce violations
and to apprehend violators as mentioned in the Diplomatic Section's
memorandum of October 22.
Recently the Mission has again received instructions from its
home government regarding further cases of violations by Japanese
fishermen out side authorized areas. The following two cases of
violations have been reported by the Fishery Rehabilitation
Administration:
"Two Japanese fishing steamers, No. 31 OTAKI MARU and No. 32
OTAKI MARU, were found by S.S. 'E.E.Johnson' of this Administration
to be operating within the Chinese territorial waters at Longitude
120049'E and Latitude 26044'N on May 21, 1947 at 07:50 A.M. Both
of these steamers were paint ed green, iron-clad and equipped with
hand-operated fishing nets.
"The fishing steamer 'Fride of America' belonging to this Ad-
ministration, while operating in the sea near Taiwan at Longitude
121055iE and Latitude 26044'N on July 20, 1947 at 09:40 A.M.,
sighted a Japanese fishing boat No. 8 KAYAKI MARU, equipped with
pulling fishing net, which is also operating within the same area."
The GHQ's attention is further invited to the above cases of
violations. Prompt action and early reply will be highly appreciated.
Tokyo, November 27, 1947.

Enclosure No. 7 to Despatch No. 234 dated April 191

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
Tokyo, December 18, 1947.
MEMORANDUM
Reference is made to the Chinese Mission's memorandum dated
November 27, 1947 requesting that the Mission be informed of the
results of investigation by the Japanese Government of reports of
fishing by Japanese vessels in unauthorized areas.
The Japanese Government was duly directed to investigate the
alleged violations by the OTAKI MARU No. 31 and the OTAKI MARU No.
32 on May 21, 1947, and by the KAYAKI MARU No. 8 on July 20, 1947.
The Mission will be informed of the results of this investigation
as soon as possible.
With reference to the Mission's request for a report of the
prosecution of those responsible for operations of the AKASHI MARU
in unauthorized waters, the cases involving the AKASHI MARU No.58
and the TAIYO MARU No. 15 were brought before the Shimonoseki Local
Court on August 4, 1947. The court reached a verdict of "not
guilty" in all cases on the ground that insufficient evidence had
been presented, but the Japanese Government prosecutor is now on-
deavoring to appeal the cases to the District Court. In order to
do so, however, it is required that the name of the person report-
ing the violation, such as the captain of the reporting Chinese
vessel, be submitted in evidence. This requirement of Japanese
law applies also to the cases of the TOKYO MARU, KUJIRA MARU, and
NOYANAGI MARU, which were brought before the Japanese Local Court
in Fukuoka on December 1, 1947.
In order that the legal proceedings against these vessels and
crews may not be unduly delayed, and in order to assist the prose-
cutor in his appeal, it is requested that the Chinese Mission pro-
vide this Headquarters with the names of the individuals who in
each case observed and reported the above violations.
To the
Chinese Mission in Japan,
Tokyo.

Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 234 dated Apoil

subject of: "Australian and Chinese Representatioms to

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
Tokyo, January 6, 1948.
MEMORANDUM
Reference is made to the Chinese Mission's memorandum dated
November 27, 1947 and to earlier correspondence with regard to
establishment of a patrol in the East China Sea in order to main-
tain proper surveillance of the authorized fishing area and to
int ercept Japanese violators.
The Japanese Government has submitted for the approval of this
Headquarters the following plan for operation of a patrol vessel.
The patrol boat, a three-hundred and fifty-ton unarmed vessel named
the Hatsutaka Maru, would operate within the sea area bounded as
follows:
From a crosspoint of 34 degrees north latitude and the
present line, to 34 degrees north latitude and 122 degrees 30
minutes east longitude, to 28 degrees 30 minutes nosth lati-
tude and 122 degrees 30 minutes east longitude, to 26 degrees
north latitude and 120 degrees 30 minutes east longitude, to
the crosspoint of 26 degrees north latitude and the present
line.
The patrol boat would refrain from calling at foreign ports and
from contacting any foreign vessels at sea. It would operate about
three weeks a month, and during the remaining week it would return
to port to take on fuel and provisions. The home port of the
patrol vessel, in order to avoid publicity as to its operations,
would not be located at any of the important fishing ports.
To facilitate detection of violators, the Japanese Government
has also requested permission for the patrol boat to do a mall
amount of fishing at occasional periods. Since the kinds of fish
differ in various parts of the East China Sea, it is possible to
determine on the basis of the catch where a vessel was fishing.
Such information has been used in the past on several occasions to
help fix the position of Japanese fishing vessels which have been
reported outside the authorised area. It is estimated that the
patrol vessel would catch approximately six and one-half tons of
fish by such int ermittent fishing during each month of its operation.
The Diplomatic Section requests that the Chinese Government
be informed of the above plan and inquires whether the Chinese
Government would object to the establishment of a patrol in the
area as outl ined and intermittent fishing by the Japanese patrol
boat. An early reply would be appreciated.
To the
Chinese Mission in Japan,
Tokyo.
DS/WJS/RBFinn:cgf

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Depatch No. 238 dated April 21, 1948 from
Office of the United States Political Adviser at Tokye on the
ject of: "teturn of Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expedition."
PIO - GHQ - SCAP, April 7, 1948
ALLIED OBSERVFRS WITH WHALING EXPEDITION RETURN TO JAPAN
Three Allied observers who have been with the 1947-48
Japanese Antarctic Whaling E"pedition for five months returned
to Japan aboard the 10,O00-ton Hashidate Maru, factory ship
belonging to the Nipron Suisan Comrany, which docked at Osaka to-
day. The SCAP representative is Lt. Col. Valdon C. Winston,
Columbia, io., who is assigned to the Fisheries Division of
Natural Fesources Section. Observers for the gcvernments of
Australia and Norway are Kenneth Coonan, who was also an observer
on the Japanese expedition lest year, and Comdr. Herman K. Sundt,
veteran Norwegian whaling inspector.
Claude M. Adams, NRS Fisheries Division official, welcored
the Allied observers and Capt. T. Hiyeta, 45-year-old, English-
speaking skiprer of the Hashidate Maru, and his 3oo Jaranese crew
members. "As the representative of the Suprere Comrancer for the
Allied Powers on this occasion, it gives me great pleasure to
welcome hrme the officers and crew of the Hashidate Maru.
"The Nippon Suisan Company's fleet caught and processed 488
baleen whales during the rigorous four-ronth whale hunting season.
From these whales, 6,765 retric tons of whale oil and 10,191
metric tons of reat, blubber, and other whale products were
obtained. The vhale o1l produced represents 105 barrels per blue
whale unit. By adjusting this figure to include the whale oil
that might have been prcduced if the meat and blubber had been
processed for sil instead of returned to the homeland for food,
whale o1l production would have.been 123.3 barrels per blue whele
unit. This is a greater yield than that ever before obtained by
any Japanese whaling fleet. The production of all whele products
amounted to 44.6 metric tons rer blue whale unit. These figures
are standing records of the orerational efficiency of the
Nippon Suisan whaling fleet.
"This fine record was not acoorplished by any one individual
or special group of persons, but vas rade possible by the hard
work and cooperation of each rember of the expedition. The
officials of the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries, Ministry of Agri-
culture and Forestry, and the Nippon Suisan Company who planned
and organized the expedition are also to be congratulated upon the
thonoughness and dispatch with which the job was accomplished.
You are all justified in feeling proud of the fine record your
combined efforts have produced."
Japanese officials who were also at the Osaka vharf to welcome
the whalers included N. Sogawa and Hi Onura, representatives of
the Bureau of Fisheries; S. Masui, president of Nippon Suisan
Company; and S. Kuboi, managing director of Nippon Suisan.
A whaling fleet belonging to the Taiyo Gyogoyo Company also
participated in this year's Antarctic expedition. Approximately
27 ships, which included a factory ship from each corpany and
numerous killer or catcher boats, transports, and tankers, were
involved.

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
Whaling operations carried on in the southern waters by the
two Jaranese fleets were completed March 10 with a total catch
of 1,321 whales. Although detailed production figures are not yet
available, it is estirated thet this season's catch will yield
17,829 metric tons of whale o1l and about 35;000 metric tons of
meat and other whale products. According to Mr. Adans, afl food
products from the expedition will be distributed in Japan through
legal ration channels. The whale oil which was offered for
allocation to oil deficient countries by the International Emergen-
cy Food Council, has been designated for Jananese consumption by the
UN Food and Ag.'Organization.
In addition to the Allied and Japanese rersonnel aboard, the
Hashidate Maru carried a cargo of 1,923 metric tons of whale o1l
and 957 metric tons of frozen vhale reat and blubber.
Three rore Allied observers are expected to return to Japan
fror the Antarctic Sunday when the Nisshin Maru, factory ship
belonging to the Taiyo Gyogoyo Company, docks at Tokyo. The SCAP
representative aboard the Nisshin Maru is william M. Terry, NRS
Fisheries Division, who also shipred tith the Japanese Antarctic
expedition last year. Lt. (SG) Francois Bourgois, French Navy,
and Capt. A.V. Herming (Royal Navy, retired) are the observers
aboard for the French government anc the United Kingdom,
respectively.

12 April 1948
"BELL OF PEACE" TO FEPLACE BFLL IELTED FOR WAR
A bell for Kizaki-cho, a small village about 3oo riles west
of Tokyo, will soon replace the old bell, melted dorn during the
war, according to a recent letter sent to General Douglas MacArthu
from Dr. Masanori Masuda, a native of Kizaki-cho.
Before the bell was romoved by government order for war
purposes, it hung in the village's Shingon Buddhist térrle. Now
Îr. Masuda, a medical instructor at the Kyoto Prefectural Medical
College, has ordered a four-foot bell to be presented to the
villagers.
In his letter to the General, Dr. asuda wrote, "I want this
bell to be a symbol of peace and to exert an influence over the
villagers so that when they hear it, they will be reminded of the
folly of war, and so that henceforth in all their relations,
neighborhood and familial, they will be guided by peaceful motives
Dr. lfasuda calls his gift. "the Bell of Peace" and has re-
quested the Supreme Comrancer to give him a "suitable sentiment"
to be inscribed on the bell.

WHALING EXPEDITION FACTORY SHIP DOCKS AT TOKYO
Five months of duty in the Antarctic whaling grounds ended
today for three Allied observers who were aboard the Nissin
Maru, 10,Ooo-ton factory ship belonging to the Taiyo Gyogoyo
Corpany, which tied up at Shibaura Dock, Tokyo. William M.
Terry, Wilmington, Del.,: aCAP repre sentative, also accompanied
last year's Japanese Antarctic whaling expedition as SCAP
observer. He is assigned to the Fisheries -Division of SCAP's
Natural Resources Section. Observers for the French government
end the United Kingdom who were aboard the Nissin Maru were Lt.
(s.g.) Francois Bourgois, French Navy, and Capt. A.V. Hemming,
Foyal Navy, retired.
With the arrival of the Nissin Maru, all ships corposing the
two Jerenese fleets of this year's Antarctic vhaling expedition
have returned to the homeland.
Brig. Gen. R.E. Starr, Commanding General, Headquarters and
Service Group, Lt. Col. Hubert G. Schenck, Chief of SCAP's
Natural Fesources Section, and Claude M. Acams, Fisheries
Division official of NIS, were on hand to welcome the Allied
observers, Capt. S. Yokogi, skipper of the Nissin Maru, and his
300 crew members.
"As the representetive of the Supreme Comrander for the
Allied Powers on this occasion, it gives. me great pleasure to
welcone home the officers and crew of the Nissin Maru," Col.
Schenck stated. "Both SCAP. and the people of Japan are proud of

ANTARTIC EXPEDITION WEIGHS 46 WHALES
Forerost among the oceanographic and scientifio
were conducted under the supervision or Lt. Col. Waldon O
Columbia, Mo., and William M. Terry, Wilmington, Del., SCA
sentatives accompanying the 1947-48 Japanese Antarctio 
dition, was the the actual weighing, part by part, of 46
blue and fin species. Both, men are assigned to SCap's Natur
Resources Section.
According to the best available informetion, orly thre
in the world hed ever been weighed, part by part, and the cata
recorced prier to the work cone by Col. "inston and ir. Terry
Weighing of the first whale is reported to have been accorplish
in 1903 somewhere in Newfoun land by Dr. F. A. Lucas, Direotor of
the Arerican National Museum in New York City. This blue whal
77 feet long and was weighed in at 63 metric tons. The. second
weighed whale was also e blue, was 66 feet long, and weighed 49
metric tons. It was recorced as official in 1924 in an edition
Norsk Hvalfangst-Tidende, a Norwegian whaling magazine. The thi
blue whale was weighed in 1926 vith the inforration being furmi
the riscovery Corrittee (British Colonial Office, London) by Cap
T. Sorille, Stronness, South Georgie. It wes 89 feet long, weis
114 metric tons, and had an estirated total weight of 122 retrio t
Japanese government insnectors, biologists, and ship personn
aboarc the Hashidate l aru, 10,O00-ton factory ship belonging to th
Nipron Suisan Comnany, weighed and made detailed records of 16 blu
whales and 11 fin whales during the three ronths that fleet was in
operation in the Antarctic. This work in no way delayed production
operations on the 490. whale catch since it was accomplished during
slack pericds when the flensing deck was completely clear of all
other vhale carceses.
It is believed that the world's record for the largest blue
whale ever to be weighed, part by part, can be credited to the.
Nipron Suisan Corpany fleet. It was 89 feet long and hea a measured
circumference of 43t feet. Tre actual weight by parts (which in-
cluded the tongue, bones, blubber, ventral grooves, meet, internal
organs,. and baleen) was 127.5 metric tons. The total estimated
weight, including blood and stomach contents, was set at 136.4. This
estimate was based on the methods internationally erployed by the
Discovery Comrittee. Heretofore, the largest weighed blue whale was
the one from South Georgia which was. processed in 1926.
Meat products from this giant whale totaled. 40.5 metric tons
and the total whale oil production reached 133 barrels. The liver
which weighed one metric ton, yielded 37 kilograms of liver oil
the vitamin A content of which had an IU (international 'unit) count
of 110,000. The skull of this whalc reasured 23 feet long and was
10 feet wide. The length of the lower jaw bone was 22 feet 10
inches and the fluke (tail) meesurerent from tip to tip was 20
The heart alone weighed 900 poinds. Fighty men worked nearly
hours in flensing (strirping off the blubber), cismembering,
weighing this animal. Longer end heavier whales, both.blue
were caught by expedition killer boats this season, but they
officialy weighed and recorded.

DATEMay 4, 1948
Australian Reaction to Matters within the Far Eastern Cemmissien

1-1408
The Australian Ambassador called at his request and was received by
.Mr. Saltsman 4:45 p.m., Wednesday, May 4.
Mr. Makin read the attached Aide Memoire regarding Antarctic whaling.
He departed from the Aide Memoire to emphasise: (1) that Australia regards
Antarctic whaling by the Japanese as a matter of principle upon which the
FEC should adopt a poliey; (2) that the addition of a third factory ship would
enable the Japanese to handle more whales and thus reduce the mumber available
to Allied countries; and (3) that Australia felt the treatment acoorded its
observer on the last expedition was not satisfactory.
Mr. Saltsman pointed out the need of the Japanese for food and the great
value of whaling in supplying this need. Mr. Makin said Australia felt this
need should not be supplied at the expense of the Allies who needed the same
food. Mr. Saltmman said the U. S. appreciated this view but also felt that
Japan should not be deprived of the benefits of whaling in a way to eause
additional expense to one of the Allies - the United States. Mr. Saltmman
said the U. S. had not been aware of Australia's feeling that the treatmmt
of its observer was not satisfactory.
Mr. Makin then expressed concern at what appeared to be the developing
attitude by SCAP to bypass the Far Eastern Commission. He made the follewing
declarations in addition to the points made in the attached statement frem whish
he spoke.
CONFIDENTIAL

AUSTRALIAN EMBASSY

4 May. 1948.
The Austreli Gover.ament is increesingly concerned at a number
of recent develcpments concerain the occupation and control of Jepan which
seen to suost thut certain metters which clearly belong to the jurisdiction
of the Fer Eastern Co. mission ere beins resolved unilaterally by the Occupation
authorities. Aus ralia proposes to brin. this tendency to the attention of
the Far Eastern Co:mission.
Jepene e haling
The Supreme Co mander's action in proposing another whaling expedition
F.W.894.62
is in itself & unilateral action and the Australian Government considers
it a matter of rinciple which should be decided in FEC.
Supreme Comnander's Press Release.
It vill be recalled thet a atatement was in de recently by the
Austrelian Representative at the Fer Eastern Conmission on the subject of a
press release issued b: ti.e Su reme Cosnander in Jepen. The mustrelian
Government interprets this to meen thet the Supreme Cormander was advocating

Japanese Coastal Patrol
This question was the subject of discussion at the last meeting of

2

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
Japanese going abroad
The Australian Government is also most concerned at the
tendency towards unilateral action on the pert of the Supreme Commander
on the question of Japanese going abroad.
There are three proposals before the Far Eastern Commission
on the question of Japenese going abroed. The first relates to Japanese
going abroad for cultural purposes, the second relates to Japanese
business men going abroed end the third to Japanese officials accompanying
SCAP officials to international conferences as technical advisers. In all
the aforementioned categories Australia can only support the last mentioned.
the fact that the United States submitted three proposals to the Far
Eastern Commission for its approval indicates that in the view of the
United States Government these questions must be approved by the Far
Eastern Commission before Japanese can be permitted to go abroad and
enjoy the same privileges as nationels of the Allied Governments. In
spite of this the Far Eastern Commission hes been informed that pending
a decision by the Far Eastern Commission the Supreme Commender will go
ahead and allow the Japanese to make visits abroad. The Australian
Government would be prepared to agree to visits abroad by Japanese,
subject to the concurrence not only of the country concerned but also
of the Far Eastern Commission. There ere numerous reports in the press,
confirmed by informetion from the Australien Mission in Tokyo, which
indicate that the Supreme Commander is about to take unilateral action
before a decision has been reached by the Far Eastern Commission on
these questions.
Fishing
On the question of fishing the Australian Government would be
prepared to agree to Japanese fishing outside of Japanese waters only
with the concurrence of the country particularly concerned and of the
Far Eastern Commission. While there might be a possible case for

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
case for/
Japanese fishing outside Japanese waters from the point of view of
supplying food and oil there is no case for pearl fishing. There are
reports of Japanese expeditions being in proximity to the Australian
mandated territories. One Japanese fishing vessel has already been
discovered south of the area authorised by the Supreme Commander and
within Australian territorial waters. Australiais concerned also
about reports that the Japanese may be permitted to fish in the
vicinity of the former Japanese mandates. These mandated islands
are being administered by the United States in trust for the security
of Australia and all other members of the United Nations.
Australia does not consider that the Japanese should have access to
these areas.
Bauxite
Another example of unilateral action is the recent permission
given by the Supreme Commander for the Japanese to import 100,000
tons of bauxite. Under the existing interim reparations removals
programme there is a decision of the Far Eastern Commission, the spirit
of which is that the Japanese shall not be permitted to have an aluminum
indsutry. The importation of bauxite at the present time for the
purpose of reviving the Japanese aluminum industry is also contrary
to the spirit of the FEC decision on the reduction of Japan's industrial
war potential. It is true that a paper "Policy towards Japanese industry"
which is at present under discussion in the Steering Committee does
contemplate a change in the existing policy decision but this is far
from being decided and the Supreme Commander is not justified in tak-
ing unilateral action which would in fact pre-judge a decision which
is still under consideration in the Commission.

AUSTRALIANEMBA
WASHINOTO
4 May, 1948.
AIDEMEMOIRE
The Australian Government has given careful consideration to the State
Depertment's aide memoire of 13 April, 1948, concerning the proposed
Japanese Antarctic whaling expedition for the 1948/49 season and desires
to remind the Uniteu States Government that the atti tude of the Aust ralian
Government towards the resumption by Japan or Antarctic whaling so soon after
the cessation of hostilities has been made clearly known throughout the past
ti.o years. The Australian Government sees no reason to modify its attitude
and wishes to re-state its contention that Japanese participation in Antarotic
whaling is properly a matterof principle vhich should be decided by the
Far Eastern Commission. The Austrelian Government is particularly opposed
894.628/5-448
to the proposal that a third factory ship be included in the projected
1948/49 expedition. Employment of a third factory ship would not incroase
the total amount of  hale oil taken from Antarctic waters since the seasonal
limit of 16,000 BWU as prescribed by the Internetional Convention for the
Regulation of Whaling was reached during the 1947/48 season. It would
however have the effect of reducing the amount of whale oil, meat and other
products take: by other Allied expeditions whose countries are badly in need
of fats, oils and meat. To ailow Japan a third factory ship would clearly
amount to giving equel treatment to an enemy country to the detriment of
fcrmer allies.
The Australian Government notes thet "Allied observers will be
S
welcomed on the expedition to observe compliance with the regulations.
A1
In this connection the Australian Government desires to point out that in
its view the treetment accorded the Australien observer during the last
expedition could not be regarded as satisfactory. The Australian observer
states that he was di scouraged fro.. seeking technical information from
Japenese members of the expedition end that in general he was made to feel
that his presence was not welconed by the representative of the Sapreme
at
FILED
Commander.

Following the Steering Committee Meeting at the FEC this
morning, Mr. Bullock stated that the Australian Ambassador had
been instructed to take up various questions with the Department
this afternoon. These would include (1) a third Antarctic
whaling expedition; (2) a query concerning SCAP's purchase of
bauxite and its effect on the reactivation of the aluminum
94.63
industry; (3) question of exchange of persons from Japan and;
(4) Japanese fishing areas in the Pacific. I understand he is
raising these questions with Mr. Saltzman.
ESA
1n

informally of the advisability of raising such questions in the
Pw
Department and keeping them out of the FEC but that the Ambassador
had received instructions from Dr. Evatt to raise them not only
with the Department but in the FEC. Mr. Bullock concluded by
saying that he thought his superiors in Canberra would insist

A
hope that we would make more use of our Embassy in Canberra as
994.628

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
CONFIDENTIAL
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: May 4, 1948
Australian Reaction to Matters within the Far Eastern Commission
SUBJECT:
PARTICIPANTS:
The Australian Ambassador, Mr. Makin
O - Mr. Saltman
The Australian Embassy, r. Bullock
0 - Mr. Claxton
The Australian Embassy, Mr. Harry
NA - Mr. Allison
COPIES TO:
O - Mr. Saltzman
OE - Mr. Whitman
FE- Mr. Butterworth
BC - Mr. Richards
NA -Mr. Allison
1-1408
The Australian Ambassador called at his request and was received by
Mr. Saltzman 4:45 p.m., Wednesday, May 4.
Mr. Makin read the attached Aide Memoire regarding Antarctic whaling.
He departed from the Aide Memoire to emphasize: (1) that Australia regards
Antarctic whaling by the Japanese as a matter of principle upon which the
FEC should adopt a policy; (2) that the addition of a third factory ship would
enable the Japanese to handle more whales and thus reduce the number ayailable
to Allied countries; and (3) that Australia felt the treatment accorded its
observer on the last expedition was not satisfactory.
Mr. Saltzman pointed out the need of the Japanese for food and the great
15
value of whaling in supplying this need. r. Makin said Australia felt this
need should not be supplied at the expense of the Allies who needed the same
food. r. Saltzman said the U. S. appreciated this view but also felt that
Japan should not be deprived of the benefits of whaling in a way to cause
additional expense to one of the Allies - the United States. Mr. Saltzman
said the U. S. had not been aware of Australia's feeling that the treatment of
its observer was not satisfactory.
8
Mr. Makin then expressed concern at what appeared to be the developing
attitude by SCAP to bypass the Far Eastern Commission. He made the following
declarations in addition to the points made in the attached statement from which
he spoke.
40
CS/H
CONFIDENTIAL
911

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2
SCAP's Press Release
He felt it was appropriate for SCAP to send this letter to the Department
of the Army but not to release it to the press.
Japanese Coastal Patrol
He expressed Australia's surprise that action should be taken on the coastal
patrol of Japanese waters without policy action by FEC. Australia feels the
Japanese Diet should not be regarded as having the right to make a determination
in this situation without realizing the right of the Far Eastern Commission to
make a policy determination - and thus to bypass the FEC. He emphasized that this
problem relates to the external relations of Japan. A Japanese patrol, he said,
could intercept and search Allied vessels. The Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers is for all the Allies. FEC should be allowed to express a policy in order
that its member nations may be sure that a coastal patrol would not grow to be a
serious security threat twenty-five years from now. FEC should be able to pass on
the size and speed of the vessels and the size of the crew. Despite the fact that
FEC had a policy paper under consideration, even delay pending consideration of
this paper was vetoed by the U.S. FEC was not given a chance to consider the final
bill itself. The fact that seven members supported, two abstained and two
opposed the proposal for delay, shows the concern of other members over the uni-
lateral character of the action. In the course of r. Makin's remarks,
Mr. Saltzman pointed out that SCAi's action was properly within his authority and
not contrary to any FEC policy decision.
Japanese Going Abroad
Australia does not deny that SCAP has the power to allow Japanese to go
abroad but questions whether SCAP should exert these powers when the FEC has
the matter under consideration. The U.S. itself has three papers on this
subject in the FEC, thus recognizing the right of FEC to take action. "Where
do we come in", ir. Makin asked. Lir. Saltzman assured Lir. Lakin that the U.S.
has no intention to bypass FEC on matters coming under its terms of reference.
Mr. Makin expressed appreciation of this assurance and repeated that Australia
does not say that such action is beyond SCAP's powers - it feels that FEC should
be consulted while the question on the agenda. Australia would not object to
visits abroad by Japanese upon the concurrence of the country to be visited
and upon the approval of the FEC. He felt there should be no difficulty in
referring the matter to the FEC for concurrence. He emphasized that if the
Japanese were allowed to circulate too freely, the Allies would find themselves
unable to impose on Japan in the peace treaty the terms which should be imposed.
Australia does not want the Japanese to be able to build up the idea that they
are a highly desirable people, to be readily accepted. Australia does not
want to let them escape their just punishment. At this point Mr. Saltzman
stated that the U. S. does not want any more than Australia or the other Allies
for the Japanese to be able to revive their aggressive abilities in any field,
military or otherwise. On the other hand, the U. S. is oonsidering the entire
situation carefully in order to be sure that we are taking the proper steps to
build up a new and peaceful Japan with which we can all live and which will not
be subject to the blandishments of other ideologies.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
CONFIDENTIAL
Bauxite
Mr. Makin stated that Australia questions SCAP's importation of bauite
at a time when the interim decision of the FEC on availabilities from the
aluminum industry provides for its entire removal. This bauxite has been
brought in without consultation with the FEC. He said that if Scap had come
to the FEC and stated its needs for specific purposes, FEC would have agreed
on the modified use of facilities. Mr. Makin said that no one appreciates
SCAP more than Australia but because he is the Supreme Commander for all the
Allies, Australia feels it should be brought in to such questions through the
FEC.
Fishing
Mr. Makin said nothing beyond the attached written statement.
Mr. Makin concluded his statement by saying that he may raise some of
these matters with the FEC on Thursday. He wished to advise the U. S. in
advance.
because
Mr. Harry said that Australia's concern had not been/ of individual
instances but with a series of actions which were felt to indicate a trend.
He therefore regarded as very interesting Mr. Saltzman's statement that the
U. S. was giving general consideration to the problem of establishing & peace-
ful Japan which could resist the blandishments of other ideologies. He
hoped that the U. S. would be able to discuss its further views at a later
time. Mr. Saltzman stated that the U. S. was fully appreciative of the very
real interest of Australia and the other Allied countries in this problem and
that it was our intention to have further discussions with Australia and other
interested countries later on.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE LEGAL ADVISER
May 24, 1948
TO:
L - Mr. Gross
FROM:
L/P - Conrad E. Snow
SUBJECT: Aide-Memoire to Australian Ambassador
1. Attached is draft of an Aide-Memoire to the Australian
Ambassador in reply to an Australian Aide-Memoird of May 4, 1948.
For political reasons it has been determined in NA not to take
full advantage of the plenary authority believed by L/P to reside
in SCAP, and the paper is therefore referred to the Legal Adviser
for clearance purposes.
2. It is believed by L/P that in the absence of a relevant
policy decision by the FEC, SCAP has full power to take any action
which he considers desirable, even though such action necessitates
that SCAP himself must make a prior policy decision which concerns
the fulfillment by Japan of its obligations under the Terms of
Surrender, and which is therefore within the jurisdiction of the
FEC. It is believed that this power to take action is expressly
referred to in the Moscow Agreement, par. A-II A-2, which states
that it is a function of the FEC to review "any action taken by
the Supreme Commander involving policy decisions within the juris-
diction of the Commission". It is believed that any other interpre-
tation of the Moscow Agreement, with reference to SCAp's powers
thereunder, would have left SCAP helpless, immediately following the
making of the Agreement and prior to relevant action by the FEC, to issue
all orders for the "implementation of the Terms of Surrender", or
for the "occupation and control of Japan" (Moscow Agreement, par. B-5),
Since suc! an order can hardly be conceived which is not postulated
on some prior policy decision. If the necessary policy decisions are
not provided by the FEC, or by the U.S. by way of interim directive, they
must be made by SCAP, or he will be deprived of the power to act.
3. The proposed aide-memoire states that "it is the view of the
U.S. Government that the Supreme Commander has the authority 'as the
sole executive authority for the llied Powers in Japan' to take any
action which he considers necessary where adequate policy basis, in
previous FEC or U.S. directives, exists for his taking the action, or
where administrative necessities of the occupation require, pending
a policy decision of the FEC, and where existing FEC or U.S. directives
do not preclude his taking the action". It will be noted that in the
absence of a policy decision of the FEC, this position statement bases
the power of SCAP to act on the "administrative necessities of the
in SCAP to
occupation", and that this falls somewhat short of
take action "involving policy decisions within the jurisdiction of
the Commission".

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.98)
2
4. The chief difficulty with the proposed position is that in
the absence of a policy decision by the FEC or of an interim directive
by the United States, SCAp's freedom of action is confined to
"administrative necessities of the occupation". The burden of proof
seems to be thrown on him to show that his action is an "administrative
necessity". The proposed position also seems to conflict with the
recent determination as to U.S. policy in Japan to take full advantage
of all of SCAP's powers.
L/P:CESnow:1m?

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
May 27, 1948
L - Mr. Gross
Subject: SCAP's Authority to Act in the Absence
of Policy Basis.
A narrow but important question has arisen as to SCAP's
exact powers. It is generally agreed that SCAP may act where
894.
there is a "policy basis" for his action, which I understand may
be derived either from some specific or general policy statement
from the FEC or from a U. S. directive to General MacArthur
issued prior to the establishment of the FEC. It is also agreed
that SCAP may not act contrary to a FEC policy decision. There
is uncertainty, however, as to whether SCAP may act where there
is no "policy basis" for such action and no policy which would
preclude such action.
This question is being raised specifically in connection
with the statement made at the bottom of page 3 and the top of
page 4 in the proposed U. S. reply to an aide memoire given the
Department by the Australian Minister, which is attached. I
believe this U. S. reply may already have been brought to your
attention. However, in view of the importance of the question
and strong views of the interested officers of the Department,
I would appreciate it if you could give the matter very careful
consideration. The views of NA on the legal and political aspects
are set out in an attached memorandum.
TC
P. P. CIaxton, Jr.
As:l
Att.

AIDE-MEMOIRE
The Legation of New Zealand desires to/refer to the aide
memoire from the Department of State dated/April 13th, 1948
on the subject of Japanese manned Antarctic whaling expeditioms.
The Legation is instructed to inform the Department of
State that the New Zealand Government, while it appreciates
the action of the Government of the United States in recognising
its obligation to consult with other allied countries concerned,and
does not, having regard to the satisfactory record of the 1947-48
expedition in observing the International Whaling Convention,
object to the despatch of an expedition during the 1948-49 season,
it is still firmly of the opinion that the whole question of
894.628/5-648
Japanese participation in Antarctic whaling should be settled on
a regular basis in the Far Eastern Commission as a preliminary
to final determination in the Japanese Peace Treaty. Moreover,
the New Zealand Government is not convinced of the need for the
addition of a third factory ship to the proposed 1948-49
expedition.
1七
The New Zealand Government will of course expect to be
provided with details of the route of the expedition and its
operating area if and when the expedition is finally determined
upon.

expeditions up to the season of 1934/35. In the early
thirties cooperation was established between Norway and
Great Britain with a view to secure a certain limitation
of these activities for preservation purposes.
When Japan and Germany took up Antarctic whaling,
steps which incidentally formed parts of the preparations
on the sides of these governments for the World war II,
Norway and Great Britain took the initiative to bring
about an international convention for regulation of
whaling. Japan disregarded vigorous representations
from the other whaling nations, refused to join this
convention, and perpetuated its operations unbound by
the restrictions these nations had imposed upon them-
selves.
Confident that after the war her special whaling
interests would be respected, and that Germany and Japan
for years

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.98)
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY
- 3 -
WASHINGTON 7. D. C.
for years to come would be exoluded from Antarotio
whaling, Norway has made every effort to rebuild her
whaling fleet up to prewar level. At the same time
the Administration has been authorized by the legisla-
tive branch of the Norwegian Government to regulate
Norwegian whaling in order to prevent further expansion,
a power which has been used to a rather large extent.
In view of these circumstances, it will be under-
stood that the Norwegian Government was greatly alarmed
by the fact that Japan was given the opportunity to par-
ticipate with two expeditions in the 1946/47 Antarotic
whaling operations. The Norwegian Government had in
mind to raise this question at the International Whaling
Conference in Washington in 1946, but refrained from
doing so, confident that this was clearly a singular
occurrence.
It was therefore most unsatisfactory to the
Norwegian

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY
WASHINGTON 7. D. C.
Norwegian Government that Japan was permitted whaling
during the 1947/48 season, notwithstanding the fact
that other nations' expeditions, as could then well
be foreseen, would be able to catch the stipulated
quota of 16,000 bluewhale units.
For the coming season, another Norwegian factory
ship, which is the last one to be built under the plan
for reconstruction of the whaling fleet, will be ready
to participate, and Japanese participation will conse-
quently under no circumstances increase the total oil
production. It can only have the effect of reducing
the outcome of other countries' expeditions.
The results of the whaling season just concluded,
fully confirm that the quantity of bluewhale is steadi-
1y decreasing, and it is obvious that under these ciroum-
stances an increase of the total quota is out of question.
It will be known that bluewhale was in the past the
most important species, and the actual problem, which
may

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY
- 5 -
WASHINGTON 7. D. C.
may arise in the very near future, is that of further
reducing the quota.
The whaling industry is of paramount importance
to the Norwegian economy. For this reason, and in view
of the above, the Norwegian Government and Norwegian
whaling interests are gravely concerned about the in-
formation transmitted, that Japanese participation 1s
under consideration again, and even on an expanded soale.
In view of the above, the Norwegian Government
protests against continued Japanese whaling in the
Antarctic. It is confident that the United States
Government under its consideration of the matter at
issue will give full weight to the views submitted
herewith, end that the reasons for the protest will
be understood.
With regard to the supply of whale meat for Japan,
it can be added that, as far as cen be foreseen,
Norwegian whaling expeditions will be in a position
to deliver

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY
WASHINGTON 7. D. C.
to deliver greater quantities to Japanese transporters
than possible Japanese expeditions could produce. It
may be presumed that the Norwegian whaling industry will
be willing to do so, and at reasonable prices.
Norwegian Embassy,
Washington, May 14, 1948.

AIDE MEMDIRE
His Majesty's Government in the United Kingdom have
894.628/5-1848
studied the State Department's Aide Memoire of the 13th April,
1948, on the subject of the desire of the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers to authorise a 1948/49 SCAP-administered
Antarctic whaling expedition, and they welcome the invitation
to state their views.
2.
His Majesty's Government consider that the question
whether, in the pre-treaty period, any Japanese whaling expeditions
to the Anterctic should be permitted is one which should be
referred to the Far Eastern Commission for decision. In view
of Japan's bad record in pre-war years in failing to conform to
agreed international standards His Majesty's Government have
serious doubts as to the desirability of permitting the Japanese
to whale in Polar waters; and they believe that these doubts
are shared by a majority of the members of the Commission.
3.
In the long term the question whether Japan shall be
CS/V
allowed to participate in Antarctic whaling is one for the peace
treaty. It might well have the unfortunate effect of pre-judging
the issue if, in this interim period, Japan were permitted year
after year to send expeditions. It must in any event create an
impression in Japanese minds that their return to permanent
participation in Antarctic whaling is a foregone conclusion with
no guarantee that they would not eventually revert to their former
nefarious practices.
JUN-1 1913
AB
FILED
of
4.1

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
4.
The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers has stated
that he desires to authorise the Japanese Government to make
preparations for a 1948/49 SCAP-administered Antarctic whaling
expedition because of the continuing acute shortage of necessary
food products in Japan and the importance of maximising foreign
exchange resources for the procurement of essential imports for
Japan. But in no case could whale meat provide more than a
fraction of one percent of Japan's total protein supplies. In
the view of His Majesty's Government the argument that Japanese
expeditions are necessary to alleviate a shortage of essential
foods in Japan cannot, therefore, be mustained. The crux of
the matter is therefore whale oil. Japan does not need whale
oil in any substantial quantity and, even with two factory ships,
has produced an exportable surplus. Whale oil is moreover
allocated by the International Emergency Food Council and Japan
would receive her appropriate quota whether she sent a whaling
expedition herself or not. Her needs could then be served by
taking whele oil produced by the more modern and efficient allied
expeditions, thus allowing the Allied countries to take more
indigenous products or manufactures from Japan.
5.
When the 1946/47 expeditions were authorised there were
insufficient floating factories to exhaust the total of 16,000
blue whale units authorised by the International Convention. In
l947/48 the authorised catch was reached and the season had to be
curtailed by eight days in accordance with the terms of the Convention.
It is significant that the total reported catch of the two Japanese
expeditions is exactly equal to eight days' average catch by the
other expeditions and it is arguable that the season need not have
been curtailed but for the presente of the Japanese.
Their

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
participation in 1948/49 with three expeditions would cause even
more serious loss to all other whaling nations.
6.
While His Majesty's Government fully appreciate the
Supreme Commander's motives there are, in His Majesty's Government's
view, strong counter argumenta. It is clear that such advantage
in foreign exchange as will accrue to Japan as a result of the
1947/48 expeditions has been at the direct expense in hard currenoy
of the United Kingdom and other wheling nations. It is moreover
also clear from offers reaching London through commercial channels
that almost the sole purpose of future expeditions would be to
produce whale oil for conversion into hardened oil, which was the
form in which Japan mainly exported this product before the war.
This processing would need the import of substantial additional
quantities of fuel oil into Japan, of which, as the United States
authorities are well aware, there is a world shortage against
present world requirements.
7.
The export of Japanese whale oil in any form to the
sterling aree would deplete British dollar resources. In any
event the continued participation of Japan in Antarctic whaling
must prevent the United Kingdom from making full use of her own
resources and thus directly affect her economic recovery. The
authorisation of a third Japanese expedition would substantially
increase the adverse effect on United Kingdom economy and further
affect her dollar-earning capacity. In His Majesty's Government's
opinion the interests of the allied nations should be given first
consideration, and they would regard it as inequitable that Japan
should be favoured at their expense.
8.
His Majesty's Government would be grateful if the State

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Department would convey to the Supreme Commander their strong
objections to the suggestion that a further Japanese whaling
expedition should be permitted. His Majesty's Government trust
that their views on the competence of the Far Eastern Commission
in this matter are shared by the United States Government and
that it will not be found necessary to proceed with any scheme
such as that outlined in the State Department's Aide Memoire
without prior approval of the Commission.
BRITISH MBASSY,
WASHINGTON, D.C.
18th May, 1948.

Sir John Balfour, Chergé d'Affaires ad interim,

Mr. Norman Armour, Assistant Secretary

Sir John Balfour called this afternoon and under instrüotions
from his Government left an eide-mémoire strongly protesting
against SCAP's projeoted plan of authorizing a 1948-49 SCAP-
administered Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expedition. Sir John
emphasized the fact, which is not brought out in the attached
aide-mémoire, that SCAp's desire to add a third Japenese factory
ship this year would in fact put the Japanese in proportionatoly
greater strength in comparison with the other chier whaling
nations, namely the United Kingdom and Norway, than Japan was
before the war. The U.K. Government considered this a parti-
cularly serious matter in view of the fact that the whole
British and Norwegian pre-war whaling fleets were destroyed
during the war against the Axis Powers in which, of course,
Japen played a prominent part. It would seem to the U.K. Govern-
ment manifestly unfair that the Japanese should now be placed
by the victors in a superior position than previously.  The
C
S
U.K. Government feels very strongly that if SCAP considers,
in spite of the objections raised by protesting powers, that
FW894.628
it is necessary to dispatch a 1948-49 expedition he should at
least confine it to the two factory ships which took part in
previous expeditions.
Mr. Armour pointed out that the total Japanese catch during
the past season of only some 7oo blue whale units seemed to
him a very small proportion of the total permissible catch of
Ao
16,000
Ao

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
16,000 blue whale units. Mr. Graves pointed out, however, that
while it was small it nevertheless was equivalent to 8-days'
catch for the U.K. and Norweglan whaling fleets and that their
fleets had been penalized to that extent by the Japanese ex-
pedition. Mr. Armour further pointed out that in the view of
the United States Government it was not proper for the viotors
to take advantage of their position to impose on the vanquished
artificial restrictions on access to either markets or sources
of supply and that the United States did not therefore feel
that the Japanese should be prevented from Antarctio whaling
merely because they were an ex-enemy. Sir John said that
while this might be a proper position to take he nevertheless
wished to reiterate his former contention that the sending of
three factory ships this year would not only place Japan in an
equal position relative to the U.K. and Norwegian whaling
fleets but in proportionately a better position than she had
been prior to the war and that on any moral grounds this seemed
to the British iniquitous.
Mr. Armour concluded the interview by assuring Sir John
that his views would at once be made clear to the Supreme
Commender.

DATE: May 19, 1948
PROM : NA - Mr. Allison
NC
SUBJECT:
Dispatch of Attached Telegram re Third Whaling Expedition.
It seems clear from the third and fourth paragraphs of the
attached wire that SCAP now understands that the U.S. has ne
intention of actually authorizing him to permit the Japanese to
conduct a third whaling expedition, as we (mistakenly) did last
year, but that we intend that the formal responsibility shall
be his, with him, unavoidably, bearing the onus in the protesting
nationst eyes for the final decision. He has therefore seized
on the Australian and New Zealand thesis that the decision falls
within the province of the FEC and not of SCAP, but realizing
that if that argument were carried to its logioal conolusion
there would be no expedition, he proceeds to the proposal that
the U.S. issue an interim directive to him, insuring the expedi-
tion &nd shifting the onus to the U.s. Government.
It may be that the U.S. should bear the formal responeibility
and onus for the decision, coneidering that the U.S. stands to
394.628/5
gain most from the expedition. The proposal that we issue an
interim directive, however, seems improper and inadvisable for
several reasons:
(1) An interim directive would not provide an "inoontro-
vertible legal basis" for the action, since it would be hard to
show that it 1s presently an "urgent matter". Ir we admit FEC
jurisdiction, the proper thing would be for us to introduoe a
policy paper, allow adequate time for ite consideration, and then
issue an interim directive on the grounds of urgency and necessity0
SCaP does not propose this, however, but rather, as far as can
81
be gathered, that the interim directive be issued without any
preliminaries whatsoever.
(2) There is no legal basis for the Australian and New
Zealand contention that the matter is not within SCAp's proper
jurisdiction. The failure of the FEC to pass a policy deoision
on the subject after the completion of two expeditions olearly
leaves the matter within the authority of SCap, whose powers are
894.
plenary in any field where the FEC has not approved a policy
deoision.
(3) There is no reason to believe that the protesting
countries would be any better satisfied fof having gone through
the motions of an interim directive than if sCaP went ahead on
his own, with a statement, as we already intend, of approval and
S5
support
H
6

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
support by the U.S. Government.
(4) For the U.S. to issue an interim direotive for the
third expedition would constitute clear admission that we acted
1mproperly in authorizing SCAP, on our own authority and without
benefit of an FEC or U.S. interim directive, to go ahead with
the expedition last year.
(5) The suggestion that the U.S. issue an interim direotive
"permitting the Supreme Commander in his disoretion to authorize
the third expedition" seems particularly ill-advised and un-
necessary, since the Supreme Commander, as stated above, already
has the authority, in view of the fact that there has been no
FEC policy decision on the subject.
While the last two of the above five considerations have
a bearing on the problem, they are not necessary to our oase
and have been omitted from the proposed telegram to SCAP. It
is requested that this telegram be expedited in every possible
way in view of SCAP's previous statements that it is necessary
to authorize the expedition at the earliest possible date.
nAr

SUBJECT:
Japanese Whaling
Mr. Wilhelm Munthe de Morgenstierne, Ambassador of Norwayoo
PARTICIPANTS:

94.628/5-2448
Mr. J. M. Allison, Chief, NA

US POLAD, Tokyo.
The Norwegian Ambassador called this afternoon under instruotions
from his Government to re-emphasize the serious concern of his
Government at the projected SCAP-administered Japanese whaling
expedition to the Antarctic. The Ambassador referred to the aide-
mémoire (copy attached) which the Acting Counselor of the Norwegian
Embassy had left with Mr. Butterworth on May 14 and said that
he himself would have come in at that time except for illness.
The Ambassador pointed out tnat the wnaling industry was one of
the most important in the Norwegian economy and that the Norwegian
whaling industry, acting on the belief that because of Japan's
past bad record she would not be permitted to engage in whaling
CS/V
in the post-war period, had gone to considerable expense to
modernize ana increase its whaling fleet. Only recently, he
reported, a new 26,000-ton factory vessel had been completed at
a cost of approximately $14 million. This most recent addition
to the Norwegian whaling fleet brings the total number of factory
ships to some 1l or l2. With this large capital investment and
with the total permissible catch limited by international agree-
ment to 16,000 blue whale units, the Norwegian industry and
Government is naturally concerned at any threat to the possible
9
profitable operation of these ships. When the first Japanese
whaling expedition was announced two years ago, his Government,
matter in order to tide over tne difficult immediate post-war
period, but when the second expedition was authorized last year
and when
00

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
and when it was announoed that a third expedition was now con-
templated which would consist of 3 instead of the previous 2
factory ships with attendant catchers, his Government was
seriously alarmed. It was pointed out that the inoome from the
whaling industry was one of the most potent sources of dollar
revenue of the Norwegian Government and as such played a large
part in the ability of the Norweglans to rehabilitate their
country after the devastation of the war. The serlousness of
the problem was further emphasized by the fact that,instead of
increasing during the war period, the whale population had
apparently decreased so that there would be no possibility of
the 16,000 blue whale limit not being reached. The Ambassador
said that he realized how busy Secretary Marshall is at the
present time and that he therefore did not wish to bother him
unnecessarily, but that in view of the seriousness with which
his Government considered this matter he wondered whether or not
he should seek a personal interview with the Becretary.
Mr. Butterworth assured the Ambessador that the Department
was fully conscious of the special part that the whaling industry
played in the economy of Norway and he stated that he had caused
the Norwegian Embassy's aide-mémoire of May l4 to be sent
immediately to Tokyo for the information or the Supreme Commander.
Mr. Butterworth also assured the Ambassador that he would bring
this matter to the attention of the Seoretary and inform him
of the Norwegian Government's deep conoern and the faot that
the Ambassador had only refrained from seeing the Secretary
personally on the matter because of h1s consciousness of how
occupied the Seoretary 1s at the present time.
Oma

SECRET
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
APO 500
MEMORANDUM FOR THE CHIEF OF STAFF
1. THE PROBLEM.
Revi sion of the northern boundary of the present SCAP-
authorized Japanese fishing area is necessary in order to decrease
current navigational hazards to fishing vessels; to increase fish
production in the region concerned; and to achieve maximm utilisa-
tion of available fishing equipment.
II. FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM.
1. The northern boundary of the SCAP-authorised fishing
area runs close to the coast of Hokkaido, especially in the vicin-
ity of Soya-misaki, where the boundary rounds the northwest tip of
the island at a distance of only three miles, and where the boundary
line passes between Hokkaido and Kunashiri-ghima at a distanee of
only three miles from the Hokkaido coast. See SCAPIN-1033, 22
June 1946
2. Proximity of the established boundary line to the
Hokkaido coast denies local fi shermen access to a large part of
the traditional fi shing grounds upon which, in the past, they were
dependent for their livelihood. It reduces the catch of crabs and
other marine products, and increases the hasards of navigation by
requiring Japanese fi shing vessels to remain close to shore while
traversing certain sections of the northern coast.
3. Current Japanese fish production is estimated to be
approximately 3 to 3 million metric tons amnually. Both SCAP and
int erested Japanese Government agencies are actively engaged in a
progrm to obtain supplies and equipment sufficient to maintain
and, if possible, to increase production from the present authorised
area. However, present fishing intensity in the authorised area
is equal to, or greater than, that of the most intensive fishing
operations before the war. Consequently, it is not anticipated
that any c∞nsiderable increase in production can be obtained under
present limitations.
III. DISCUSSION.
1. SCAP has established a policy of permitting the Japa-
nese to fish in areas accessible to the Japanese fishing fleet:
a. When the fisheries resources of such areas are
not fully utilized by other nations.
b. When Japanese fishing operations will not com-
promise the security of other nations.

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
No. 323, June 5, 1948.
-2-
c. When fishing vessels, materials, and manpower
are available and adequate for such purposes.
2. The conditions set forth in the preceding paragraph
apply under the proposed revision.
3. Extension of the fishing area north of Hokkaido
would contribute to marine production in that region, particularly
of crabs, which are a valuable export item. Conversely, it would
tend to reduce current over-exploitation of the presently author-
ized northern fishing grounds, in accordance with SCAP policy to
obtain the highest possible marine production on a sustained yield
basis.
4. The hazards of navigation are increased because of
the present requirement that Japanese vessels keep within three
miles of certain stretches of coast along Hokkaido. Such hasards
are greater in the northern area during seasons when stormy and
foggy weather prevails. During such seasons, fishing operations
are reduced because of the three mile restriction.
5. Legitimate int erests of the USSR would not be af-
fected materially by adoption of the proposed change, because only
minimum shifts in the boundary line are involved. At no point will
the new boundary line approach beyond midpoint of the distance be-
tween the Hokkaido mainland and Soviet occupied territory.
6. The present Japanese fishing fleet is capable of ex-
ploiting a greater fishing area than currently authorised. With-
out extension of the authorized area, some fishing vessels must
either be idle part of the time or must concentrate in areas
which provide decreasing yields because of over intensive fishing.
This represents inefficient use of vital material and jeopardises
future production.
IV. CONCLUSIONS.
The northern boundary of the authorized fishing area, as
given in paragraph 2 of SCAPIN 1033, beginning with a point 45°30'
North Latitude, 140o East Longitude, should be changed to read as
follows: "Thence north to 45045' North Latitude, 1400 East Longi-
tude, thence east to 45045' North Latitude, 141054' North Longi-
tude, thence south to 45042' North Latitude, 141054' East Longi-
tude, thence east to 45042' North Latitude 142016' East Longitude,
thence north to 45045' North Latitude, 142016' East Longitude,
then east to 45045' North Latitude 145035' East Longitude; thence
south to a point approximately 44015' North Latitude, 145035' East
Longitude; thence keeping to mid-channel between Hokkaido and
Kunashiri-shima to a point midway between Nokke-saki, Hokkaido,
and Keramui-saki, Kunashiri-shima; thence to a point midway be-
tween Nosappu-misaki, Hokkaido, and Kaigara-jima; thence following
the boundary line described in SCAPIN 1O33."
V. RECOMMENDATION.
Recommend that the attached Memorandum for the Japanese
Government, amending SCAPIN 1033, dated 22 June 1946, be approved.
ment extending the authorized fishing area northward in aceordance
with the boundaries described in paragraph IV above
HUBERT G. SCHENCK, Lt Co1, CE

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch No. 234
of April 19, 1948 in which a plan of the Japanese Government for
patrol of the authorized fi shing area in the East China Sea was
described.
Following the rejection of the Japanese Government's plan by
the Chinese Mission in Japan as described in the refermce despatch,
the Natural Resources Section of this Headquarters proposed that
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers issue a memorandum to
the Japanese Government authorizing operations by a Japanese patrol
boat in both the East China Sea and the Yellow Sea; this proposal
was concurred in by G-2, G-3, and G-4 Sections of this Headquarters
1
and by the Commander, Naval Forces Far East. Copy of the staff
study and draft memorandum for the Japanese Government, prepared by
the Natural Resources Section, are enclosed.
This Mission, acting in its capacity as the Diplomstie Sestion,
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, did
2
not concur in the above proposal. Copy of the communication to the
Natural Resources Section dated June 7, 1948 stating the Diplomatic
Section's reasons for not concurring is enclosed. In consequence,
it is probable that this plan for a patrol will not be acted upen
for the present.
Officials of the Natural Resources Section consider that opera-
tion of a patrol by the Japanese Government is the only effective
method of reducing Japanese fi shing operations beyond the ares
authorized by SCAPIN 1033 of June 22, 1946. It is admitted that
Japanese fishing vessels are operating beyond the permitted area
despite warnings from both this Headquarters and the Japanese Govern-
ment. Legal action by Japanese courts against captains of vessels
alleged to have violated SCAPIN l033 has almost always resulted in
failure to convict, or in some cases, even to prosecute, because
of the lack of evidence required by Japanese law. Legal experts of
this Headquarters consider the Japanese standards of evidence in
these cases to be fair and concede that trials in United States
military courts against alleged violators would probably mo
successful. Surveillance of the authorised fishing area 
tion authorities is also unfeasible since naval units atteeh
RESTRICTED

=== Page 270 === (confidence 1.00)
Tokyo's No. 337,
June 9, 1948.
-2-
this Headquarters lack the necessary vessels, personnel and operat-
ing funds.
As the Diplomatic Section, this Mission is seeking clarifica-
tion from other Sections of Headquarters of the legal standards
imposed by Japanese law in cases of this kind. It is hoped that
this information can be conveyed to int erested governments and that
these governments can lend such assistance in supplying the re-
quired evidence that more effective legal action in Japanese courts
can be taken in the future.
Because Japanese fishing boats operate beyond the authorized
area, Korean and Chinese authorities are resorting to measures
which, in the opinion of this Headquarters, are illegal and inde-
fensible in order to punish violators. By command of the United
States Army Military Government in Korea, the Korean Coast Guard
has been directed to apprehend Japanese fishing craft operating in
Korean waters in violation of SCAPIN 1O33. Cases involving these
vessels are to be tried by United Stat es Army Provost Courts. If
a verdict of guilty is returned, the vessels are to be confiscated
and the Japanese crews deported to Japan, although no standards of
proof and no legal authority conferring jurisdiction over Japanese
fishing craft operating on the high seas on courts in Korea are
3
cited. Copy of a Korean Military Government letter dated April 24,
1948 containing this mandate is enclosed. Several cases where
Japanese fishing vessels have been confiscated in accordance with
the above have already been reported to this Headquarters. In a
communication dated May 17, 1948 to the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers, the Commanding General of United States Armed Forces
in Korea sought to justify this action by stating that "Korean ter-
ritorial waters are specified as those waters extending from the
shores of Korea to the line established by SCAP in September 1945
as extended 22 June 1946."
It is believed that the actions of United States Military
Government authorities in Korea in confiscating Japanese fishing
vessels can be corrected by military communication from the Com-
mander in Chief, Far East, to the Commanding General, United States
Armed Forces in Korea. Remedial action has already been initiated
in this Headquarters.
The Chinese Government in a memorandum to this Headquarters
dated May 8, 1948 stated that it would have to take appropriate
measures unless violations of the authorized area by Japanese fish-
4
ing boats are effectively checked. Copy of this memorandum is
enclosed. The fact that unconfirmed press reports of attacks by
Chinese vessels on Japanese fi shing boats have been noted by this
Headquarters may indicate what the Chinese intend by "appropriate
5
measures." Copy of one such press report is enclosed.
It is the opinion of this Mission that if the Chinese memo-
randum cont emplates measures of reprisal and attempts by the Chinese
Government to enforce orders of the Supreme Commander, these actions
will be in violation of international law and infringe upon the
authority of the Supreme Commander as the sole executive authority
for the Allied Powers in Japan. In so far as we are aware, nter-
national law provides no penalty for vessels carrying on normal
operations
RESTRICTED

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's No. 337,
June 9, 1948.
-3-
operations on the high seas, unless otherwise stipulated by tresty.
Nor does int ernational law appear to sanction arbitrary and 
reasonable extension by a nation of its territorial waters, as
directed by authorities in Korea in the communication quoted above,
and as possibly intended by the Chinese Government in its memo-
randum of May 8, 1948.
It would appear incontrovertible that jurisdiction to imple-
ment and enforce Allied policy toward Japan belongs exclusively to
the Supreme Commander, and that the Government of China is without
authority to punish infractions by Japanese of orders of the
Supreme Commander, unless such infractions clearly occur within
Chinese territorial waters. As indicated in enclosure No. 2,
should the Chinese Government endeavor to do so, this Mission as
the Diplomatic Section of this Headquarters would be compelled to
protest to the Chinese Mission in Japan to the effect that such
action by Chinese authorities is unwarranted.
Respectfully yours,

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
APO 500
MEMORANDUM FOR THE CHIEF OF STAFF
I. THE PROBLEM.
To maintain effective surveillance over the operations of
Japanese fishing boats which are restricted to SCAP-authorised
fishing areas within the East China and Yellow Seas.
II. FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM.
1. SCAPIN 1033 dated 22 June 1946 established authorised
areas for fishing and whaling by the Japanese. A portion of the
defined authorised area is in the East China Sea and southerm part
of the Yellow Sea.
2. The Japanese Government, in accordance with the r
quirements of SCAPIN 1033,is responsible for Insuring that Japanese
fishing boats remain within the authorized fishing area, and is re-
quired to prosecute, by appropriate action in the Japanese courts,
any violation of such instructions.
3. The Japanese Government has attempted to adhere to the
requirments of SCAPIN 1033, but has found it difficult to prevent
violations because of lack of authority to operate patrol vessels.
outside the muthorized fi shing area. Violations of SCAPIN 1033 by
Japanese fishing vessels have occurred frequently between Jamwery
1947 and March 1948. NR has information concerning eight cases of
violations involving 18 Japanese boats, occurring during the period
January 1947 to March 1948. Most of these violations have been
reported by the Chinese Government. In addition, it is reported
that there are approximately 10 Japanese boats now being retained
by Korean authorities on the harge of violations occurring during
the past two months.
4. Reports of violations obtained from the Chinese Goverm
ment were submitted through the Chinese Mission in Tokyo to Diplo
matic Section. These reports were based on observations by Chinese
fishing vessels of Japanese fishing boats operating beyond the
SCAP-authorized fishing area in the East China Sea.
III. DISCUSSION.
1. The establishment of a Japanese fishery patrol in the
East China Sea would help reduce the number of violations. The use
of a patrol boat would make it possible not only to establish u-
mistakable identity of violators but to obtain other besic infer-
mation concerning the violation which is needed to meet the legal
requirements
RESTRICTED

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.98)
Emolosure No. 1 to Tokyo's
No. 337, June 9, 1948.
- 2 -
requirements of Japanese courts. In some of the past reperted
cases of violations legal action has been delayed or not cempleted
because, in the opinion of the Japanese courts, insufficient evi
dence has been submitted. The Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry has already procured a suitable patrol vessel, the
Hatsutaka-Maru, of 286 gross tons. Personnel are available to mm
this vessel. A budget to cover the cost of patrol operations has
been submitted by the Ministry to the Diet for approval. On the
basis of discussions by NR with SCAJAP and G-3, it is proposed
that the patrolled area be bounded as follows: Within the Bast.
China and Yellow Seas south of 36o North Latitude and north of 24
North Latitude.
2. It is also stipulated that the Japanese patrol boat
will not approach closer than 12 miles to any mainland or to any
island within the authorized patrol area, which lies south of 36
North Latitude and north of 24oNorth Latitude, with the exseption
of the Japanese mainland or Japanese islands. Personnel from the
patrol vessel will not have contact with any of the inhabitants
from the foreign mainland or from the foreign islands inside of
the 12-mile limit area referred to herein.
3. One vessel cannot patrol a large part of the area at
one time but it will be able to provide concrete evidence on some
violations and the threat of its presence will exercise a psycho-
logical influence on other potential violators.
IV. RECOMMENDATION.
That a Japanese fishery patrol in the East China and
Yellow Seas be authorized in accordance with provisions outlined in
the attached SCAPIN /sub-enclosure.
