MEMORANDUM FOR: JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
SUBJECT:
Authorized Japanese Fishery Patrol in East China
and Yellow Seas.
1. Reference is made to Menorandum for Japanese Government,
from General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
AG 800.217 (22 Jun 46)NR, SCAPIN 1033, 22 Jume 1946, subjoct: "Area
Authorized for Japanese Fishing and Whaling."
2. Effective this date and until further notice, a Japanese
patrol for the purpose of maintaining surveillance over and report-
ing violations by Japanese fishing vessels which are restricted to
operating in present authorized fishing area, is permitted within
the area bounded as follows: Within the East China and Yellow Seas
south of 360 north latitude and north of 24° north latitude.
3. Authorization in paragraph 2, above, is subject to the
following provision:
a. The Japanese patrol boat will not approach closer
than 10 miles to any mainland or any island within the authorised
area which lies south of 360 north latitude and north of 240 north
latitude, with the exception of the Japanese mainland or Japanese
islands. Personnel from the patrol vessel will not have contact
with any of the inhabitants from the foreign mainlands or from the
foreign islands inside of the l0-mile limit area referred to berein.
4. The following vessel only is permitted to engage in patrol
duties in the area outlined in paragraphs 2 and 3, above: Hatautake-
Maru, 286 gross tons, operating under Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry.
5. The Japanese Government shall make such periodic reports
on patrol activities as may be required by General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
6. The present authorization is not an expression of allied
policy relative to ultimate determination of national jurisdiction,
international boundaries or fishing rights in the. area concerned
or in any other area.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
CHECK SHEET
DS/WJS/RBF/ogf
From: Diplomatic Section To: NRS
Date: 7 June 1948
1. DS does not concur in the plan for establishment of a
Japanese fishery patrol in the East Chinmand Yellow Seas as de-
scribed in the draft memorandum prepared by NRS.
2. DS considers that, on political grounds, authorisation
by SCAP of Japanese patrol boat operations within 12 miles of the
China coast from 36° to 24º north latitude would encounter strong
objections from the Chinese Government and would endanger present
Japanese fishing operations as well as plans for future expansion.
If the proposed patrol operation were authorized by SCAP, intensi-
fication of Chinese reprisals against Japanese fishing boats dis-
covered beyond the authorized fishing area and against other Jape-
nese vessels, such as SCAJAP authorized ships, might be expected.
In addition, Chinese objections to the United States proposal now
before the Far Eastern Commission for a southward and eastward ex-
tension of the authorized area might be intensified; the veto power
held by the Chinese Government in the Far Eastern Commission could
be fatal to this proposal.
3. The opposition of the Chinese Government to a Japanese
patrol in the East China Sea is indicated by the Chinese rejection
of the patrol plan embodied in NS c/n check note of Docember 26,
1947 to DS, although it is recognized that the Chinese rejoction
(see DS c/n of March 18, 1948 to NRS) included the plan for inter-
mittent fishing by the patrol boat as well as the plan for establish-
ment of the patrol. DS nevertheless believes that an attempt to ac-
complish unilaterally what failed on a preliminary bilateral ap-
proach will create needless opposition.
4. DS also believes that on practical grounds the present
proposal is open to objection. It is manifestly impossible for one
patrol boat to patrol effectively the vast area desoribed in the
attached map. In addition, the implication that Japanese fishing
boats might now be operating as near as 12 miles from.the Chinese
coast tends to cast doubt on the effectiveness of SCAP orders.
5. As DS views this problem, the control of fishing vessels
within or without authorized areas is essentially a matter for
determination by SCAP as the sole executive authority for the Allied
Powers. In consequence, SCAP should not agree to the principle ap-
parently maintained by the Chinese Government that Japanese fish-
ing vessels apprehended outside of the authorised areas are subject
to disciplinary action on the part of the Chinese authorities.
Even
RESTRICTED

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.97)
Eaclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's
o. 337, Jume 9, 1948.
- 2 -
Even though a Japanese fishing vessel should be eagaged in fish-
ing outside the authorised fishing area but on the high sees and
not within Chinese territorial waters, the limit of Chinese jurie-
diction over such fishing vessel would be to report apprepriste
information to SCAP in order that disciplinary action may be taken
by SCAP. It is strongly recommended, should a situation arise
where a Japanese fishing vessel is apprehended and taken into
Chinese waters by a Chinese Government vessel, that a protest
based upon the forogoing principles should immediately be filed
with the Chinese Mission in Japan.
6. In summary, DS is unable to concur with the proposed patrol
for political reasons, and on the ground that the patrol would in
any event be ineffective and endanger the proposed extension of
the present authorized fishing areas.
-W.J.S.

SUBJECT: Japanese Fishing Craft in Unauthorised Waters

1. It is anticipated that in the future certain cases will
be referred to Provost Courts for trial involving the capture of
Japanese deep sea fishing craft caught in Korean waters in viale-
tion of the fishing areas established by the Supreme Commender
Allied Powers in Septenber 1945 and as extended on 22 Jume 1946.
2. The Korean Coast Guard has been directed to apprebend
these fishing craft when found in Korean waters, and to deliver
then to the nearest Korean Customs Office. The Customs officials
have been directed to refer these cases to the nearest US Army
Provost Court for trial.
3. Should the trial result in a verdict of guilty, the
Provost Court will crder the fishing craft confiscated and placed
in the custody of the Marine Bureau of the Department of Tramsporta-
tion. The Department of Transportation has been instrusted to
make all confiscated fishing craft available to the Korean
Fisheries Corporation.
4. Japanese personnel aboard the confiscated vessels will be
detained and ordered deported to Japan. In order to acoomplish
this deportation the Office of Foreign Affairs, SKIG, will be
notified by letter, giving complete information as to names,
addresses, etc., of the persons to be deported.
/s/ W. F. DEAN

CHINESE MISSION IN JAPAN
TOKYO
MEMORANDUM
Referring to the reported cases of Japanese fishing boats
operating outside the authorized area, the Chinese Mission wishes
to express its appreciation for General Headquarters' action in
having repeatedly instructed the Japanese Government to investigate.
However, despite GHQ's efforts some Japanese fishermen are still
unlawfully fishing in unauthorized areas and many cases of viola-
tions have further been reported to this Mission.
Under these circumstances, the Mission is directed by its
home Government to notify the Japanese Government, through GHQ,
SCAP, that the Chinese Government will have to take appropriate
measures, unless such violations are effectively checked.
Tokyo, 8 May 1948

CHINA NAVY SINKS JAPANESE VESSEL
SHANGHAI, June 4 (UP)—The Chinese Navy disclosed that ome
Japanese fishing vessel was sunk and another boat captured out-
side the mouth of the Yangtze river May 29, but the Japemese
crew members were picked up and now are detained for questioning.
In all, 25 Japanese are being held, the navy said.
Chinese reports, meanwhile, claimed that the crewmem were all
former Japamese Navy men "who were sent into Chinese territoriel
waters by high Japanese authorities."
The reports further claimed that the Japanese vessels sent
urgent messages in secret code to Tokyo as the Chinese gumbest
closed in on then.
SCAP officials said they had received no information from the
Chinese Government regarding reports of alleged violation of
Chinese waters by Japanese fishing vessels.

Dear Mr. Holm:
This will acknowledge your letter of June 25, 1948
to Mr. Looney of this office relative to certain legal
aspects of a project whereby you would purohase fisk on
the high seas of the Central Pacifie from Japanese Wessels
and transfer it to American vessels for shipment to apd
processing in the Territory of Hawaii.
As you state in your letter, there are two majer
legal aspects involved in the project you propese.
Control of Japanese fishing is vested in the Supreme
Commander of the Allied Powers in Tokyo and the feasibility
of your operation depends upon the regulations whieh he
may have in effect now or in the future. The seeend
legal point concerns the eligibility of your carge to
enter American ports as a product of an American fishery.
With regard to the regulations of SCAP, it is net
within the competence of this Department to advise you
either on present or on future regulations and I aseer-
CS/V
dingly suggest that you write to Scap for this infermatien.
The question concerning the entry of the fish inte the
United States is within the province of the Treasury
Department and I am, therefore, referring your letter to
The Bureau of Customs in that Department for answer
directly to you.
We are gratified that you found the Department of
assistance to you in your recent visit to Washington.

Dear Butterworth:
At long last I have an opportunity to answer your letter of
June 2, 1948, on the subject of the third whaling expedition. In
the interim, as you are aware, the announcement has been made and
preparations for the third expedition are now going forward on
schedule. I did what I could here to convince the working levels of
Headquarters that only two factory ships should be sent, in view of
the rumpus which was raised when three were contemplated. Prior to
General MacArthur's announcement, it was fairly well settled in Head-
quarters that only two ships should be sent and since that time it
appears that everyone is satisfied.
I appreciate the information concerning what happened in Washing-
NA
ton with regard to the UNTCOK matter. I have not attempted to tell
General MacArthur the details as the whole affair appears to have
blown over and no useful purpose would be served at this time again
to raise the subject. If, at some subsequent interview, it should
appear appropriate to tell him this background, I will, of course, do
so but it might be just as well to let sleeping dogs lie.
Drumright arrived on June 26 and I am glad at long last to have
some competent help with the many burdensome details that have been
my lot for a long time. I feel certain that he will take hold in very
short order and do an excellent job here. In this connection, I might
mention that Drumright was told that Cabot is making an effort to have
2
him transferred to Shanghai. I hope that this will not transpire as
I really need some relief and easing off of carrying most of the burden
alone. I also believe that the time has arrived when some consideration
should be given to my being recalled to Washington for consultation--it
has been eighteen months since I have been home--but I will make this
the subject of another letter at a later date.
-2848
With kindest personal regards.
Sincerely yours,
Brill Suace
W. J. Sebald
W. Walton Butterworth, Esquire,
Director, Office of Far Eastern Affairs,
Department of State,
Washington 25, D.C.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D.C.
Sir;
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch No. 140 of
1
March 2, 1948 and to enclose five copies of a directive from this
Headquarters to the Japanese Government (SCAPIN 1913 of June 21, 1948)
authorizing Japanese whaling operations in the Antarctic for the 1948-
1949 season. This directive, which has been issued to the Japanese
Government and thereby made public, authorizes an expedition consist-
ing of two fleets and in other respects is substantially similar to
the authorization given the Japanese Govemment for the 1947-1948
ACTION
Antarctic whaling expedition (SCAPIN 1737 of June 21, 1947).
Also enclosed is a copy of a staff study dated March 10, 1948
TU:
prepared by the Natural Resources Section of this Headquarters for
2FE
submission to the Supreme Commander's Chief of Staff. This staff
study, which described the necessity of a third Japanese Antaretic
whaling expedition, proposed that three Japanese whaling fleets be
authorized to operate in the Antarctic rather than two fleets as in
the past. The proposal was approved by this Headquarters and a radio
requesting the approval of the United States Government was dispatched
by the Supreme Comnander to the Department of the Army (CX 59325 of
Bdap
March 18, 1948).
In reply, WAR 996O8 of April 15, 1948 from CSCAD ECON to SCAP
stated that an assurance had been provided the British Government by
the Department of State on October 8, 1947 that the British and other
interested governments would be advised should the Supreme Commander
contemplate authorization of an expedition for the 1948-1949 season
and that such views as these governments might care to submit would
be Torwarded to him for his careful consideration. Some delay in the
FILE
final formulation of plans for the 1948-1949 expedition resulted.
Because of this delay and because of strong opposition by inter-
ested governments to the expedition, and particularly to the addition
of a third whaling fleet, authorization of the third expedition was
dimited to two fleets.

Expedition for the 1948-1949
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COM-LANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
HGS/WCH/CMA/vs
APO 500
10 March 48
MEMORANDUM FOR THE CHIEF OF STAFF
I. THE PROBLEM
Authorization of three Japanese whaling fleets to operate
in the Antarctic whaling area during the 1948-49 whaling season.
II. FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. SCAPIN 1103, dated 6 August 1946, authorized the Japa-
nese to conduct whaling operations in the Antarctic for the 1946-47
season.
2. This operation consisted of 2l vessels including two
factory ships and accompanying catcher boats and tramsports. The
expedition produced 22,229 metric tons of meat, blubber amd other
products and 12,260 metric tons of whale oil. The meat and blubber
produced was all consumed in Japan and represented 42.5 percent of
all animal meat produced in Japan in 1946. The whale oil had a
world market value of about 4, 866,000. Of the total mount of whale
oil produced, 5,000 metric tons were consumed in Japan md the balmee
was allocated to USMG in Germany.
3. SCAPIN 1737 dated 21 June 1947 authorized the Japanese
to conduct whaling operations in the Antarctic for the 1947-48 whal-
ing season.
4. This operation consists of 27 vessels including two fac-
tory ships and accompanying catcher boats and transports. Operations
will be completed on about 8 March 1948 and it is expected that the
production of about 30,000 metric tons of meat and blubber and about
18,O00 metric tons of whale oil will be realized. The inerease in
production over the 1946-47 expedition is accounted for by the fact
that catching and processing equipment was improved and the present
expedition will operate approximately three weeks longer than the
previous one.
5. The SCAP-administered, Japanese-manned whaling expedi-
tions were authorized because  a critical shortage of protein foods
and edible oils existed in Japan. Authorization permitted access to
world resources in international waters and was in accord with pro-
visions of Pot sdam Declaration which stated in part: "Japan shall be
permitted to maintain such industry as will sustain her economy and
permit the execution of just reparations in kind, but not those which
SECRET

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's
No. 406, July 2, 1948.
-2-
would enable her to rearm for war. To this end, access to, as dio-
tinguished from control of, raw materials shall be permitted." The
authorization permitted Japan to make a material contribution to the
world supply of protein foods and edible oils by using Japanese-
manned vessels and equipment.
6. Four nations engaged in whal ing operations in the
Antarctic in the 1946-47 season with a total of 15 factory ships. In
the 1947-48 season, two more nations entered the field, bringing the
total number of factory ships in operation to 17. Norway is reported
to have nine of the factories in operation and to be supporting a
fleet from South Africa.
7. The Int ernational Whaling Convention) signed in Washington,
D.C., 2 December 1946, provides that no more than 16,000 blue whale
units will be taken from waters south of 40° South Latitwd (Antaretic
whaling area) in the 1947-48 season. (A blue whale unit eonsists of:
One blue whale; two fin whales; or six sei whales.) A radio from
International Bureau for Whaling Statistics, Sandefjord, Norway, to
the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries dated 25 February 1948 states that
by 2l February the total number of blue whale units taken in the
Antarctic was 1l,556. (This report includes the catch of all partic-
ipating nations except Russia whose report for the week 14-21 February
had not been received.) The 1947-48 season officially ends on 7 April
1948. The approximate average weekly catch for all nations during
this season is 1,O0o blue whale units. Thus, it may be concluded that
the 16,000 allowable blue whale units will not be taken during this
season unless the catch continues at the present rate, which is un-
likely due to adverse weather conditions, and unless all of the fac-
tory ships presently engaged continue operating until the end of the
season. The Japanese expedition will complete operations on or about
8 March 1948.
8. The critical shortage of food and edible oils noted in
paragraph 5, above, remains acute. The justification advanced for
authorization of Antarctic whaling operations by Japanese-manned ves-
sels for the past and present season continues in force.
III. DISCUSSION
1. In order for Japan to obtain an equitable share of whale
products from the international waters of the Antarctic, it is advis-
able that a whaling expedition authorized for participation in the
1948-49 season should be composed of three factory vessels and accom-
panying catcher boats and transports. More complete utilization of
existing equipment would thus be made as a sufficient number of
catcher boats are available in Japan and the conversion of other ve
sels to a factory ship and cargo carriers is possible.
2. That some nations which engage in Antarctic whaling will
object to increased participation by Japanese-manned vessels may be
expected.
3. One of the grounds upon which objections may be based is
that any increase in the number of whales taken by Japanese vessels
will
SECRET

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's
No. 406, July 2, 1948.
-3 -
will reduce by that amount the number which may be taken by other
participating nations. Regarding the validity of an objection of
this nature, the following points may be considered:
a. The purpose and intent of the establishment of a
seasonal catch limit by International Whaling Conventions was to
preserve and protect the stock of certain species of whales and not
to create an advantage for any nation or group of nations.
b. The objection is prejudicial and inhumanitarian. It
is in direct contradiction to the fourth basic principle laid down
in the Atlantic Charter which states as follows: "They will endeavor
with due respect for their existing obligations to further the enjoy-
ment by all States, great or small, victor or vanquished, of access,
on equal terms, to the trade and to the raw materials of the world
which are needed for their economic prosperity." This principle is
also one of those incorporated in the declaration by the United Nations.
4. Criticism was levied against resumption of Japanese
participation in Antarctic whaling in 1946 because Japan was never a
party to International Whaling Conventions and her operations were in
some cases in violation of the principles of good conduct as defined
by the Conventions. Japan's methods of operation were considered
wasteful of natural resources. This criticism may be reiterated as
an objection to increased participation by Japan.
5. The number of barrels of oil obtained from each blue
whale unit is used internationally as a basis for determining effi-
ciency of operations and utilization of whale carcasses. The yield
of llo barrels per blue whale unit is considered a satisfactory level
of production. The average oil yield of the two Japanese-manned
fleets from 8 December 1947 to 14 February 1948 is 114.7 barrels.
6. International Whaling Conventions specify that it is
prohibited to take whales under certain lengths and lactating whales
for processing. It is the desire of all member nations that this
restriction be strictly observed but cccscionally, due to fog or snow
or misjudgment by 2 gunner, an illegal whale is killed. Statistics
available show that Norwegian expeditions have a record of less than
1 percent of illegal whales killed. This record is probably better
than that of any other participating nation. Sufficient statistics
to make an actual comparison are not available. By 21 February 1948
one of the Japanese-manned fleets had a record of 1.1 percent. The
other has taken no illegal whales. If the first fleet continues to
take whales without a further violation the percentage will be re-
duced to possibly less than 1 percent. The record of the second
fleet is exceptional.
7. From a standpoint of efficiency of operation and
observance of International Whaling Conventions, Japan has proved she
is capable of maintaining a standard comparable to that of other
whaling nations.
8. In discussing the proposed third Japanese Antarctic
Whaling Expedition with other interested SCAP sections and other
headquarters, the following comments were received:
a. N. W. Bard, Capt USN, Chief of Staff, COMNAVFE,
stated: "The deci sion to authorize Antarctic Whaling Expeditiona i
SECRET

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's
No. 406, July 2, 1948.
-4-
not within the purview of COMNAVFE's responsibility. It is desired
to invite attention to the fact that there are insufficient operable
vessels available for a third fleet. There are laid-up vessels which
could be recommissioned for this purpose if so authorised."
b. A check note from G-4 to NR dated 2 March 1948, sub-
ject: "Antarctic Whaling" stated: ". . . G-4 offers no objection in
principle to the proposed increase in whaling activities during the
1948-1949 season. However, the implementation of this increased pro-
gram is contingent upon completion of financing arrangements for the
additional fuel oil requirements. The FY 1949 GARIOA budget was pre
pared on the basis of two fleets only. The additional fleet will in-
crease requirements by 50%. It is believed this additional fuel can
be provided, but the cost must be defrayed either by increased appro-
priation to Project 132 or by allocation of funds from the SCAP Trust
Fund."
9. Radio from WAR (WDSCA ECON) to SCAP, WAR 85641, dtg
O42309Z September 1947, referred to the 1947-48 Japanese Antaretic
Whaling Expedition and stated in part: "The Britiah Government would
be grateful if the U.S. Government would give the followdng assurancesi
a. No further Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expoditions
whatever will be authorized without concurrence FEC until docision on
Japanese whaling has been reached by peace conference.
b. Adequate steps will be taken by SCAP to ensure all
international whaling regulations are scrupulously observed by second
Japanese Antarctic whaling expedition and Allied observers will be
permitted to accompany expedition to supervise its complianee with
those regulations.
c. All supplies of whale oil obtained by apedition
will be made available for allocation by International Emergency Food
Council."
10. Radio from SCAP to WAR (WDSCA ECON) C-55377, dtg
091009z September 1947, is in reply to the radio referred to in para-
graph 9 above. This radio indicated that assurance requested in para-
graph 9 above should not be given, and that assurances requested in
paragraph 9 b and 9 c above had been given.
IV. RECOMMENDATIONS
1. That a SCAP-administered, Japanese-manned expedition be
authorized to participate in Antarctic whaling during the 1948-49
season and the number of factory ships be limited to three.
2. That a radio be sent to Department of the Army stating
that SCAP is taking the action recommended in paragraph 1 above and
that the expedition will be operated under the same general conditions
as were the two previous expeditions.
HUBERT G. SCHENCK
Lt Col

Chief, Natural Resources Section

Enclosure to Despatch No. 429 dat

subject: "Enforcement of Directives Li

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COM4ANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
MMORANDUM
Tokyo, June 22, 1948.
Reference is made to the Chinese Mission's memorandum of May
8, 1948 and to the Diplomatic Section's memorandum of February 6,
1948 concerning reports of violations by Japanese fishing vessels
and crews operating beyond the authorized fishing area in the East
China Sea.
As indicated in the Diplomatic Section's memorandum of Febru-
ary 6, reports of violations received from the Chinese Mission
have been found to lack sufficient probative value to warrant con-
victions of captains of the concerned vessels by Japanese courts.
In order to assist prosecution in Japanese courts, the Diplomatic
Section has endeavored to secure clarification of the standards of
evidence required.
According to expert information the following documents are
essential to Japanese procurators in their efforts to convict vio-
lators of Occupation directives establishing fishing areas:
1. An affidavit or other formal document giving the name
and position of the Japanese vessel alleged to be operating
beyond the authorized area and stating the date of the occur-
rence. The manner in which the vessel was identified should
be clearly noted and the procedure by which the violation as
recorded, whether in the vessel's log, by radio communication
to the appropriate Chinese authorities or by other means,
should be established. This document should be signed by a
competent observer and wherever possible the names of other
witnesses should be affixed. Other evidentiary material such
as photographs of the Japanese vessel concerned would be of
considerable use.
2. A document certified by responsible officials of the
Chinese Government, stating that the reported information has
been verified and is considered to be reliable.
Inasmuch as fishing activities of Japanese on the high seas are
governed by directives issued by the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers and which are subject to enforcement by the Supreme Commander
only, the competent authorities are desirous that information be sup-
plied as far as practicable in the form prescribed, in order that ade-
quate prosecution can afford appropriate protection to the legitimate
interests involved. The Mission's kind assistance is solicited.
To the
Chinese Mission in Japan,
Tokyo.

OE has been asked to concur in a request made upon 0 to obtain declassifica-

RM/R
proposal for the following reasons:
groups to participate in policy formulation with respect to occupied ares
matters. Often the groups involved would be affected by the policies formmls
Cases in point, for example, are textile producers, textile machinery producep
toy manufacturers. Both the Department of State and the Department of the
Army have consistently felt required not to accede to such requests. Consuls
tion of the kind here recommended for one industry group, if not extended tetN
other industry groups, would constitute discrimination in the formalatien ef
policy; if all groups were consulted on such matters the Department would
an impossible administrative and policy task.
2. The function of the Department in the formulation of polioy is to
consider the interests of all who would be affected by a policy. The policy
taxpayers) and therefore more than the industry should be consulted if
consultations are to be held. The Department should not jeopardise its
position by selection of only the windustry" as those with whom to consult.
3. The proposal is questionable in terms of the security classificatien
propos ed. The policy involved appears to fit the criteria for the "Confidenti
classification. On p. 7 of the Security Handbook it is stated that the
classification "Confidential" should be given to "material relating to inter
departmental consultations on questions of policy, the premature disolesure of
which might precipitate undesirable public controversey." The memorandum from
Mr. Chapman to Mr. Lewis recognizes the importance of SANacc 99/4 in these 
quoted terms.

anese fishing in part of the mandated areas.

FROM
SUBJECT:
Request for Declassirication of "Conclusions", pages 23-24,
paragraph 4 thru 7 of SANACC 99/4 15 Apr11 1948.
I have not initialled the attached memorandum of
Mr. Chapman requesting declassirication of the
Be Returned to
"Conclusione" of SANACC 99/4. I do not believe declas-
sification of this paper is necessary in order to permit
consultation with the leaders of the fishing industry
prior to final formulation of the State Department views.
FW
The Conclusions of SANACC 99/4, particularly that 1n
paragraph 4, are entirely in line with overall U.S.
894.628/8-1048
commercial policy and specifically in accordance with
our general policy that we will not take advantage of
a defeated enemy to gain economic benefits for any U.S.
industry. Paragraph 4 of the Conclusions is also in
line with paragraph 1l of the Potsdam Declaration which
provides that Japan shall have "access to, as distin-
guished from control of raw materials". In similar
situations affecting other American industries, such as
textiles, it has not been considered necessary te make
confidential papers accessible to members of the industry
concerned. I see no reason why Mr. Chapman cannot carry
on consultations with the fishing industry in which the
views of that industry can be elicited on problems
raised without the necessity of giving precise informa-
tion as to the terms of proposed U.S. polioy. For
example, it would seem to me to be perfectly in order
for Mr. Chapman to request the views of the fishing
industry as to possible extension at this time of Japan-
8
ose fishing areas or the fishing industry's views as to
permitting Japanese access eventually to all high seas
areas. This can be done without any statement on
Mr. Chapman's part that it 1s presently contemplated
that this will be the recommended U.s. policy.
m
From a detailed administrative point of view, I
894.628
am not certain whether it is possible in the time allowed
to have the Conclusions to this paper declassified even
if it was advisable to do so, as it may be that it would
be necessary to obtain the agreement of the Army, Navy
and Air Force to such declasqification.

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.98)
RESTRICTED
of the United States Pelsa
for Japen, Tekyo, entitled 
Government Monograph, and Natural
Resources Section Comments".
(COPY)
Comments by
Natural Resources Section
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
August 26, 1948.
NR's comments on the monograph "Fishery Problems" prepared by the
Foreign Office, Japanese Government, are based on data collected frem
the Japanese Government, the fishing industry and other sourees. Prob-
lems considered in the monograph are closely related to some of the
principal problems dealt with by NR, consequently, much effort has been
expended during the past two years on compilation, checking, and analysis
of data in order to obtain the most reliable informstion possible.
Japanese statistics on the same subject obtained from different
sources or at different times, practically always differ to a greater or
lesser extent. This is taken into consideration in these comments. Only
major differences in values for the same item are thomght to be sigmifi-
cant and are remarked on in the following paragraphs.
It is stated on page 2 of the monograph that the average cateh for
1930-34, minus the catch of overseas territories, was 4,851,000 metrie
tons. The most reliable figures that we have for the Japmese csteh,
not including colonial and overseas, average 3,618,000 metric tons for the
1930-34 period. If the colonial catch is included, it averages 5,021,000
metric tons. Apparently, the colonial catch is included in the Japemese
figure. This is misleading since practically all of the colonial cateh
was consumed in the colonies or exported from the colonies to natioms
other than Japan. On the same page, the 3,566,000 metric tons showm for
the coastal and inland fisheries is about 639,000 metric tons higher them
our figures. Their figure of 6,194,000 metric tons for 1933 (exsept for
overseas territories) does not compare to our figure of 5,686,000 metrie
tons for the entire Japanese empire.
On page 6, figures are given in the monograph for catches during
1944-47 compared to the 1930-34 average. The catch given for 1945 is
about equal to our figure for reported catch. This is considerably lower
than the actual catch since it makes no allowance for an unknowm but
considerable percentage of fish handled through the black market. The
1947 figure also is t∞ low according to the figures we have from various
sources, on the percentage of fish in the black market. Consequently, the
status they show for the postwar catch as compared to the 1930-34 cateh,
is misleading.
The fioree
RESTRICTED

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
Enol. No. 2 to
Tokyo's 567
Augst 30, 1948
-2-
RESTRICTED
The figures shown on Page 7 and 8 for number and tonnage of the
postwar fishing fleet as compared to the prewar fishing flest are
misleading. Data on the postwar fleet of powered boats of 5 tons and.
over, obtained from the complete registration of Japanese boats required
by the recent "Fishing Boat Registration Law" show the present flest of
powered craft to be greater in number than the prewar fleet. Tonnage
is less because of the reduction in large factory ships used in the
Antarctic and overseas fisheries. If the figures are adjusted for such
factory ships, which are not now needed, they show the present fleet to
be greater than the prewar fleet in both numbers and tonmage. Steel
boats have increased over 90% in number and 40% in tonnage. The total
for all powered wood boats has increased 1% in number and 7% in tonnage.
The figures shown on page 10 for fishing materials are much too high.
Heavy oil requirements are given as 560,000 kilolitres. In 1936, the year
of peak catch, records show only 440,000 kilolitres used in both colonial
and local fisheries, while 1939 and 1940 show 383,000 kilolitres and
301,000 kilolitres, respectively. The monograph gives 15,000 metrie tons
as the requirement for raw cotton. The best records available show the
average cotton consumption 1932-36 as about 7,000 metric tons. We estimate
1948 requirements at about 14,000 metric tons because of the poor condition
of the gear resulting from low replacements during the last several years.
The monograph shows manila hemp requirements as 33,000 metrio tons. The
average prewar requirements during years of peak catch were about 16,500
metric tons. Our best figures show current requirements as about 23,000
metric tons, but once the deficit in gear is made up, this will drop back
to about 16,000 metric tons annually. If there is some extension in fish-
ing areas, which now appears possible, postwar requirements may equal but
should not exceed prewar.
The Japanese report on page 14 shows an average of about 2,713,000
metric tons of fish used as food between 1930-34. Our best figures for the
same period are 2,862,000 metric tons which is a good agrement. Our
figures show that this is about 74% of the fish landed in Japan. The
Japanese figures show fish used for food as a percentage of the total
production of the Japanese empire. This reduces the percentage to 48%
since a large part of the colonial production was used for fish oil and
fertiliser for export. Their estimate for the production necessary to give
the Japanese the same amount of fish for food as they received in 1930-34
is 5,542,000 metric tons. This assumes that 40% of production would be
used for inedible fish meal and oil and for exports. Our figures show
that from 1930-34 about 74% of fish landed in Japan was used for food in
spite of the fact that large quantities of sardines and herring were made
into non-edible meal and oil. With greater accent now than in 1930-34 on
the use of fish for food, it should be possible to increase this percentage
to 80 or 90 percent. With 80% used for food, the Japanese would receive
the same amount of fish per person (assuming a population of about 78,000,000)
as in 1930-34, from a production of 4,000,000 metric tons. With 90%
utilisation for food, the total catch requirement would be 3,560,000 metric
tons. On this basis, Japanese food requirements would be met but little
fish would be available for export.
The figures given on pages 18 and 19 of the monograph for expected
catches by 1950 are unrealistic.
RESTRICTED
The 500.000

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
Enal.No. 2 to
Tokyo's 567
August 30,1948
-3-
RESTRICTED
 The 500,000 metric tons they show for Antarotic
whaling are close to their prewar peak. Such a catch would
require about 7,150 blue whale units which is about 45% of
the quota for the entire world. This assumption is fantastic
unless Japan resumes unrestricted whaling operations. The
100,000 metric tons of finished products is not impossible if
the improved utilisation of whales required of the Japmese
by .SCAP is contimued. Based on the performance of the 1947-58
expedition, this quantity of finished products could be ob-
tained from 2,200 blue whale units, which is 14% of the world
quota. This is about double the average catch of the two post-
surrender expeditions.
b. Figures given for their high-seas fisheries, northerm
seas fisheries, and trawling fisheries, assume that these
waters will be completely, or almost completely, open to
Japanese fishing operations. This assumption does not appear
realistic under present world conditions.
c. The Japanese expect a total production of 4,380,000
metric tons by 1950. According to their figures, this would
leave a deficit of 1,000,000 metric tons to provide the sme
per capita daily intake of protein as in 1930-34. Our figures,
shown in paragraph 7, indicate it would be possible to attain
an average protein intake comparablè to 1930-34 with a catch of
between 3,600,000 metric tons and 4,000,000 metric tons: A
goal of 3,600,000 metric tons is a practical one for 1950,
4,000,000 metric tons is remotely possible. The goal of
5,540,000 metric tons they give is completely unrealistic.
The requirements for vessels, oil, cotton and manila hemp for l950
shown on page 20, are as unrealistic as the expected production figures.
The present Japanese fishing fleet is greater than is required by the present
authorized area and, except possibly for tuna boats, is sufficient to exploit
any additional areas which might be opened to Japanese fishing between now
and 1950. This can be substantiated by our owm figures and by numerous
statements obtained from Japanese to the effect that crowded conditions re-
sulting from too many boats in the present area are leading to financial
failure of many operators. The only additional boats required between now
and 1950 will be for replsoement purposes and perhaps a small number for
special purposes. Requirements for fuel oil, cotton yarn and mamila hemp
are also excessive according to any figures which we can obtain on past
and current records.
On page 21, paragraph 2, it is stated "the fishing industry has ad-
vantage in that it does not involve the resources of other countries". If
the Japanese believe this, then they are probably the only people who labor
under such a delusion. Certainly no other Pacific nation is ready to
concede that the aquatic resources of the Pacific are the property of the
Japanese people. In paragraph 3, it is stated that the principal problems
of the future are to "better the method of hauling fish and improve the
percentage utilized for food". The first problem is hardly a major one
for Japan today when over-exploitation already exists in many of her
fisheries. The second problem stated is important, but the most important
RESTRICTED
problem

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl: No. 2 to
Tokyo's 567
August 30, 1948
RESTRICTED
problem is not even mentioned. This is the development and application
of methods for managing the coastal fish populations in order to maintain
their productivity. Japanese neglect of this factor is one of the chief
reasons their activities are so resented and feared by other fishing
nations.
On the whole, the report exaggerates the magnitude of prewar production,
under-estimates war-time and post-surrender production, exaggerates the re-
quirements for fishing materials needed now and in the future, exaggerates
the eatch necessary to provide an animal protein supply to the Japanese
people equal to that of 1930-34, and assumes that by 1950 the Japanese will
be allowed to resume fishing operations on much the same basis as during the
prewar period. These conclusions are achieved apparently by a conscious
selection of slanted data to prove their case rather than by use of unbiased
and representative figures. If this report is released for general dis-
tribution in Japan and to outside nations, it can do a very effective job of
unduly alarming other nations over Japanese (and SCAP) objectives and develop
greatly increased resistance to any moves for reasonable expansion of Japanese
operations.
On 23 August 1948, a meeting was held with pepresentatives of the
Japanese fisheries Agency, and the Japanese Foreign Office, and members of
the Natural Resources Fisheries Division. Discrepancies between the figares
in the report of the Foreign Office and the figures from the data on file with
Natural Resources Fisheries Division were discussed. Representatives of the
Foreign Office stated that they were not aware of the differences between the
available figures and stated that the document would be made subject to
revision based in part on a re-check of the figures submitted by the Japanese
Fisheries Agency. Representatives of the latter Agency assumed the respon-
sibility for the figures which were submitted to the Foreign Office and in-
cluded in the report. The Japanese Fisheries Agency will make a further
check and furnish Natural Resources Fisheries Division with an explemation
of the discrepancies. It was stated the document had been prepared about a
year ago at which time the Japanese Government did not have reliable figures
on some of the problems stated in the subject report.

Tokyo, November 2, 1948
No. 706
hil
CONFIDENTIAL
Subject: Movements of Japanese Fishing Vessels Outside
Authorized Area.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to invite the Department's attention
to the reported movements of Japanese fishing vessels outsido
the authorized fishing area in waters adjacent to the Kuril
Islands and the southern part of Sakhalin.
CTICN
FE
Between May 7, 1948 and October 7, 1948 Soviet coastal
OPI
patrol authorities have taken into custody twelve Japanese
fishing vessels, nine of which were reportedly released by
the Soviet authorities. There have been some discrepancies
in the reported positions of these vessels at the time of
u/Fn
seizure, but at least one eppears to have been within tho
authorized area and another was on the high seas approximately
TRC
seventy-five miles west of the southern tip of Sakhalin.
On October 19, 1948 the Soviet Member, Allied Council for
Japan, addressed a letter to the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers protesting against the elleged violations of Soviet
-611
territorial waters by Japanese vessels in the vicinity of the
Goyomai and Nemuro Straits (off the eastern coast of Hokkaido),
OLI
in waters adjacent to the island of Shikotan, and in waters
1/
off the southern part of Sakhalin. A copy of the letter is
enclosed with this despatch. The letter from the Soviet Member
noted thet Soviet coast guard authorities had seized a total
of twelve Japanese vessels, but that nine had been released
because their violation of Soviet territorial waters was
"unpremeditated". The soviet Member requested the Supreme
MAR 18 1949
Commander to direct the attention of the Japanese Government to
the necessity of taking measures to put an end to such violations
of Soviet territorial waters.
The letter from the Soviet Member is of significance in
that it makes reference to the waters of the Kuril Islands as
Soviet territorial waters. It is also of interest that there is
the
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 706,
November 2, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-2
the implied suggestion that the Japanese vessels seized were on
missions of an espionage or intelligence nature, and nowhere
in the letter are the craft referred to as fishing tessels.
The apparent Soviet view, as expressed in this letter, is that
Soviet patrol authorities have the right to deal with cases in which
Japanese vessels violate the limits of the authorized fishing area
which was established by directive of the Supreme Commander on
June 22, 1946 (SCAPIN 1033). The measures requested, however,
are measures to put an end to violation of Soviet territorial
waters by Japanese vessels.
Enclosure No. 2 of this despatch is a copy of my reply
(as chief of the Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters, Supremo
Commander for the Allied Powers), made on November 2, 1948, to tho
Soviet Member's letter. The Soviet Wember's attention is drawn
to the fact that it is the responsibility of the Supreme Commander
to enforce directives issued by the Supreme Commander. Japanose
fishing operetions have been restricted to an area established by
a directive of the Supreme Commander and any movement on the high
seas of Japanese fishing vessels outside the authorized area is
a matter with which only the Supreme Commander has authority to
deal. The Soviet Lember, therefore, is requested to instruct the
locel Soviet authorities in the areas concerned to refrain here-
after from interfering with or seizing Japanese vessels found on
the high seas outside the authorized area. In such instances, the
Soviet, Member is asked to provide the Supreme Commander with a
properly certified report showing the time and place of the incident,
the identity of the vessel, and the identity of the local Soviet
authority reporting the alleged violation. The reply did not specify
action desired when a Japanese vessel outside the authorized fishing
area is within waters under the administrative jurisdiction of the
Soviet Union.
Most of the incidents referred to in the Soviet Member's letter
of October 19, 1948 have taken place in waters off the Nosappu
peninsula of eastern Hokkaido. The Japanese Government has been
directed to warn the Hokkaido fishing concerns of the serious
nature of such violations of the authorized fishing area and to
prosecute those responsible for such acts. The Japanese Government
has also undertaken to patrol the waters of the Soya Straits and
the waters around the Nosappu peninsula, but only two small patrol
craft are available in Hokkaido for this purpose.
On October 30, 1948 a United States Navy destroyer under orders
of the Supreme Commander was sent to the Nosappu peninsula area
for the purpose of patrolling the waters where most of the reported
violations have occurred.
The fishing season in Hokkaido waters normally lasts from May
until about the middle of November.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald
CONFIDENTIAL

ALLID COUNCIL FOR JAPAN
TOKYO
OFFICE OF THE .EBER
FOR
T UI.ION OF OVIET SOCIALIST RPUBLICS
COLANDR IN CHLEF, ALLID POW RS, JAPAN
GENLRL OF THE RY, DOUGLAS MaCARTHUR
Dear General:
I have the honor to iniorm you of the following:
Lately there has been an increasing number of violations
of territorial waters of U.S.S.R. by Japanese vessels in the area
of Goyomai and Nemuro Straits, in the waters off the island of
Shikotan (in the lesser Kuril chain) and in the waters off the
southern part of Sakhalin Island.
For example, during the period from 5 August to 15 October the
territorial waters of U.S.S.R. were violated by 12 Japanese vessels.
which were detained by Soviet Coast Guard Authorities in order to
ascertain the circumstances of their entry into Soviet territorial
waters. Upon investigation of the circumstances and establishment
of the fact that the entry of Soviet territorial waters by nine (9)
of the Japanese vessels was unpremeditated, these vessels were
released and have departed for Japan. At the same time, a preliminary
investigation of the circumstances of the violation of Soviet
territorial waters by the other three (3) Japenese vessels established
the fect thet entry by these vessels of territorial waters of U.S.S.R.
was premeditated. On board some of these vessels were found rations,
as well as naval maps with plotted courses leading toward Soviet
territory. The investigation of circumstances of unlawful entry
and violation of Soviet territorial waters by these vessels; namely
the schooner "Daiga Kotuki Maru", detained in the Matsugahama Bay,
Shikotan Island, and schooners No 3330 and NK 2-3029,
detained in the territorial waters of Aniwa Bay (Sakhalin Island)
is still continuing.
Informing you of the above, I request that you direct the attention
of the Japanese Government to the necessity of taking measures to put
an end to violation of territorial waters of U.S.S.R. by Japanese
vessels.
Any information or measures taken by you in regard to this
matter, which you may find possible to give to me, will be greatly
appreciated.
Respectfully yours,
MEBER, ALLIED COUNCIL FOR
JAPAN FROM THE U.S.S.R.
General-Leytenant
/s/ K.· Derevyanko

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to D
dated November 2, 1948f
United States Politioal 
Japan, Tokyo, on the mubjoet,
of Japanese Fishing Vessels Outs
zed Area."
(COPY)
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREE COLLANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
Tokyo, November 2, 1948.
Lieutenant General K. N. Derevyanko,
Member for the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics,
Allied Council for Japan,
Tokyo.
Dear General Derevyanko:
I acknowledge the receipt of your letter of 19 October 1948
addressed to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers with
regard to the alleged presence of Japanese vessels in unauthorized
waters and stating that corrective measures would be approciated.
Although this Headquarters is not of the opinion that the
evidence submitted has established violation of waters under
Soviet administrative control in all instances cited in your
letter, pursuant to your request steps are being taken with the
object of restricting Japanese fishing and other vessels to
authorized areas.
With regard to measures to prevent Japanese vessels from
fishing outside the limits of the authorized fishing area, the
attention of the Soviet authorities is invited to the faot that
such measures are the responsibility of the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers as the sole executive authority for the Allied
Powers in Japan. It is the opinion of the Supreme Commander that
only those enforcement agencies under the authority and control
of the Supreme Commander are authorized to enforce directives
issued by the Supreme Commander. It is, therefore, requested
that Soviet patrol authorities in the areas concerned be instruoted
to refrain from interference with or seizure of Japanese vessels
on the high seas beyond the authorized fishing area. In such
instances it will be appreciated if the Soviet Member will supply
the Supreme Commander with full particulars regarding such violations
in order that the violators can be properly identified and prosecuted
in Japan in accordance with duly established procedures.
In such cases it is essential to have a properly certified
affidavit or formal document giving the name of the vessel, the
position at which it was reported operating beyond the authorized
fishing area, the date end time of the occurrence, and pertinent
data with regard to the observer of the alleged violation.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
November 10, 1948
To
: The Under Secretary
Through : s/s
894.628
Subject : Japanese Fishing
At the request of ir. Royall, Col. Lincoln (Mr. Draper's Deputy)
asks to present to you the Arny's argument in favor of allowing Japa-
nese to fish in the U. S. Trust Territories of the Pacific.
You decided against such action at the present time on the basis
of memorandum submitted to you on September 10, 1948 by Mr. Chapman
(see Tab A). r. Nitze recommended that Japanese be authorized to fish
2
in such areas, subject to agreement between the Army and the Navy to
insure security and administrative convenience (see Tab B). SANACC 99/6
(see Tab A), which expressed your decision, was not adopted by SANACC in
/11-1048
view of objections of the Army. An effort to reach a compromise is set
forth in SANACC 99/8, which proposes in effect:
a.
Authorization by the FEC to SCAP to allow Japanese fishing
in any areas except that SCAP should obtain approval of
any country which might have "direct concern" in a particu-
lar area before fishing in that area.
b.
.ithin three months SANACC should consider expansion of
Japanese fishing into the Trust area (where fish resources
Ihis Docu
are worth some $2,500,000 per year).
c.
Within 60 days, whether or not FEC action had been obtained,
the U. S. Government would advise SCAP that he is authorized
to extend Japanese fishing into a large area north of the
Trust area (where fish resources are worth some 7,000,000
per year).
Col. Lincoln will request an amendment to SANACC 99/8 (see Tab C),
which would propose FEC authorization to SCAP to allow fishing in any
area regardless of agreement by countries having a direct concern therein
and would provide authorization for the Japanese to fish in both the
trust area and area north of it within 15 days, irrespective of FEC action.
j He will undoubtedly refer to kMacArthur's cable (see Tab D).
The decision you formerly made was based on issues raised by the
attached memoranda of Messrs. Chapman and Nitze, the essential issue
C
C
FILED
contained
1
Cet.
1960
SECRET
353

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2
contained therein being that of giving priority to U. S. fishermen to
establish themselves in the Trust Territory before the Japanese are re-
admitted. I agree with Lir. Nitse that Japanese fishing in the Trust
ares should be prohibited only on the grounds of security, conservation
or administrative convenience and that protection of American industry,
at least in this case, does not appear to be sufficient gwounds for
such prohibition. I understand, moreover, that Mr. Chapman no longer
urges protection of American industry as a major consideration and it
therefore appears that all offices of the Department are in agreement
that the only significant considerations are security, administrative
convenience and, especially, conservation.
The Navy claims that the security or well-being of the island natives
would be adversely affected by Japanese fishing in the Trust area, while
the Department of the Interior is concerned that the Japanese wili not
conserve the fish resources of the area. Furthermore, there is a direct
State Department concern that Japanese fishing in this area would pro-
duce strong objections from the FEC countries so that we should at least
advise them informally before SCAP takes any steps in this direction.
Accordingly, I do not believe we can agree without qualification to the
Army proposals.
It seems clear that the essential objectives are that the Japanese
comply with appropriate standards of conservation, administrative con-
venience and security-so that their fishing activities will be so
conducted as to be acceptable to the fishing nations of the world. It
therefore appears to me that the correct solution to satisfy all
interested offices of this Department and the Departments of the Govern-
ment is to provide SCAP with standards desired by the Government, with
the understanding that SCAP will put such standards into effoct, com-
pelling the Japanese to carry them out and seeing to it that they are
rigorously enforced. SCAP should be advised that when, but only when,
he is satisfied that the standards are actually in effect and that from
the practical point of view he will be able to see that they are en-
forced, he may authorize the Japanese to fish in the high seas of the
Trust area. I therefore believe that we might agree to some compromise
with the Army proposals along the lines presented below (referring to
the three points in SANACC 99/8 set out above):
a. No change except that possibly SCAP should only be required
to obtain agreement to Japanese fishing from those countries
"adjacent to" as well as "directly concerned in" the areas
involved. This change would be designed to avoid the neces-
sity of obtaining agreement of the Australians to fishing
north of the equator even though they claim an interest there.
b. Agree that as a matter of principle the Japanese should have
free access to fishing areas subject to interest of security,
conservation and administrative convenience. The Departments
of
SECRET

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.98)
SÈCRET
-3-
of State, Interior, Navy and Army will immediately provide
SCAP with internationally accepted standards of conserva-
tion in fishing and with standards satisfying administrative
considerations to govern Japanese fishing in the open ses
areas. SCAP should be advised that prior to the extension
of Japanese fishing into the high seas of the Trust Terri-
tory he must be satisfied that such standards have been put
into effect as applicable to all Japanese fishing activities
and that as a practical matter he can rigorously enforce
such standards on the Japanese in all open sea areas to
which the Japanese have been or may be admitted. Upon being
thus satisfied he may permit the Japanese to extend their
fishing activities into the high seas of the Trust Territory,
subject to special provisions laid dowm for the protection of
the native population.
c. Reduce the 60 day interval to 30 days.
Recommendation:
I recommend that you (a) approve the proposed compromise set out
just above and authorize me to negotiate with the Department of the Army
on this basis, or (b) if you do not feel that you have been provided with
sufficient information to approve the proposals as set out above that you
hold a conference on the subject with the interested officers of the
Department.
Charles? Sathmar
Charles E. Saltzman

Y
September 10, 1948
U - Mr. Lovett
E - Mr. Nitse
Japanese fishing privileges in the Pacific Trust Territory.
Pending a final determination of Japanese fishing privileges in the
peace settlement, SCAP has restricted Japanese fishing activities in
π
accordance with administrative and security requirements of the ocoupation.
m
SCAP has recently proposed an extension of these privileges into several
new areas, including the waters surrounding the Pacific Trust Territory
in the Central Pacific. It has been urged that the U.S. Government oppose
that part of the extension which relates to the waters around the islands
of the Trust Territory. The arguments in favor of this position are given
in a memorandum to you from Mr. Chapman. I recommend that the Department
of State not support this position for the following reasonss
1. The U.S. Government has consistently followed the principle that
Japanese economic activity should be restricted as a result of defeat and
occupation only in so far as was necessary on strict security grounds or,
during the period of occupation, for reasons of administrative convenienoe.
This policy has been considered essential to the restoration, as promptly
as possible, of conditions of economic stability and prosperity without
which permanent peace is impossible. It is also consistent with our desire
to end, at the earliest possible date, U.S. financial assistance to the
defeated countries. To violate this principle would be inconsistent with
strong positions taken in the past with other governments on such matters
as Japanese whaling in the Antarctic, would embarrass us in the peace settle-
ment negotiations, particularly with China and one or two other countries
which are expected to seek strict limits on the capacity of the Japanese
textile industry, and might even present difficulties in the impending
negotiations to sharply reduce reparations deliveries.
Whether or not exceptions to this policy may be justified in this case
on security grounds is a matter on which the Department of State should
look for guidance to the Department of National Defense, acting in consul-
tation with SCAP.
Insofar as administrative considerations are crucial, they are of
direct concern to SCAP and the Navy (as the administrative power in the
Trust Territory) and should be settled by agreement between them.
2. In the absence of conclusive administrative or security considera-
tions, a U. S. position which would exclude the Japanese from fishing in
the Trust Territory could be supported only on the ground of the need for
aiding the
SEURET

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
- 2 -
aiding the U. S. tuna industry. This would be a violation by the U.s. ef
MW treatment toward Japan with respect to commercial activities, treatmemt
which the U. S. is now actively and agressively seeking to secure for Japem,
from the nations on whose trade Japan's economic future is dependent. To
take such a step would materially weaken our position in these negotiatioms
and might indirectly affect substantially the economic situation of Japem
over the next few years and, in turn, the drain imposed by Japan on the
U.S. Treasury. Moreover, it is just this type of discriminatory actiem
which the U.S. is continually protesting when practiced by other countries
and which the whole U.S. trade policy is designed to outlaw.
3. In the absence of conclusive administrative and security considere-
tions, to take a step which was contrary to the general principles of U.s.
policy stated in 1 and 2 above, could only be justified, if at all, by the
prospect of a very great advantage for the U.S. It appears from available
evidenoe that to deprive the Japanese of fishing rights in the Trust Terri-
tory would deprive Japan of about two million dollars worth of food whieh
might well have to be made up out of the U.S. Treasury. In the foreseeable
future the profits of the U.S. tuna industry from fishing operations in the
Trust Territory are apt to be negligible. Their total take is not estimated
at over two million dollars and it will be a year or two before they oan
reach that level.
The only other possible factor affecting this point is the indireet
effect of the granting to the U.S. tuna industry of exclusive fishing
rights in the mandates on the discriminations now being practioed by certain
South American countries, particularly Mexico, against the U.S. tume fishing
industry. It is not believed that this somewhat uncertain and indireet gain
justified the probably adverse effects on the U.S. position generally and
with respect to Japan, which would result from an abandomment of the
principles of 1 and 2.
It is, therefore, recommended that the Department of State support a
U.S. Government position which would seek to secure FEC approval for the
extension of Japanese fishing in the areas outlined by SCAP, with the
understanding in this Government that the authorization would not be exer-
cised by SCAP with respect to the Trust Territory until there hed been an
agreement between the Army and Navy as to its desirability, based on two
considerations only -- security and administrative convenience.

Colonel Lincoln departed for the weekend before I was able to
discuss with him the status of the Japanese fishing matter. The Army
has therefore not been informed as to the results of the meeting Mr.
Lovett held Friday afternoon with essrs Nitze, Chapman and Wilds.
Mr. Lovett's decision embraced these points:
1. The Department must continue to support the compromise set
forth in SANACC 99/8.
2.
With respect to Paragraph C, he had no objection to shortening
the time period if practicable.
3.
That with respect to point B, we should make clear to the Army
and SCAP that an agreement to reconsider the trust area wi thin
three months, we were in no wise prejudging the decision; in
other words, that there was not a moral commitment to let the
Japs in after three months.
4.
In developing a policy on this subject we should make clear
that the Navy, with respect to administrative and security
responsibilities, and Interior, with respect to conservation
responsibilities, are over-riding in the application of our broad
responsivilities to the area.
He rejected the "adjacent to" language on the score that it
would adversely affect us in certain areas, for example the
Galapagos with which we are directly concerned in behalf of Panama
defenses but are not adjacent to.
In general r. Lovett added further that we should be prepared to
resist pressure from Army and SCAP which might result in going beyond the
compromise set forth in 99/8.
TT
He agreed that there should be promptly formulated and communicated to
894.6
SCAP the criteria and standards to which the Japanese should conform in their
fishing operations in behalf of conservation objectives.

Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch M
dated November 19, 1948 from th
of the United States Political
Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expediti
Source: Public Information Offiee,
General Headquarters, Far
East Command.
Press Release:
2 November 1948
TWO-FLEET WHALING EXPEDITION LEAVES NOV. 12. 13
For the third consecutive year since the beginning of the
Occupation, a Japanese whaling expedition, consisting of two fleets
belonging to the Nippon Suisan and Taiyo Gyogyo companies, will
leave Japan for a six-nonth whale hunt in the southern waters of
the Antarctic Ocean.
William M. Terry, Fisheries Division official of SCAP's Natur-
al Resources Section, estimates that the total catch of the 1948-49
expedition will amount to approximately 1,250 blue whale units.
Maximum production efforts and utilization of these blue whale
units will result in 21,250 metric tons of whale oil, 16,860 metric
tons of frozen meat, 11,634 metric tons of salted meat, and approxi-
mately 6,198 metric tons of bone meal, baleen plates and other
assorted by-products.
Maj. James A. Crombie, 336 Riverside Avenue, Buffalo, N.Y.,
and Lt. Francois Bourgois of the French Navy, both assigned to NRS,
XR
Fisheries Divi sion, will accompany the Japanese whaling fleets as
SCAf representatives, serving in supervisory capacities to insure
that all whaling operations are in conformity with the most recent
International Conventions for Regulation of Whaling. Kenneth Coonan
of Sydney, Australia, and Capt. A.V. Hemming of London, Royal Navy,
retired, have been desi gnated to accompany the Antarctic expedition
as Allied observers for the governments of Australia and the United
Kingdom. Both Captain Henming and Lieutenant Bourgois accompanied
last year's expedition, and Coonan has served as Allied observer for
Australia on the two previous trips.
The Nisshin Maru No. 1, 11,781 ton factory ship belonging to
the Taiyo Gyogyo Company, is scheduled to depart from Yokosuka
Harbor at 12 noon Nov. 12. Shigeo Takeda is shipping as fleet
general manager and Major Crombie and Coonan will be aboard. The
Nisshin Maru will be in a fleet comprising 16 other vessels.
The Hashidate Maru, 10,841 ton factory ship belonging to the
Nippon Suisan Company, will depart from Yokohama Harbor at 12 noon
Nov. 13. Hiroshi Kurebayashi will ship as fleet general manager
and Lieutenant Bourgois and Captain Hemming will be aboard. This
3TAT2
fleet will include 13 other vessels.

1. C.L.C.0. Letter No. 3724(2P), subject, "Amendment to P1an
for Japanese Whaling Operations in the Antarctic in the 1948-1949

a plan for Japanese whaling operations in the Antarctic during the
1948-49 Season, which plan was submitted with C.L.C.0. Letter No.
2550(2P), dated 12 July 1948, and approved by SCAPIN 5822-A/1,
dated 29 July 1948. Amendments 1, 2, and 3, as stated in C.L.C.0.
Letter No. 3724(2P), would make the following changes:

Maru No. 1l in the first fleet.
b. Revise the sailing schedule of both fleets.
c. Increase the allocation of fuel for the first fleet.

with no objection from NR. The replacement of Kyo Maru No. 3 by
Shonan Maru No. 1l will improve the efficiency of the first fleet.
The revision of sailing schedules is made necessary by the coming
into effect of the International Convention for the Regulation of
Whaling, signed at lashington, D.C., 2 December 1946, with which
Japanese whalers must conform. However, NR feels that additional
fuel requested in paragraph l. c. above is not justified.

C.L.C.0. Letter No. 3724(2r), but specifically refuses approval of
amendment 3 stated in C.L.C.0. Letter No. 3724(2P).

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.99)
PROPOSED DIRECTIVE TO SCAP-FISHING ACTIVITIES
It is recognized that obtaining the largest pos-
sible portion of Japanese food requirements through
Japanese effort is an important occupation objective, and
that fishery products are a significant diet item of
Japan. Likewise it is recognized that export of fishery
products is important to the program for reducing the
financial burdens upon the U. S. Government, However, in
view of the failure of Japan to abide by normal inter-
national fishery practices prior to the war, and the
resultant international friction and animosity, occupa-
tion policy regarding Japanese fishing should give special
emphasis to education of the Japanese to a full appreciation
and acceptance of those responsibilities concomitant to
the conduct of extensive fishing operations, and to measures
which will facilitate negotiation of a peace settlement
which will not impose special prohibitions or restrictions
on Japanese fishing.
In order to break down existing prejudices and
scepticism existing both in the United States and abroad,
it would appear necessary that a program designed for
this purpose should involve more than minimum Japanese
compliance with normal and customary conduct and programs
of other major fishing nations. The program may necessarily
V
involve adoption by the Japanese of specific regulations,
research effort and codes of conduct in advance of any
now in effect in any other nation. It will be necessary
that full publicity be given to all phases of such program,
in order to assure that the American people, as well as
the governments and people of the other Allies having
concern with fisheries, will recognize that the Japanese
are not likely to revert, after the occupation, to their
prewar attitude and practices with respect to fisheries
matters. Citation by foreign nations, as well as certain
American groups, of the pre-war Japanese attitudes and
practices, with the assumption that Japan will immediately
revert to such practices appears to be the basic argument
for the inclusion in the peace settlement of provisions
for restricting Japanese fishing operations to their home
waters. It is believed that this issue will present
serious difficulties at the time of the settlement. Con-
sequently, in principle it would appear advisable that
every effort be made to provide convincing demonstration
of this program within the fishing areas authorized.
SCAP

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
SCAP may be assured of the full cooperation of the
agencies of the United States Government in bringing
to the attention of foreign governments as well as the
American fishing industry, members of Congress, and the
American public the accomplishments of SCAp and the
Japanese in this respect.
Special consideration should be given to assuring:
1.
Strictest possible conformity to the spirit and
letter of the provisions of international agree-
ments relating to conservation of aquatic resources.
It may be desirable, particularly with respect to
whaling, to give special publicity to all measures,
programs, and actions, which are in excess of the
treaty requirements and which will ocoasion favorable
comment in whaling and Government circles.
2.
Recognition of and conformity with any conservation
laws and regulations which foreign nations apply to
their own vessels fishing in any particular area in
which the Japanese are also operating.
3.
Conformity with rules of the road, courtesies and
customs prevailing generally among fishing vessels
or in particular areas or fisheries.
Maintaining both in home and other waters full and
continuing scientific programs for each fishery to
ascertain the effect of Japanese fishing on the re-
source and immediate imposition of such regulation
of fishing as may be shown necessary to ensure a
maximum sustained yield from the resource. Where the
Japanese and foreign fishermen are harvesting the
same resource consideration may be given to joint
cooperating action by the Japanese and such nation.:
or nations.
5.
Avoidance, in so far as possible,of action which may
tend to create or strengthen unfavorable foreign
reaction to the continuance and expansion of Japanese
fishing operations and trade in fishery products.
U/FW/WESF1ory/BMD/RB

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS

TO
: FE - Mr. Allison
DATE: December 7, 1948
DIRECTOR
Departnent of state
FROM : FE - Miss Bacon
SUBJECT: UFW- Mr. Flory's Proposed Directive to SCAP Conoerning
Fishing (attacled)
Mr. Flory is out today and no one in his office knows
who requested U/FW to prepare the draft directive. His
office is still trying to find out.
General Comments
1. The paper is in the form of a proposed directive
to SCAP. Such a directive would normally require refer-
ence to the FEC. The phrasing of the proposed directive
would seem to require some revision if reference to the FEC
is contemplated. Moreover, there would seem to be question
whether the matter might not better be handled through some
less formal means, such as a telegram or informal instruction.
2. If a telegram or informal instruction is to be
6
sent on fisheries practices, it would probably be desirable
to defer sending the message until agreement can be reached
on 99/8. It might well seem unrealistic to SCAP to be
asked to enjoin the Japanese people to use only the most
approved and scientific method of taking fish while their
fishing activities are restricted to so small an area
2
that unscientific and unapproved practices are encouraged
by circumstance.
SPECIFIC COMMENTS
1. The reference to the possibility of avoiding
stringent fishery provisions in the peace settlement might
2
be deleted from the end of the first paragraph. The sub-
ject is fully covered in the second paragraph, and undue
emphasis on this subject might be unfortunate.
748
2. The repeated references to the United States and
American people in paragraph 2 might be deleted in favor
of references to the United Nations, etc.
3. In connection with numbered paragraph 1 on page 2,
a list of the international agreements in question might be
helpful to SCAP if such a list has not already been provided.
In the next to the last line of the same numbered paragraph,
it is suggested that the word "will" be changed to""might".

aloug above lines by pliove t o/Fw- RB
