PORM NO. 64
TO
NA - Mr. Allison
DATE: Apr11 21, 1948
FROM :
FE - Mr. Hamilton
SUBJECT:
SANACC 99/4 "U.S. Policy for Japanese Fishing and
Other Aquatic Industries".
This is an extremely important subject, both from
the Japanese point of view and from the United States
domestic and international point of view. U.S. Pacific
Coast fishing interests are greatly interested and have
made many approaches to the Department on the subject.
I recommend that NA study this subject with special
care and in so doing take into account the following
questions:
1. Is Mr. Flory and the Fisheries Division of the
Department in full accord? Have they any suggestions?
2. Is this a subject which should be referred to
the FEC? The original SCAP determination of the Japanese
fishing area was not based on anFEC decision. Fishing
on the high seas is not foreign trade or foreign commerce.
The arrangements for the Japanese participation in
haling were made outside the FEC. If the present
extension of the Japanese area is simply to include the
former Japanese mandated area, would that fact constitute
a reason why the matter need not be referred to the FEC?
Was the Japanese mandated area included in SCAP juris-
diction? I believe it was not. Sgertconst, 4E.
3. The first conclusion is extremely broad. It
would certainly be opposed by the Pacific Coast fishing
interests. The statement in APPENDIX "C", paragraph 3
is better, for there is a stipulation that Japanese
fishing operations should conform to "recognized conserva-
tion practices including conservation measures instituted
by littoral states".
4. I am somewhat skeptical whether a statement
along the lines of APPENDIX "C" could be successfully
negotiated in the FEC.
3..H.
FE:MMHamilton:fhh

=== Page 380 === (confidence 0.99)
FE - Mr. Hamilton
DATE: Apr11 23, 1948
TO
SUBJECT:
Your Underlying Memo on SANACC 99/4, "US Policy for
Japanese Fishing and Other Aquatio Industries"
This 1e, as you say, an important subject, and NA has
therefore kept in close touch with OE and IR in the drafting of
the paper. I reviewed it again carefully before giving final
NA clearance some two weeks ago.
In answer to the questions in your first nunbered paragraph,
Mr. Flory and the International Resources Division took an active
part in the preparation of this paper, as they have in the nego-
tiation of fishery issues in the FEC, and gave the paper as pre-
eented here their approval. While aware of the West Coast fishing
interests' point of view on the subject of Japanese pelagic fishing,
they, and NA and OE, have nevertheless considered it essentiel
that Japan be permitted to restore its fisning industry in accordance
with the Potsdem Declaration and the Department's general policy
that Japen should be accorded equal opportunity with other nations
to engege in peaceful enterprise. While West Coast fishing interests
have made clear their desire to restrict future Japanese fishing
to the waters west of Haweii it hes never been the intention in
the Department as far ae I know to accept this position. The
extended fishing area provided in the present peper is confined
to the Western Pecific and to waters in which U.S. fishing
vessels do not now operate.
Regarding the questions in your second numbered paragraph,
there are several reesons why it has been considered advisable
to refer the attached paper to the FEC. The question of Japanese
fishing wes first raised there when the Australian member intro-
duced a paper about a yeer and a half ago providing that the
Japanese fishing area should not be extended beyond the area
described in an early U.S. directive to SCAP, subsequently put
into effect by SCAP, except with the prior approval of the FEC.
The Australian proposal took the form or an amendment to the
originel U.S. directive, whicn had been filed with the FEC as
FEC-O35. The U.S. representatives on the FEC have consistently
opposed the Australian paper, and a similar paper more recently
introduced by the Soviets (Sć-271/2, attached), because of
reluctance to give the FEC this important power. While BCAP
can at present, therefore, extend the permitted fishing area
without reference to the FEC, and submission of the attached
paper to FEC is unnecessary in that sense, it has been felt by
him and by the interested divisions of the Department that it
would be inadvisable to do this because of the opposition it would
stir

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
stir up in the FEC and the increased difficulty which would
later be encountered in obtaining the permission of countries
like China, the Philippines and Australia for the Japanese to
fish near their shores.
In answer to your other specific questions in this paragraph,
the extended fishing area provided in the attached paper includes
considerably more than the former Japanese mandated area, which
has never been included within SCAP'e jurisdiction. Some of the
most important fishing grounds which would be provided the
Japanese in the enlarged area lie between the Trust Territory
and New Guinea, where there are considerable quantities of tuna.
Regarding the point in your third numbered paragraph, the
firet conclusion is broad in scope, but, for the reasons stated
in the second paragraph above, would seem wise and necessary,
both in the light of Japan's future economic needs and overall
Departmental policy as expressed in the Charter of the ITo and
other documents.
In answer to your final question, there is a fishing policy
paper now before the Economic Committee of the FEC (C2-271/4,
attached) which is similar almost to the point of being identical
with Appendix "C" of the attached document. Whereas the U.S.
was for a long time a minority of one in its efforts to provide
the Japanese with ample fishing grounds, two or three other members
now active?y support the above-mentioned paper and the others
have suggested only minor modifications or entertained mild
objections to the paper. The U. S. representatives on the FEC
are therefore quite hopeful that they will be able to obtain
FEC approval of the proposed policy statement. If this should
prove impossible SCAP would probably be advised to go ahead on
his own.
nA
NA:RAFearey/pm

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.99)
: MA - Mr. Allison
DA: May 3, 1948
TO
Handling of Attached Fishing Area Paper
SUBJECT:
Mr. Hamilton spoke to me Friday about this paper and our
memo of April 23. He raised again the question of whether it
was necessary to have an FEC decision on this problem and I
said that legally speaking, according to Mr. Snow, it was
not because SCAP's powers are plenary except where the FEC has
approved a policy decision, as it has not on any fishing
matter. On the other hand, from a political and tactical
point of view it would seem we would only be doing our cause
injury by withdrawing the paper from the FEC, considering that
we introduced 1t, a rgely on SCAP's suggestion, that Australia
and other countries have a strong and theoretically valid in-
terest in the matter from a security point of view, and that,
according to Whitman and Bowman, there is good reason to be-
lieve that the FEC will approve the paper.
Mr. Hamilton agreed that it might be unwise to withdraw
the paper, but said that he greatly doubted, notwithstanding
Whitman's and Bowman's view, that the FEC would approve it.
He therefore suggested that you and/or Dr. Blakeslee inde-
pendently soundout the more questionable countries' represen-
tatives on the FEC (I should think Hugh might be of help here
too) to find out what the chances of approval are. If they
are not good, we would either advise SCAP to go ahead on
his own or put the paper into the FEC with the intention of
issuing an interim directive after a certain, limited time.
I personally believe that the latter procedure would be
better but that Mr. Hamilton's procedure will cost us nothing
while the paper is still before SANACC, and may be of real
help.

SUBJECT:
Approval of the Baguio Agreement on the Establishment
ofr the Indo-Paciric Fisheries Counoil
The FAo has formally requested this Government's
approval of the Agreement for the Establishment of the
Indo-Pacific Fisheries Counoil which was signed at
Baguio on February 26, 1948. The text of the Agreement
is attached.
It would be appreciated if you would indicate whe-
ther your Division had any objection to the approval of
the Agreement in question.
The Agreement would set up a Counoil concorned
chiefly with the assembling and publishing of information
about aquatic resources, recommending to member govern-
ments research projects, proposing, and where necessary,
adopting, measures to bring about standardization of
scientific equipment, techniques, etc.
It is believed that in general FE would favor inter-
national cooperative efforts which might result in an
increase of food supplies from fish in the Pacifie.
The following points about the Agreement, however,
may be particularly mentioned:
1. The scope of the area within the jurisdiction of
the Counoil 1s vaguely referred to as "the Indo-Pacifio
areas". This phrase might inolude all of the Pacifio
Ocean and all of the Indian Ocean.
2. One of the Council's functions is "to undertake,
where appropriate, cooperative researoh and development
projects", directed to filling gaps relating to technical
information concerning aquatio resouroes.
As deoisions of the Council are taken by majority
vote and as the Agreement 1s open to acceptance by all
members of the FAo, it might technically be possible for
the Counoil to vote to undertake a cooperative researoh
or development projeot relating to the Trust Territory,
for example, The likelihood that such a projeot would be
undertaken

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
undertaken without U.S. cooperation or against U.s. wishes
would seem to be remote and, in any case, this Govermment
would probably have means or ensuring that no security
risks so far as the Trust Territory is concerned would
result. This feature of the Agreement, however, is brought
particularly to NA's attention.

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.99)
FAO FISHERIES BULLETIN NO. 2
APRIL, 1948
PREFACE
The first trial run of the Fisheries Bulletin was completed in the
Fisheries Division of FAo at the beginning of March. It was sent to member
Governments and FAo National Committees in English, French, and Spanish
oditions. This second trial run has been prepared before comments on the
first reached these headquarters, and it is suggested that the two 1esues
together may be a better indication of the Division's work in the field of
current fisheries statistics than could be produced in one issue. We look
forward to comments on both issues.
In this issue we publish in full the recommended agreement reached
at Baguio on 26 February. The proposed formation of an Indo-Pacific Fisheries
Council 1s an important development in the work of FAO's Fisheries Division,
which will furnish the Council's permanent secretariat.
The Council will operate in an area where probably there is greater
need of increased food production than in any other part of the world, and
where there is particular need of the animal protein which fisheries can help
to supply. At the same time, the scientific work projected for the four
permanent committees will certainly produce new knowledge, some of which will
be applicable to fisheries far removed from the Indo-Pacific area. Moreover,
experience gained in the establishment and operation of this Council will be
applicable to the projected Councils for other parts of the world not actively
served by such bodies.
The sea is international. Fish are no respecters of frontiers. Nore-
over, the behavior and content of the ocean in one area depends on circumstances
created in another; fishing techniques of one area may be applicable to similar
conditions elsewhere. For example, a method of fishing developed in Indo-Pacific
coral reefs might be applied to fisheries in some other coral-bearing sea. At
present an important aspect of FAo's work is that of coordinating scattered
knowledge and making it generally available.
Dr. D.B. Finn, the Director of the Fisheries Division, following the
conclusion of the meetings in Baguio, proceeded to Shanghai, Hong Kong, Bangkok,
and Singapore. He is scheduled to leave Singapore on April 4 for London and to
arrive back in Washington about April 15.
Dr. J.L. Kask, Head of the Biological Branch, who just recently joined
the Organization, returned from Baguio on March 15. Mr. Mogens Jul, formerly
Head of the Technological Laboratory of the Danish Department of Fisheries and
Co-director of Food Preservation Laboratory of the Royal Institute of Technology
of Demmark and newly appointed Head of our Technological Branch, will take up
his duties about April 15.

"RESOLVED that the representatives of governments members of the Food and
Agriculture Organization of the United Nations here assembled, recognizing
the mutual interest of their several nations in the development and proper
utilization of the living aquatic resources of the Indo-Pacific areas, and
believing that this end can best be attained by international collaboration,.
do hereby recommend to their respective governments for consideration, the
acceptance of the following agreement for the establishment of an Indo-Pacific
Fisheries Council."
Preamble
The Governments of Burma, China, France, India, the
Netherlands, the Republic of the Philippines, the
United Kingdom, and the United States of America, mombers of the Food and
Agriculture Organization of the United Nations, having a mutual interest in the
development and proper utilization of the living aquatic resources of the Indo-
Pacific Areas, and desiring to further the attainment of these ends through
international co-operation by the establishmont of an Indo-Pacific Fisheries
Council agree as follows:
Article I
1. The contracting Governments agree to esteblish
THE COUNCIL
a Council, to be kmown as the Indo-Pacific Fisheries
Council, for the purpose of carrying out the functions
and duties hereinafter vet forth in Article III.
2. The mombers of the Council shall be the Govern-
mente which accept this Agreement in accordance with the provisions of Article
IX thereof.
Article II
1. Each momber Govertment shall be represented at
ORGANIZATION
meetings of the Council by a single delegate, who
may be accompanied by an alternate and by experts and
advisers. Participation in meetings of the Council by alternates, experte and
advisers shall not entail the right to vote, except in the case of an alternate
who is acting in the place of a delegate during his absence.
2. Each member Government shall have one vote.
Decisions of the Council shall be taken by a simple majority of the votes
cast, except as otherwise provided by this Agreement. A majority of the
total membership of the Council shall constitute a quorum.
3. The Council shall elect a Chairman and a Vice-
Chairman.
4. The Council shall determine the frequency, date's
and place of its meetings, and establish rules governing its procedure.
5. The chairman shall call a moeting of the Council
at least once in every year, unless dirocted otherwise by a majority of the
momber Governments. The initial meoting shall be called by the Food and Agri-
culture Organization of the United Nations within six months after the entry
into force of this Agreement and at such place as it may designate.

Article III
The Council shall have the following functions and
FURCTIONS
duties:

and other techmical aspects of the problems of development and proper utiliza-

oceanographical, bilogical and other technical information relating to living.
aquatic resources;

and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations upon its activities, for the

Agriculture Organization of the United Nations on matters falling within the
competence of the Council as may seem to it necessary and desirable.

AREA

AMENDMENTS
the approval of a two-thirds majority of all the Members

ACCEPTANCE

be deposited with the Director-General of the Food and Agriculture Organization of

their receipt.

COMMENTS ON TRADE STATISTICS
In Table 1 a summary is given of the trade statistics of 61 countries
dealt with in the FAo Yearbook of Fisheries Statistics, which is now in press.
It is hoped that the second volume of the Yearbook will cover nearly lo0 percent
of the world trade in fisheries products; but even in its present form the table
is fairly comprebensive - only a few of the countries to be added have considerable
fisheries industries.
The summary was prepared for the year 1938, and where figures for 1938
were not available the nearest year was used. A rough totaling of the prewar
summary table indicates a total export of 406,354 motric tons of fresh and frozen
products, 708,206 metric tons of salted, dried, and smoked products; 271,671
metric tons of canned products; 113,245 metric tons of crustaceans and molluscs;
215,054 metric tons marine oils; and 132,987 metric tons marine meal and
fertilizers.
For only a few countries has it been possible to bring the exports and
imports tables up to date so that postwar trade could be compared with immediate
prewar trade. Therefore no summary for 1946 was included in the first Yearbook.
Since the manuscript for that Yearbook was completed at the end of 1947, it has
proved possible to bring the series more up to date for some countries for which
trade in fisheries products, and particularly exports, are of significant impor-
tance. Summarized postwar trade statistics as compared with 1938 are included in
this issue of the Bulletin for the following countries:
Canada
Export statistics
France
Exports and imports statistics

Import statistics
Iceland
Export statistics

Export statistics

Postwar statistics for these countries, compared with statistics for 1938,
are given in Tables 2 to 10.
For some of these countries the two postwar years, 1946 and 1947, are
completely covered. For others, only some months of 1947 have been covered, and
for two countries figures for January, 1948, as compared with January, 1947, are
available.
It is intended to contimue these surveys and to expand the list of
countries included, but the present sample covers a significant proportion of
the total world trade. The i938 exports of the countries which are represented
in these postwar surveys accounted for 56 percent of the total trade in fresh
and frozen products, 47 percent of salted products, otc., 40 percent of canned
products, 66 percent of crustaceans and molluscs, 64 percent of oils, and 94 per-
cent of meal and fertilizers.

In 1946 fishermen from 20 reporting countries (see Table 12) landed
altogether three million metric tons of herring and herring-like fish, which is
more than 15 percent of the estimated total prowar world landings of all types
of fish, herring included. About 1,l00,000 metric tons, wet basis edible weight,
or approximately one-third of the quantity landed in 1946, were used for humen
consumption. About 1,l00,000 metric tons round fresh weight were used for con-
version into oil and meal, and possibly l00,000 metric tons for bait. The
remaining quantity, about 700,000 metric tons, were "waste", which might inolude
some small quantities used for fertilizer and for animal fodder other than meal.
The marketed weight in 1946 of the various herring products in 16 of
the 20 countries was as follows:
Fresh and frozen. . . . 297,000 metric tons
Salted
382,000
11
Dried only b/

n
"
Canned.
195,000
Meal.
217,000
011
104,000
Figures showing the quantity of raw material used for these products
are not yet available.
For 14 countries it has been possible to make a retrospective survey
of the herring landinge. This is shown in Table 12, where statistics are given
for the years 1935-1947. The 1946 landings of herring and herring-like f1sh
were slightly below the immediate prewar level. Landings in the United States
and Norway, which accounted for 43 percent of the 1946 total, were 21 percent
and 16 percent respectively below average of the last five prewar years. United
Kingdom and French landings show decreases as compared with prewar years, but in
Belgium, Denmark, Netherlands, and, to a lesser extent, Iceland c/ landings ex-
ceeded the prewar average. Export statistics for Newfoundland, which are here
converted to round fresh weight, indicate a considerable increase in the quantity
landed.
Only eight a/ of the countries which reported l946 landings have so far
reported figures for l947. Their total landings of herring and herring-like fish
in 1946 amounted to 1,494,380 metric tons. In 1947, 1,663,471 metric tons were
landed in the same countries, that is, an increase of il percent over 1946 figures.
If we take the herring landings of the countries which have not yet reported
figures for 1947 as equal to those of 1946, the total postwar landings in 1947
are on a level with those of 1938.
a Including herring, menhaden, sardine, anchovy, pilchard, sprat (brisling),
sild, and similar herring-like species.
b/ Japan only.
 Due to increased catching and processing capacity, Iceland anticipates
an increased catch.

Norway.

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.99)
- 18 -
ICELAND, 1947 1
(Supplementary to note on page 8, Fisheries Bulletin No. 1)
Two species dominate the Icelandic Fisheries; Cod and Herring. Up to the
year 1947, the fishing seasons were determined by the catch of these species and there
were two main seasons: the winter season for the codfisheries and the sumer season
for the herring fisheries. The cod fishing season started at the beginning of January
and lasted till the middle of May, whereas the herring season started at the beginning
of July and lasted till the end of August and sometimes till the middle of September.
From October till December the fishing fleet was usually little engaged in fishing
except the big steam trawlers.
In the year l947 a change took place in the fishing seasons. In December
1946, herring was detected by echosounder in the inlets of Faxabay, at the southwest
corner of Iceland, and fishing started on a fairly big scale in January 1947. The
herring seemed to be there in abundance, but owing to lack of suitable fishing gear
the fishing could be carried out by only comparatively few boats. The codfishing
season was therefore not affected by this fishing, which lasted till the beginning of
March. The total herring catch amounted to 12,000 metric tons. 2/
The fishing during the cod season was fairly good, especially in the first
half of the season. The total catch till the end of May was 145,800 tons of cod and
codlike species, compared with 142,000 tone in the same period of 1945 (all fish
gutted with head on).
The herring season in the summer of 1945 and 1946 had been a failure, whore-
as the season in 1944 had been the best one up to then. The season 1947 was very much
like the two previous seasons. About 260 boats participated in the fishing, the
biggest number of boats ever participating in the herring fisheries. The total catch
was only 122,500 tons.
In the middle of October herring showed up at the west coast, which is quite
unusual at that time. Some boats started fishing there and carried on until the middle
of November, but the catches were not big, owing to unfavorable weather. Then o 1
November, the winter herring fishing started in the Faxabay, more than two months
earlier than last year. Since then this fishing has been going on and the total catch
up to the end of December amounts to 82, 500 tons. This new herring season has been of
great importance to the fishing fleet after the disappointing summer season. Contrary
to the summer fishing, when the fish is caught while visible in the surface, the winter
herring is caught in a great depth, up to 20-25 fathome, and detected by echosounders.
Both the summer and winter herring is caught with purse seine nets.
The total catch in the year 1947 amounted to 431,170 tons, of which 217,000
tons were herring and 214,170 tons cod and codlike species.
The utilization of the cod catch was as follows: About 34 percent was ex-
ported fresh, about 31 percent was salted, about 33 percent was filleted and frozen.
The herring catch was utilized as follows: About 92.5 percent went to the
reduction plants for production of meal and oil, about 3.8 percent was salt-cured,
about 3.1 percent was frozen for bait and the rest was exported fresh.
This survey was submitted by Fiskifelag Islands (Fisheries Association of Iceland).
2
Metric tons throughout the report. These figures have not been converted to round
fresh weight.

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.99)
19-
Owing to high production costs, the fishing industry has im the
last three years been in a rather difficult situation. At the beginnin
of l947, the situation was so difficult that the Government had to guarenteo
minimm prices for salted and frozen fish, except herring. Neither did the
guarantee cover the fish caught by the steam trawlers, which in the year 19k?
was about 36 percent of the total catch of cod and codlike species. The
guarantee, which resulted in subsidy payments, made it possible for the fish-
ing industry to keep up production in the year 1947. These measures were
continued for the year l948 in a similar form.
In the last two years, rebuilding of the fishing fleet has taken
place on an unprecedented scale. Both motor vessels, mostly of the size from
50 to 110 gross registered tons and steam trawlers of the size 650 gross
registered tons, have been contracted for and built, the former mainly in
Sweden and the latter in Great Britain. At the end of the rebuilding program,
which is to be the year l948, the gross tonnage of the fishing fleet is
expected to be about 58,000 compared with 26,000 in 1939. About 50 percent
will be steam trawlers and the rest motor vessels.
A great increase has also taken place in the capacity of the fish-
processing industry, mainly in the freezing plants and the herring reduction
plants.
VENEZUELA, 1947
The report received by FAo Fisheries Division from the Venezuelan
National FA0 Committee states that the increase in production in 1947 compared
with previous years is explained by the intensive encouragement which the
Government has given to the industry. "The Banco Agricola y Pescuario during
the first nine months of 1947," the report goes on, "granted 567 fisheries
credits amounting to Bs. 1,268,942 (approximately $38,000). In 1946 the number
of such credits granted was 472, amounting to Bs. 594,398 (approximately
$17,800) and in 1945 only 54 such credits were granted, amounting to Bs. 97,800
(approximately $2,900).

CURRENT FISHERIES
Canada, February, 1948
(Extract from Monthly Review of Canadian Fisheries Statistics, February, 1948)
An exceptionally large catch of herring in Pacific waters was the
outstanding feature of Canada's commercial fishery during February. The
herring for the most part was converted into meal for poultry and animal
foode. The Atlantic Coast fishery also enjoyed better fishing. The total
value of landings in the four eastern provinces was $710,ooo for the month
compared with $308,000 in February, 1947.
Total landings of fish and shellfish in the sea fisheries during
February, 1948, amounted to 45,071 metric tons, valued at $1,694,000. The
huge increase over February 1947 was attributable to two main causeae the
herring catch in British Columbia accounting for 36,957 metric tons as
against 2,949 metric tons and the continuance of normal operations on the
Atlantic Coast, whereas a year ago a strike of deep-sea fishermen was in
progress. Total landings on the Pacific Coast amounted. to 37,830 metric
tons valued at $984,000. On the Atlantic Coast the total catch was reater
in all provinces. Increased landings of sardines were the main factor in
the better showing in New Brusnwick.
The total value of fisheries products imported into Canada in 1947,
approximately $5,000,000, was $473,000 above the 1946 total. The total value
of cod imported from Newfoundland decreased, but imports from European sources
began to figure more prominently in Canada's imports in l947. Norwegian canned
herring and sardines returned to the market after several years, reaching a
value of $685,000 for the year. Peru entered the Canadian market with canned
tuna to the value of $182,000. Imports from the United States, mainly canned
shellfish - clams, crabs and shrimps - increased sharply, but imports of
shelled oysters declined.
The index number of wholesale prices of fishery products rose 5.4
points during the month, while that of meat and poultry prices rose by 29.3
points. The general index of all foods rose 9.4 points. Prices to fishermen
at Atlantic Coast points were higher for groundfish but lower for bbsters.
Retail prices of fresh, frozen, and smoked fish in Eastern and Central Canada
were higher in February than in the previous month.

According to Fiskaren1/(February 13), Dr. Jens Eggvin, Chief of
the Oceanographic research carried on by the Fisheries Administration,
Norway, explained that due to the small rainfall of the last year, saltier
North Sea water approached the shores of the Norwegian coast than was usual.
This may be, he explains, part of the reason for the increased herring
fisheries.
Owing to stormy weather, the landings of the cod fisheries are still
lagging behind the previous year. According to statistics brought up to 20
March, 68,993 metric tons had been landed, compared with 158,262 metric tons
for the same period in 1947. Of the total landings in 1948, 4,751 metric tons
were used for stockfish, 38,295 for saltfish and 25,947 were markoted fresh or
frozen. The quantities used for stockfish and saltfish are still much smaller
than the previous year. The quantity used for fresh fish almost oquals that
of the previous year. In the last prewar year, 1939, the total landings for
the same period were about 90,000 metric tons; out of this, however, only
4,0o0 metric tons were marketed fresh or frozen.
The height of the Lofoten fishery season is generally at the end of
March, when between 25,000 and 30,000 fishermen are actively engaged. On
March 13 this year about 20,000 fishermen had arrived and more were still
arriving.
According to Fiskaren (February 13), experienced fishermen have
indicated that the results from the main spawning cod fisheries of Norway,
which are carried on off the Lofoten Islands, may be smaller than the previous
year, which was record-beating. It is true that the spawning cod is coming
1 Weekly paper issued at Bergen, Norway, by the Norwegian Fishermen's Union.

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.95)
-24-
late to the Lofoten fishing field but Mr. Rollefsen, Director of Biologio
Research at the Fisheries Administration of Norway, expresses the view to
Fiskaren that one should still be optimistic about the total landings. Cod
spawned in 1937 are now 11 years old and this very mumerous year-class still
dominates the stock available. Study of the distribution of the salinity and
temperature of Arctic waters indicates that the cod has probably been further
east in the Arctic Ocean than has been usual for some years. The delayed arrival
of cod at the Lofoten spawning grounds may therefore be due to the greater dis-
tance which the fish have had to travel.
As to the future, the outlook is not so favorable, for, after 1937,
there are not any year-classes which are extremely numerous.
Donmark, 1948
A cable from the Ministry of Fisheries, Denmark, states that total
landings in January, 1948, amounted to 20,000 motric tons, compared with 15,000
tons in January, 1947. Total landings in February, 1948, amounted to 17,000
metric tons, compared with 6,000 tons in February 1947. Such 1948 figures
show a considerable increase in total landings.
The Newfoundland Fisheries Board reports that stocks of salted codfish
on hand as of 20 February, 1948, were 15,108 metric tons (297,396 quintals) as
compared with 3,951 metric tons (77,683 quintals) on 28 February, 1947. Exports
(unrevised) of salted codfish during February, 1948, amounted to 3,504 metric
tons (68,985 quintals). Of this amount 1,851 metric tons (36,438 quintals) were
shipped to Puerto Rico. Exports in February, 1947, reached 8,175 metric tons
(160,919 quintals).

Subject:
SANACC 99/1O, U. S POLICY FOR JAPANESE FISHING AND
OTHER AQUATIĆ INDUSTRIES
Reference is made to your memorandum of December 3, 1948,
894.628/12-1048
in which you request my comments in connection with the state-
ment by the Army Member of SANACC proposing amendments to 99/8.
The main effeots of the proposals made by the Army Member
would seem to be (1) to shorten the period before reconsidera-
tion of the question of possible Japanese expansion into the
entire southern portion of the area recommended by SCAP from
three months after approval of the paper (as provided in 99/8)
to the fixed date "February 15, 1949" and (2) to extend the
area of expansion to be permitted SCaP within one month (in-
stead of two months) after approval of the paper to include,
in addition to the eastward area contemplated in 99/s, a
southern area that skirts Wake, then drops a few degrees, dips
up to avoid the Marianas, and then drops again.
With regard to the second point above, the general effeot
of the Army proposal would be to accord practically the whole
southern expansion desired by SCAP short of admitting the
Japanese to the territorial waters of the Trust Territory.
Acceptance of the Army proposal would thus go far apparently
toward prejudging the issue of southern expansion.
894.6
I feel that it is highly desirable that a policy decision
be reached at the earliest possible date on the question
whether we are or are not to permit southern expansion of
C
2
Japanese fisheries. We have been discussing the elements
in-
8
yolved in this question for many months. Several of the
15
arguments
ILED
-1048
SECRET

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
SECRET
arguments presented in 99/10 by the Army Member of SANACC would
seem to have much to commend them, such as, for example, his
reference to the possible effects of 99/8 upon our efforts
internationally to obtain most-favored-nation treatment for
Japan. At the same time, it is recognized that there are other:
legitimate but conflicting interests involved. It would seem
that these interests could be reconciled as effeotively now
as they could be on February 15, 1949 - or, for that matter,
after the lapse of three months as proposed in 99/8. It is
difficult to perceive what developments in the situation are
likely to occur in such brief space of time that cannot now be
foreseen.
If, however, it is in fact impracticable to reach agreement
on the overall question, it would seem to me that the solution
proposed in 99/8 presents a reasonable temporizing solution --
that is, it permits Japanese expansion within two months into
the non-controversial eastern area, thus easing SCAp's situation
to some extent, and at the same time it keeps the issue of
possible southern expansion alive by providing for reconsider-
ation of that question after three months of the paper's approval.
I peroeive no objection to the proposal of the Army Member
that appropriately detailed assurances from SCAP of Japanese
compliance with fishing conservation praotices be accepted in-
stead of "proof of compliance". If additional reassurance in
connection with Japanese compliance is desired, SCAP might be
specifically instructed to send a supervisory patrol vessel or
vessels - possibly with international personnel - to maintain
some direct track of how the Japanese are fishing and to make
reports as required. Such procedure might meet the end sought
in "proof of compliance" without being subject to the objections
which might attach to that phrase.
Similarly, I conour in the proposal of the Army Member that
the word "entire" be inserted before the reference to the southern
portion of the area of possible expansion. (page 57, line 4)
CONCLUSIONS:
I would prefer that immediate decision on the question of
southern expansion of Japanese fishing be reached.
If that is impracticable, I would favor adoption of 99/8
as a reasonable temporizing measure but wo uld accept the proposal
of the

for the establishment by SCAP of supervisory control measures
such as visits by a patrol vessel or vessels to the Japanese
fishing grounds.

Appendix "A"
POLICY WITH RESPECT TO FISHING AND AQUATIC IMDUSTRIES
IN JAPAN
1. Subjest to the requirements of this and other policies established
in accordance with the terms of reference of the Far Kastern Comissiem, the
Japanese should use their existing resources to the maximm extent pessible
in order to obtain fish for domestic consumption and export.
2. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers may at his dissretiem
expand present Japanese fishing or other commercial aqustic estivities. If
such expansion will involve fishing or other commercial aquatie setivities
in an area in which any country has a direct concern, arrangements shall be
made between SCAP and such country.
3. The Japanese should conform strictly to the provisioms of inter
national agreements for the conservation of fishery and other marime reseuress.
4. Nothing in the foregoing should be interpreted as predetermiming
the ultimate constitution and sise of the Japanese fishing fleet nor the
possible availability for removal as reparations of Japanese fisking and
pearl fishing ships, equipment and other facilities.

POLICY WITH RESPECT TO FISHING AND AQUATIC INDUSTETES
IN JAPAN
1. Subject to the requirements of this and other policies establisbe
in accordance with the terms of reference of the Far Bestern Cammsetem
Japanese should use their existing resources to the maximm ertemt pepst
in order to obtain fish for domestic consumption and export.
2. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers may at his dissretiem
expand present Japanese fishing or other commercial aquatic activitiess 
such expansion will involve fishing or other commercial aquatie aetiviti
in an area in which any country has a direct concern, arrangemente shall be
made between SCAP and such country.
3. The Japanese should conform strictly to the provisions ef intes
national agreements for the conservation of fishery and other marime resous
4. Nothing in the foregoing should be interpreted as predetermiming
the ultimate constitution and sise of the Japanese fishing fleet nor the
possible availability for removal as reparations of Japanase fishing and
pearl fishing ships, oquipment and other facilities.

FOR OCCUPIED AREAS
DATE: Jamary 10, 1949
TO
A. L. Koyes AUK.
FROM
SUBJECT:
Japanese Fishing.
Referring to your question of January 7 on the above subjeet,
this memorandum will give you the informtion desired.
Paragraph 5c. was intended to insure that the Japanese would
abide by the same type of conservation practices in coastal fishing
which the United States enforces with respect to its coastal fisheries
(as distinct from international agreements covered in paragraph 5b.).
However, it was recognised that the language of paragraph 5 as a
whole placed an unfortunate emphasis on U. S. policies rather than
international policies. Accordingly, by informal agreement the
whole of paragraph 5 was changed when put to the FEC, so as to read
as follows: "The Japanese should conform strictly to the provisions
of international agreements for the conservation of fisheries and
2
other marine resources."
8
It is probable that no definition of "direct conoern", as
provided in paragraph 6, will be agreed by the FEC. As a result
of this, it is probable that the FEC will not adopt that policy.
Therefore, if a particular country believes that SCap is fishing
in an area of "direct concern" to it, it will as a practical mtter
file objections both with SCAP and the U. S. Government. We, of
course, would then try to reach an amicable settlement in particular
cases. This problem has already arisen due to Philippine objections
6
to a small corner of the newly proposed fishing area which runs
within 30 miles of some Philippine Islands. If the Army agrees to
a contraction of this line, SCAP will be advised that the U. S.
Government concurs in this case with the Philippine Government
and believes that SCAP should not fish in the area involved with-
out agreement by the Philippine Government.
On January 15 the U. S. Government will presumably advise
SCAP that he is authorised to permit the extension of Japanese
fishing into the new area proposed by SCaP. Our action in this
regard can be justified under either of two possibilities:
(a) If the FEC has adopted our "direct concern" policy,
215
ScAP would be justified in fishing in the new area because
it is not of direct concern to any other country.
(b) If the FEC has not adopted our policy, SCAP can act
dat_
within his administrative powers to act. This assumes that
no other polioy has been adopted, which is unlikely in view
of the shortage of time.

Tokyo.
894.628/1-1949
The Secretary of State refers to the Mission's despatch No. 4
of January 19, 1949 on the subject, "Movements of Japanese Fishing
Vessels Outside Authorised Area". In the final sentence ef this
despatch, the Department's instructions are invited as to mhat type
of reply might appropriately be made to Soviet complaints of alleged
violations by Japanese vessels of "Soviet territorial waters".
The Department is in agreement with the Offiser in Charge that
the Soviet Member, Allied Council for Japan, is expleiting the
opportunity which these alleged violations afford to advanse Soviet
territorial claims to islands adjacent to Northern Hokkaido. The
Department supports the intention of the Offieer in Charge to avoid,
in the course of Headquarters correspondence with the Soviet Member,
any acknowledgment of Soviet territorial claims. No objection is
perceived to replying to further Soviet complaints on this subjest,
provided no implied or direct expression of United States views on
territorial questions is given.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,

Sir:
I have the honor to bring to the Department's
attention the reported enactment by the Chinese Legis-
lative Tuan of a law providing for the seizure and
xn
ultimate disposition or Japanese fishing vessels found
on the high seas outside the authorized fishing area.
893.0145
A direotive of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers of June 22, 1946 (SCAPIN 1033, as amended by
SCAPIN 1033/1 of December 23, 1948) established an area
in which Japanese fishing is authorized (this Mission's
despatoh no. 12 of January 7, 1949.) Occasional movements
of Japanese fishing vessels outside the authorized fishing
area have been the oause of protests to this Mission, in
its oapacity as Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters,
by the Chinese Mission in Japan and by the office of the
Soviet Member, Allied Counoil for Japan. A number of
cases have also occurred in which Chinese and Soviet
patrol authorities have seized Japanese fishing vessels
found outside the authorized fishing area.
It is the position of General Headquarters that all
Japanese fishing activities on the high seas are governed
by the Supreme Commander's directives establishing the
authorized fishing area, that these direotives are
subject to enforcement by the Supreme Commander only, and
that it is the sole responsibility of the Supreme Commander,
or his designated agents, to deal with violations  thege
directives.

the authorized fishing area, such violations were still
continuing, and that the Chinese Mission had been direoted
by the Chinese Government to notify the Japanese Govern-
ment through General Headquarters "that the Chinese
Government will have to take appropriate measures, unless
such violations are effectively checked". In reply this
Mission under date of June 22, 1948 requested the Chinese
Mission to secure in each case of reported violation
information of sufficient probative val ue to enable
effective prosecution of the offenders, and further noted
that Japanese fishing activities on the high seas are
governed by the Supreme Commander's direotives whioh are
subject to enforcement by the Supreme Commander only.
In May 1948 coastal patrols of the Chinese Navy
sank one Japanese fishing vessel and captured another,
which action reportedly took place in waters outside the
authorized area approximately seventy miles northeast of
Jueshan island, off Hangchow Bay. Two Japanese orew
members were killed in the sinking of one vessel. The
survivors were later returned to Japan, but the one oaptured
vessel was confiscated by the Chinese Government.
It has now come to the attention of this Mission that
the August 29, 1948 issue of the Chinese newspaper Ta Kung
Pao published the text of a law reportedly enacted by the
Legislative Yuan which provides for the seizure and disposi-
tion of Japanese fishing vessels found operating outside
the authorized fishing area. An English translation of
1/
the Ta Kung Pao article is enclosed with this despatoh.
Although information concerning this law comes from
an unofricial source, this Mission considers the matter
of sufficient importance to have brought it to the
attention of the Chinese Mission. Enclosed herewith is
a copy of a communication sent to the Chinese Mission
2/
by this Mission, in its capacity as Diplomatie Section,
on January 24, 1949. The Chinese Mission is referred
to previous correspondence on this subject in whioh this
Mission pointed out specifically that Japanese fishing
operations on the high seas are governed entirely by
directives of the Supreme Commander. The Chinese Mission
is accordingly requested to investigate the matter of
the law described in the August 29, 1948 issue of Ta Kung Pao.
A somewhat similar problem hes also arisen in connection
with seizure by Soviet patrol authorities of Japanese
fishing vessels outside the authorized fishing area in
the vioinity of the Kuril Islands and Sakhalin. In this
conneotion

Regarding the matter of fishing operation by a
Japanese fishing vessel crossing the border, the Department
of Agrioulture and Forestry, after consultation with the
Departments of Home Administration, Diplomatio Relations
and Justioe, and the Naval General Headquarters, established
the Emergency Law concerning Japanese Fishing Vessel
operating Fishery orossing the border, whioh has lately
been ratiried by the Legislative Yuan of the Chinese
Government.
The whole passages of this law are as follows:
Artiole l. Any Japanese fishing vessel which operates
fishery crossing the border shall be disposed
of in accordance with this Law.
Artiole 2.
Before the conclusion of the peace treaty
with Japan, the fishing area for Japanese
fishing vessels shall be confined within the
sphere provided by GHQ, SCAP, and the tems
"any Japanese fishing vessel whioh operates
fishery orossing the border" shall mean any
Japanese fishing vessel which operates fishery
in the direction to China crossing the said
sphere.
Article 3.
Any Japanese fishing vessel whioh operates
fishery orossing the border, its fishing gears
and catoh shall be confiscated, and the master
and crew aboard the vessel shall be deported
from this country.
Article 4.
Any Japanese fishing vessel which operates
fishery orossing the border, in case of its
being captured, shall be disposed of by the
supreme competent authorities of fishery
in accordance with the provision of the
preceding Artiole after investigating the
affairs in the presence of the capturing
authorities.

and the capturing authorities shall, after

Article 6. This Law shall apply to any Japanese fishing

Article 7.

MEMORANDUM
Tokyo, January 24, 1949.
Referemm is made to the Diplomatio Seotion's
memorandum of June 22, 1948 and to related correspondenoe
concerning reports of violations of the area authorized
by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers for
Japanese fishing by Japanese fishing vessels and orews
operating beyond the authorized fishing area in the Rast
China Sea.
In the Diplomatio Section's memorandum of June 22
and in subsequent discussion with representatives of
the Chinese Mission in Japan on July 22, 1948, it was
requested that in cases of reported violations of the
authorized fishing area the Chinese Mission furnish the
Diplomatic Section with information of sufficient pro-
bative value that the offenders can be effeotively
proseouted, in as muoh as Japanese fishing aotivitios
on the high seas are governed by directives issued by
the Supreme Commander which are subject to enforcement
by the Supreme Commander only.
In this connection it has come to the attention
of the Diplomatio Section that the August 29, 1948
issue of the Chinese newspaper Ta Kung Pao, published
in Shanghai, contained an artiole desorlbing a law
reportedly passed by the Legislative Yuan oonoerning
measures to be taken against Japanese fishing vessels
operating on the high seas outside the authorized fishing
area. Aocording to English translation, Artiole 1 of
the new law, as reported by the newspaper Ta Kung Pao,
provides that any Japanese fishing vessel whioh passes
beyond the boundary of the authorized area shall be
disposed of in aocordance with this law. Similarly,
Artiole 2 is reported to refer to the confining of
Japanese fishing before the conclusion of a peace treaty
with Japan to the areas as authorized by the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers. The law is fur ther
described by Ta Kung Pao as providing for the seizure
by Chinese authoritles of Japanese fishing vessels found
on the high seas outside the authorized fishing area,
and, according to Artiole 6, this law is reportedly to
apply to any Japanese fishing vessel which was captured
prior to its enforcement.

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.96)
418S7A
mteh No. 52, Jamuary 25,
While the Diplomatio Seotion is aware that its
information concerning this law comes from an unorfieial
souree, it feels that this is a matter of sufficiest
importance to be brought to the attention of the Chimese
Mission, since the reported provisions of the law appear
to contemplate seizure by Chinese authorities of Japanese
fishing vessels not simply in Chinese waters but on the
high seas. As noted in the Diplomatic Seotion's
memorandum of June 22, 1948, Japanese fishing activities
on the high seas are governed entirely by directives
issued by the Supreme Commander whioh are accordingly
subjeot to enforoement by the Supreme Commander only.
It would be appreciated therefore if the Chinese Mission
would investigate whether suoh a law as is deseribed in
the August 29 issue of Ta Kung Pao has been promulgated
and inform the Diplomatio Section in the premises.

TO
: Mr. Claxtonr
DATE: Fobruary 10, 1949
SUBJECT: Japanese Fishing.
This memorandum will give you the position on the above subject
upon my departure. Mr. Moseley will follow-up for "O" hereafter.
It was agreed by SANACC that the matter of fishing in the Trust
Area might be broached again on February 15. I assume the Army will
raise it on that day, although I have avoided discussing it with the
Army. Dr. Chapman advises that in his opinion there have been no
894.628/2-1049
developments since the previous discussion which would warrant a
change in the U. S. Government position. Actually, the expansion
of fishing into the eastern Pacific was only authorized about six
weeks ago, so that it would be impossible at this date to have any
conclusive proof as to whether Japanese fishing in the expanded area
was being performed on a more law-abiding and conservation-minded
basis. Nevertheless, MacArthur probably believes that he can control
Japanese fishing adequately to insure protection of the natives and
fish conservation in the Trust Area. Accordingly, there will be some
dispute of fact when this issus is raised. Dr. Chapman recommends
that the State Department take no action on this subjeot until the
Army raises the question. Mr. Barnett expressed no disagreement
with that recommendation.
Mr. Barnett advises that there is no chance of FEC adoption of
the U. S. proposed policy which would authorize MacArthur to permit
r
expansion of Japanese fishing provided that fishing in an area of
"direct concern to another country should be subject to negotiation
with that country. Apparently, another paper has been introduced in
the FEC which would propose to define areas of "direct concern" to
894, 62
various countries. In all probability neither paper will be adopted,
but Mr. Barnett sees no great danger in this result.
The Philippines object to one corner of the expanded fishing area
authorized six weeks ago and we accordingly suggested to Macarthur
that he negotiate with The Philippines before authorising fishing in
that area. I believe he is conducting such negotiations, but we have
not yet heard the outcome.
cc: Dr. Chapman, U/FW

SCRET (For Department Use Only)
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
APO 500
MEMORANDUM POR THE CHIEF OF STAFF
I. THE PROBLEM:
To ensure compliance by Japanese fishermen with provisions
of SCAPIN 1033.
II. FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM:
1. SCAPIN 1033, dated 22 June 1946, defines the SCAP-author-
ised Japanese fishing area.
2. Seventy-eight violations of SCAPIN 1033, involving 127
Japanese fishing boats, were reported between 30 December 1946 and
20 October l948. Evidence sufficient to sustain convictions in
Japanese courts was obtained in only three cases.
3. The government of Russia and China have mgressed concern
over alleged violations. Both China and Korea have confiscated Japa-
nese fishing boats apprehended outside the authorised fishing ares.
4. A Japanese Government plan for a single patrol vessel
to enforce compliance with provisions of SCAPI lO33 was submitted
to SCAP for approval. The plan was not approved becanse the Chinese
Government objected to it. Continuous but unsuocessful attempts have
been made to obtain voluntary compliance. In conference 22 July 194s.
Chinese Government representatives expressed the opinion that the Jape-
nese are unreliable and that United States vessels should patrel the
authorised area.
5. On 13 August 1948, the Japanese Government submitted a
second plan for the establishment of patrolling vessels to enforce
the provisions of SCAPIM l033. This plan can be made reasonably ef-
fective with SCAP assistance.
III. DISCUSSION:
1. Violations of SCAPIN 1033 and subsequent repercussions
are affecting Occupation objectives adversely. Current inability of
the Japanese Government to enforce the provisions of SCAPI l033 is
resulting in:
a. Disrespect among the Japanese for SCaP and Japanese
Government directives.
b. Criticism
SICRET (For Department Use Only)

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 92,
February 12, 1949.
- 2-
b. Criticism by other coumtries of sCap's ability to
enforce directives to the Japanese Government. Suspicion that the
Japanese will not respect limitations on their activities will af-
fect Japan's interests at peace conferences. Currently, it is af-
fecting negotiation of international agreements in respect to fish-
ing area, Japanese attendance at international fishery meetings.
and other similar problems.
2. Representatives of COMNAVJAP, FEAF and G-3 state that
United States surface or aircraft units are not available for pe-
trolling or for supervision of a Japanese patrol program. A Japa-
nese patrol, by itself, would not provide a positive check on Japa-
nese enforcement or full assurance to other countries that Japanese
fishermen are obeying restrictions placed upon them.
IV. CONCLUSIONS:
1. The Japanese Government is responsible for enforcing
provisions of SCAPIN l033. It cannot discharge its responsibility
effectively unless a patrol system is established and assistance
provided by Occupation Forces. Effective surveillance may be obtained
by (1) establishment of a Japanese patrol system; (2) intermittent
assignment, as required, of United States naval officers to accom-
pany Japanese patrol vessels: (3) reports by CONNAVJAP vessels and
FEar aircraft on routine operations of Japanese fishing vessels ob-
served outside the anthorised area.
2. The patrol plan would require: (1) authorisation to
operate patrol vessels in certain waters outside the authorised
fishing area: (2) X100,000,000 for financing the program: (3) allo-
cation of a small quantity of controlled materials: and (4) the use
of patrol vessels with sufficient speed to be effective.
a. Operation of patrol vessels in the area specified
in map, would be necessary in order to locate, idemtify, and report
fishing vessels outside the authorised ares. Patrol vessels weald
have the same status as other vessels under the jurisdictien of
SCAJAP, which are not limited by the provisions of SCAPIM l033.
Approach closer than ten miles to the coast of any foreign country
would be prohibited. Patrol vessels would not be permitted to en-
gage in fishing operations, in conformity with the Chinese Govern-
ment's primary objection to Japanese patrol operations.
b. Appropriation of 3100,000,000 for the enforcement
program appears justified. Alternative patrol by United States
forces would be at the expense of the United States Government and
would remove responsibility from the Japanese Government.
c. Allecation of controlled materials to provide neo-
essary radio communication appears justified. The quemtity requested
is reasonable and radio commumication would contribute to effective
surveillance over Japanese fishing operations.
d. The speed of the proposed patrol vessels would ap-
proximate that of large fishing boats. Faster vessels are met 
vailable at present. To avoid delay, the preposed pregram as medi-
fied, should be approved and the Japanese Govermmeat instrusted to
replace initial patrol vessels by faster ships as rapidly as possible.
V. RECONINDATIONS:
SHORET (For Department Use Only)

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.95)
Eaclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 92,
Febrwary 12, 1949.
-3-
V. RECOMCENDATIONS:
1. That the Japanese Government be instructed to establish
a patrol system in accerdance with proposed SCAPI.
2. That COMMAVJAP be directed to instruct all surface araft
on routime patrol or voyages to report Japanese fishing boats obeerved
outside the authorised aree.
3. That CoMAVJAP be directed to assign United States naval
officers to duty aboard Japanese patrol vessels, as necessary, te im-
sure proper surveillance of Japanese patrol operations and to deter-
mine the effectiveness of such operations in enforcement of SCaPIN
1033.
4. That CG, FEar, be directed to instruct all airoraft
on routine flights to report Japanese fishing boats observed out-
side the authorised area.

staff study is a draft telegram which, it is expected, will be sent
shortly to the Department of the Army announcing the Suprme Cem
mander's intention to authorize this expedition.
As stated in the airgram above cited, it is anticipated that
any complaints made by Allied Powers against the Supreme Commander's
proposal will be directed against the addition of a third fleet
rather than against the principle of Japanese participation im Amt-
arotic whaling. I believe, however, that the addition of a third
fleet is desirable in as much as it will contribute to the reduc-
tion of Japan's continuing shortage of food and oil and besause
capture of 1500 Blue Whale Units, which is the estimsted eateh for
Japan if three fleets are authorized and which is .093 of the tetal
allowed by the International Whaling Convention, doee net appear to
be an excessive or preferential figure for Japan. Japan's emsellent
safety record in taking an average of .O4 illegal whales over the
past three seasons, and excellent production record in extresting
122 barrels of oil per whale unit for the past season, offer further
evidence of Japan's efficiency and desire to abide by internatiemal
agreements. In addition, it is estimted that operation of three
fleets during the coming season will save the United Statee Geverm-
ment about $22,000,000, or $4,000,000 more than the saving result-
ing from the 1948-1949 expedition.

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.99)
UNCLASSIFIED
Enclosure to Despatch No. 110
States Political Adviser for J
Tokyo, subject: "Catch of Jap
Antarctic Whaling Expedition at
Half-Way Point."
COPY
Press Release by General Headquarters, February 10, 1949:
WHALING EXPEDITION RADIOS HALF-MARK REPORT
Whaling operations being conducted by the two-fleet 1948-1949
Japanese Antarctic Whaling expedition reached the half-way mark
this week with a total of 654 whales. (490 blue whale units) which
have been processed into 53,990, 245 pounds of products, William M.
Terry, Production and Processing Branch, Fisheries Division, Natural
Resources Section, SCAP, announced today.
"To date the catch has fallen below last year's record of 661
whales (540.5 blue whale units)," Terry stated.
"According to radio messages received from Lt. Comdr. Francois
Bourgois, French Navy, and Maj. James A. Crombie, Buffalo, N.Y.,
SCAP representatives aboard the two factory ships, the current low
catch is due to an unusual scarcity of whales and adverse weather
conditions."
According to production figures sent to Fisheries Division,
highly efficient operation has resulted in the processing of a total
of 17,055,675 pounds of whale oil, 13,230 pounds of liver oil and
36, 204, 715 pounds of frozen and salted meat and blubber.
More than 1, 500 tone of salted and frozen meat have already
been brought to Japan by refrigerated carriers for distribution
through official ration channels. The carrier Chikuzen Maru is
expected to dock at Osaka early next week with an additional 776
tons of frozen whale products.

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.98)
United States Political A
Japan, Tokyo, on the oubjest
Japanese Antarctic'Uhaling
(COPY)
Kenneth J. Coonan,
Australian Whaling Observer
On board Nissin Maru,
Antarctic Ocean.
Tuesday lst February 1949.
Dear Colonel,
When I heard from Mr. Terry that I was to sail onboard Nissin
Maru for this season. I must admit that I didn't feel very happy
about the idea, after all, I had sailed onboard Hashidate Maru for
two seesons and consequently had made quite a few friends onboard
that ship, however after watching Nissin Maru operate I can only
say that I am grateful to have had the opportunity of sailing on her
While the actual methods of operation do not differ greatly. I
consider that this ship is more efficient. The men work with a will
and show a keenness which appeared to be lacking amongst the workmen
onbcard Hashidate Maru....The factory is in charge of a very efficient
man and due mainly to his efforts the oil producing equipment runs
very smoothly.
Whaling wes very slow for the month of December and for the egrly
part of January, but it has commenced to brighten up now, our best
weekly catch was that for the week ending 29th of January when 43 Blue.
whales and 58 Finbacks were captured, and the best daily catch was that
on the 28th January when 19 Blue whales and 4 Finbacks were taken.
The cetch of 19 Blue whales and 4 Finbacks was very good, of this
number 12 Blue and four Finbacks were processed in 24 hours and the last
of the remaining 7 Blue whales were in the boiler by O930 on the follow
ing morning. the 29th, 6 Blue whales and 12 Finbacks were killed on this
day and the number processed was 11 Blue and 6 Fins leaving 2 Blue and
6 Fins to be carried over to the next day the 30th of January when 2
Blue and 14 Fins were taken all of these with the exception of 5 fin-
backs were processed in 24 hours and the remaining 5 Fins were carried
over to the next dey.
At no time was any whale in the water over 16 hours. During the
killing of these whales the cetcher boats were ordered by the manager
to cease killing at different times during the 24 hours, this ensured
a fresh carcass and a high oil yield per B.W.U. resulted.
During the processing of these whales, Major Crombie and myself
kept watches on the flensing deck and in the factory to ensure that
there was no waste of material and I am happy to say that at no time
was there any evidence of waste or violations of the Whaling Regule-
tions, during the operations I noticed that the launches were bring-
Ing back scraps of meat from the transporters, these scraps had been
cut off the meat which was being salted down or frozen and mre placed
in the boilers.

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.99)
Bnolosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 235,
Apri1 19, 1949.
Nissin Maru appears to have no difficulty in processing l4 B.W.V.
per day, her boilers have been operating efficiently up to date, and
meat and blubber for salting down or freesing is sent to the trans-
porters with all possible speed, the transporters lay very close. to
the factory ship. this cuts down the running time of the launches.
Major Crombie has watched the daily catch and production vory
closely and is most cereful that there is no over fishing, the
catcher boats have been ordered to cease killing on several occasions
The Major is very interested in the production of whale oil and
he has carried out many experiments in the lab. with a view to im-
proving the quality of the oil and also the amount gained per B.W.U.
I wish to thank you Colonel, for the courtesy and co-operation
that I have received from Major Crombie and the officers of this ship
in regards to receiving technical information on the plant, I am sure
that this information will be of great assistance to our Fisheries
Division.
Well the season is nearly over once again, and soon we will be
pointing our bows to the North and home. I get terribly homesick
while I'm away and I aren't ashamed to admit it....after all there
is no place like home.
So once again Colonel, thanks for everything.
Kindest Regards,
/s/ Kenneth Coonan
1t/
Kenneth James Coonan,
Observer For Australia.

Formosa Disposition Must Await Treaty
Island in Same Category as Kuriles - McDermott
United Press
Washington, Apr. 17.--State Department spokesman
Michael McDermott said final disposition of Formosa
must await a peace treaty with Japan.
McDermott made the comment when a reporter drew
his attention to twe fact that.a newspaper columnist
had said the United States may be forced either to
claim ownership of Formosa or face the possibility of
it falling into the hands of Chinese Communists.
McDermott said Chinese-occupied Formosa was in the
same category as the Kurile Islands at present occupied
by Russia and disposition of both areas is subject to
a peace treaty

(For Department use only)
subject of: "Fourth Japanese Amtas

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Natural Resources Section
APO 500
HGS/WCH/CMA/WMT/jb
MEMORANDUM FOR THE CHIEF OF STAFF.
I. THE PROBLEM.
Authorization of three Japanese whaling fleets to operate
in the Antarctic during the 1949-1950 whaling season.
II. FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM.
1. SCAP authorized Japanese whaling expeditions to oper-
ate in the Antarctic during the 1946-47, 1947-48, and 1948-49 seasons
because of the shortage of protein foods and edible oils in Japan.
These three expeditions produced 135,000 tons of edible oil and
protein foods. These products not only aided in the solution of
Japan's food problem, but represented a saving of $45,000,000 to
the United States taxpayer. (Tab A)
2. Six nations engaged in Antarctic whaling during the
1948-49 season with a total of 18 fleets and 3 land stations.
Norway operated 10 of the fleets and one land station. Japan's two
fleets comprised 8 percent of the total deadweight tonnage. (Tab B)
3. An expedition consisting of three fleets may be ex-
pected to produce approximately 75,000 metric tons of odible oil
and protein foods to supplement the Japanese economy.
4. The critical shortage of protein foods and edible oil
which justified the authorization of the first three post-surrender
expeditions continues.
III. DISCUSSION.
1. It is advisable that a fourth whaling expedition be
authorized in order that: Japan may obtain an equitable share of
the world's natural resources represented by whales in the Antarctic;
the burden of meeting the food deficit, now borne entirely by the
United States, may be lightened; and use may be made of shipping
equipment, and trained personnel which now exist in Japan.
2. It is advisable that the number of factory ships be in-
creased to three, with accompapying catcher boats and auxiliaries
becaus e:
a. A third fleet may reasonably be expected to pro-
duce an additional 17,000 metric tons of whale products, increasing.
the
SECRET

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
No. 282, May 5, 1949.
-2-
the contribution of the whaling industry to the rehabilitation of
the Japanese economy and increasing the saving to the United States
taxpayer by approximately $4,000,000.
b. Adequate shipping which can be readily converted
to whaling type vessels exists in Japan and is surplus to Japan's
minimum shipping requirements. (Tab C)
3. An amount of fuel oil for the operation of a third fleet
was included in the petroleum budget for the 1950 fiscal year. If
that budget is approved sufficient fuel will be available.
4. It may be expected that some whaling nations will
object to Japanese operations in the Antarctic on the following
grounds:
a. In view of the 16,000 blue whale unit catch limit
per season, Japanese whaling operations, regardless of scale, re-
duce the catch that might be taken by other nations.
b. Japan's pre-war record of non-adherence to the pro-
visions of International Whaling Agreements and wasteful operation
should exclude her from Antarctic whaling.
c. The authorization of Japanese whaling expeditions
is a matter of principle and should be decided by the Far Eastern
Commission.
5. These objections may be answered as follows:
a. The objection that Japanese whaling would reduce
the catch of other nations is based upon an aversion to competition
and as such is prejudicial, inhumanitarian, and in direct opposi-
tion to the provisions of the Atlantic Charter and the Potsdam
Declaration. (Tab D)
b. Japan's pre-war whaling practices have no bearing
upon present operations. Under the supervision of SCAP the post-
surrender Japanese whaling expeditions have established a record
of compliance with International Whaling Agreements and efficiency
of operation which compares very favorably with those of other
nations. With continued SCAP supervision that record will be
maintained. (Tab E)
c. Radio from CSCAD ECON to SCAP W 82880 dtg 290002 Z
May 48 stated that SCAP possesses authority to permit Japanese Ant-
arctic whaling expeditions under the powers granted him by FEC.
(Tab F)
IV. RECOMMENDATIONS.
1. That a SCAP-administered, Japanese-manned expedition
be authorized to participate in Antarctic whaling during the 1949-50
season
SECRET

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.99)

no. 282, May 5, 1949.
-3-
season, and that the number of factory ships be limited te three.
2. That a radio be sent to the Department of Army stating
SCAP's intentions, and explaining that the expedition will be oper-
ated under the same general conditions as were the previous three.
(Tab G)

NR 442.3 (31 Mar 49)Fi

31 March 1949
MEMORANDUM FOR: Record
SUBJECT:
Objection of Norway and Britain to Japanese
Participation in Antarctic Whaling.
1. The Norwegian Government in an Aide Mémoire to the State
Department dated 13 April 1948 objected to Japanese participation
in Antarctic whaling on the grounds that its economy, which is to
a great extent dependent upon the whaling industry, would suffer
as a result of Japanese whaling operations in the Antarotic. The
Aide Mémoire stated in part: "For coming season, another Norwegian
factory ship . . . , will be ready to participate, and Japanese
participation will consequently under no circumstances increase
total oil production. It can only have effect of reducing outcome
of other countries' expeditions." The Aide Memoire further indi-
cated that Japanese operations in the Antarctic worked an undue
hardship upon a country which had expended great effort in the
development of the whaling industry, and had been instrumental in
establishing the International Whaling Conventions. The Government
of the United Kingdom and Australia have also advanced similar
agruments in the past.
2. Such objections are tantamount to maintaining that because
Norway and Great Britain have played a major role in the develop-
ment of the whaling industry they have acquired an inviolable right
to a major share of whale resources.
3. In answer to these objections the following may be considered:
a. The intent of the International Whaling Convention
signed at Washington, D.C., 2 December 1946, in placing a limit of
16,000 blue whale units upon annual catch was to protect the stock
of certain species of whales. It was not intended as an instrument
to creat an advantage for any nation or any group of nations.
b. The objections are based largely upon an aversion to
normal competition, and as such are prejudicial and inhumanitarian.
The Governments of the United States and Great Britain, in formulat-
ing the Atlantic Charter, agreed that, "They will endeavor, with due
respect for their existing obligations, to further the enjoyment by
all states, great or small, victor or vanquished, of access, on
equal terms, to the trade and to the raw materials of the world
which are needed for their economic prosperity." The Potsdam Decla-
ration stated in part: "Japan shall be permitted to maintain such
industry
CONFIDENTIAL (sic)

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
no. 282, May 5, 1949.
-8-
TAB D continued
NR 442.3 (31 Mar 49)Fi
industry as will sustain her economy and permit the execution of
just reparations in kind, but not those which would mable her to
re-arm for war. To this end, access to, as distinguished from
control of, raw materials shall be permitted."
The objections raised by the Norwegian and British
Governments are in obvious opposition to such policies.
c. At present the burden of meeting the deficit of pro-
tein foods and edible oils in Japan is borne almost entirely by the
United States. Authorization of an expedition consisting of three
fleets will lighten this burden. The two fleets of the 1947-48
Japanese whaling expedition produced 17,500 tons of whale oil,
enough to make up 13% of Japan's minimum edible oil requir ment
and 12% of her industrial oil requirement. A third fleet can be
expected to produce an additional 5,000 tons of oil plus an esti-
mated 12,000 tons of food products.
WILLIAM M. TERRY
Production and Processing Branch
Fisheries Division

31 March 1949
MEMORANDUM FOR: Record
SUBJECT:
Japanese Violations of International Whaling
Regulations.
1. It is probable that other whaling nations will objoct to
Japanese participation in Antarctic whaling because of Japan's pre-
war whaling practices, charging that Japanese whalers engaged in
indiscriminate, ruthless exploitation, without thought to conserva-
tion. While it is true that Japanese whalers did not adhere to the
provisions of international whaling agreements during pre-war years,
their conduct during that period has no bearing upon present opera-
tions. The following should be considered:
a. The Final Act of the International Convention for the
Regulation of Whaling, signed at Washington, D.C., 2 December 1946,
states in part: "The International Whaling Conference . . . recog-
nizes that even under the most favorable management and with the
most conscientious and experienced gunners and crews, it is inevi-
table that some whales will be taken illegally. . . ."
b. It is reported that during pre-war years Norwegian
whaling expedition maintained an average of slightly less than one
percent illegal whales taken. Sufficient data is not available to
compare this record with that of other whaling fleets, but it is
probable that the Norwegian record is the best.
c. During the 1946-47 season, the Japanese expedition took
1165 whales, 6 of which, or 0.4 percent were illegal. During the
1947-48 expedition 1323 whales were taken, 8 of which, or 0.6 pereent
were illegal. During the 1948-49 expedition 1645 whales were taken.
Three of these, or O.2 percent were illegal. The over-all average
for three Japanese post-surrender expeditionsis thus O.4 percent,
well below the record maintained by Norwegian fleets.
d. The low record of violations noted in c above is un-
questionably due in part to the presence of SCAP representatives
with the Japanese fleets. Since SCAP representatives will accom-
pany any future SCAP-authorized expeditions, the number of illegal
whales taken will continue to be small.
e. Efficiency of whaling operations and utilization of
whale carcases is normally measured in terms of barrels of oil pro-
duced per blue whale unit taken. An average yield of 1lo barrels
per unit is considered satisfactory performance. During the 1947-48
season the Japanese expedition achieved an average of 118.2 barrels
per
CONFIDENTIAL (sic)

=== Page 485 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
no. 282, May 5, 1949.
-10 -
TAB E continued
NR 442.3 (31 Mar 49)Fi
per unit. During the 1948-49 season the expedition's average was
122 barrels per unit.
2. From a standpoint of efficiency of operation and adherence
to international whaling agreements, the SCAP-supervised, Japanese-
manned whaling expeditions have maintained a standard which compares
most favorably with that of other nations.
WILLIAM M. TERRY
Production and Processing Branch

31 March 1949
MEMORANDUM FOR: Record
SUBJECT:
Australian Government's Question of SCAP Authority
Authorizing Whaling.
1. Prior to the 1948-49 expedition the Australian Government
in an Aide Mémoire to the Department of State dated 13 April 1948
explained: "(The) Australian Government . . . wishes to restate
its contention that Japanese participation in Antarctic whaling is
properly a matter of principle which should be decided by FEC .. .
The text of this Aide Mémoire was forwarded to SCAP in a radio from
CSCAD ECON WAR 81749 dtg 132212 May 48.
2, In radio to DA, C-60697 dtg 151033 May 48, SCAP answered
the Australian contention expressing the opinion that the challenge
to his authority to unilaterally permit Japanese participation in
Antarctic whaling was not without legal basis, and asked the United
States Government to issue an interim policy directive authorising
him to permit Japanese whaling at his own discretion.
3. In radio from CSCAD ECON W 82880 dtg 290002 May 48, tho
Department of State replied as follows: "Consider here you possess
all necessary authority to authorize third whaling expedition under
US directive issued you November 45 and filed with FEC . . . or
under your general powers as sole executive for Allied Powers in
Japan . . . there is no legal basis for protesting nation's conten-
tion (that) matter (is) not within your proper jurisdietion . . . ."
This radio further implies that SCAP possesses authority to limit
the size of the expedition as he sees fit.
WILLIAM M. TERRY
Pdoruction and Processing Branch
Fisheries Division

In view of continuing acute shortage of necessary food pro-
ducts and continuing pertinence of considerations set forth in
radios regarding first, second, and third expeditions, it is in-
tended to authorize a SCAP-administered, Japanese-manned Antarctic
whaling expedition for the 1949-50 season. In order to further
lighten the burden of meeting food deficits, now borne entirely by
the United States and to utilize better existing equipment, it is
intended that the number of factory ships be increased to three,
with accompapying vessels. Estimated increase in production of
whale products to be realized by addition of third fleet is 17,000
metric tons. This is a substantial contribution to the oconomde
rehabilitation of Japan and represents an increased saving of about
four million dollars to the United States taxpayer. Adequate steps
will be taken by SCAP to insure scrupulous adherence to all inter-
national whaling agreements and Allied Observers will be permitted
to accompany the expedition to observe its compliance with these
agreements.
