the primary concerns of the United Stetee Government in regard to fish-

He said that the sardine catch of West Coast companies had fallen

no reason why a provision in the peace treaty should not define the
high sess areas in whioh Japan should be allowed to fish. He pointed
to several international treaties as examples of limitation of fish-
ing areas by treety. In discussion of this point it was noted that
an unfortunate situation would result if all nations signing the
treaty with Jepan were to insiet on some Limitation of Japan's fishing
area, particularly at a peace conference where the position of Japan
as an equal had not yot been established.

I/have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch no.
284 of May 6, 1949, forwarding a memorandum of conversation with

certain representatives of the American fishing industry and to
1/
enclose a copy of a report appearing in the Nippon Times for

the American fishing experts.
The American represent atives are reported to have stated
that the Japanese Government should patrol the authorised fishing
area to ensure compliance with Headquarters' regulations and
that this area must be scrupulously observed before Japanese
fishermen can regain the good will of other nations and before
the present area should be extended.
The three American representatives are known to have had
an interview with General MacARTHUR in which he assured thm ofhis
determination to reform Japanese fishing practices and of hi
desire that the Japanese not interfere with the interests of
the American fishing industry. Officials of the Natural Resources
Section of General Headquarters state that the American experts
were very favorably impressed with the attitude of the Supreme
Commander and with the efforts of General Headquarters to regulate
the Japanese fishing indust ry.
These officials say that no consideration will be given to
expansion of the present Japanese fishing area until the Japame
demonstrate
CONFIDENTIAL
E
FION COPY
RETURN TO DC/R FILES WITHIN 14 DAYS, WITH A NOTATION GF

=== Page 495 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 317,
May 16, 1949.
-2
demonstrate their willingness and ability to comply with Occupation
directives.. A patrol by the Japanese Government, as described in
this Mission's despatch no. 92 of February 12, 1949, is considered
essential to ensure adequate Japanese compliance. À proposal for
authorizat ion of such a patrol is now being examined by the Economic
and Scientific Section to determine the effect of the proposed ex-
penditure on the Japanese budget. The Natural Resources Section
has succeeded in convincing concerned sections of General Headquarters
of the desirability of such a patrol or inspection system and it is
likely that such a system will be approved and instituted.
The attitude of the Philippine Government injects a further
element of uncertainty into proposals for expanding the present
area. The views of the Philippine Government were requested on
January 22, 1949, in regard to possible extension of Japanese fish-
2/
ing operations near the Philippines. A copy of a note dated January 22,
1949, from General Headquarters to the Philippine Mission in Japan,
containing this request is enclosed. No answer to this request has
been received. If the Philippine Government should insist on an
extensive security or conservation zone around the Philippine Islands,
further negotiation will probably be necessary.
Headquarters officials also feel that before the present area
can be expanded the Japanese must adopt effective conservation prac-
tices. Mr. Edward W. ALLEN, one of the visiting American experts,
reflected this view in the press conference of May 11, 1949, when
he said "The first recommendation we have for the Japanese fishing
industry is that the Japanese Government and the fishing industry
organizations should plan for a maximum possible sustained yield
within the authorized areas through a properly planned fishery pro-
gram." To accomplish this end Natural Resources Section officials
in cooperation with Japanese fisheries experts have prepared a bill
for presentation to the Japanese Diet providing for limitation of
Japan's fishing fleets. Details are not available but it is antici-
pated that when this bill is enacted there will be a very slight
reduction in the extent of Japan's fishing operations, principally
in the East China Sea, where Japan's catch will be reduced by about
400,000,000 pounds out of the approximate seven billion pound annual
catch. Natural Resources Section experts believe that reduction of
the size of the present fishing industry is an essential step toward
development of sound conservation practices. After a fishery in-
spection system has been established and after conservation methods
have improved, expansdon of the present fishing area will be favorably
considered by Headquarters.

the fishing area should not for international political reasons
be.delayed indefinitely, but Natural Resources Section experts
are convinced that expansion should not be authorized until the
above measures have been taken.

Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch Mo.
317 dated May 16, 1949, from United
States Political Adviser for Japan,
Tokyo, subject: "Expansion of
Japanese Fishing Area."
Nippon Times, May 12, 1949:
COPY
JAPANESE FISHERMEN WARNED OF INVADING AMERICAN AREAS
A stern warning was given Japanese fishermen that any attmpt
by them to invade the fishing areas off the coasts of North America
would be aggressively resisted."
This warning was given at the press conference yesterday by the
three-man mission of the fishing experts from the United States con-
cluding a study of the fishing situation here. The three were in-
vited by Gen. Douglas MacArthur, and they are expected to submit
their recommendations to him and the Army Department in Washington,
D.C。
The group composed of Ward W. Allen, of Seattle, Wash., Donald
T. Loker of Los Angeles, and Frederick M. Bundy of Gloucester,
Massachusetts, all three who are with the Fisheries Division,
Natural Resources Section, said that the Japanese fishermen do not
appear to be following the regulations set down by General MacArthur.
"This matter is causing him some concern," they said, "and he feels
as we do that before any other matter could be considered, Japanese
fishermen must live up to those regulations."
The group pointed out that the "MacArthur line" must be "scru-
pulously" observed if the Japanese fishermen are to regain the good
opinion of other nations.
"We believe," they said, "that the Japanese Government should
patrol the lines and that SCAP personnel should evaluate the patrol
reports and exercise surveillance over the patrolling."
As to the question of extending the present Japanese fishing
territory, that will depend, they said, in great part to the manner
in which the Japanese fishermen control their fishing within the
lines laid down by SCAP.
The visiting consultants described the technique of Japanese
fishermen as "excellent."
Mr. Allen and Mr. Bundy are leaving for the United States today
while Mr. Loker expects to depart on May 17. (Radiopress)

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
MEMORANDUM
Tokyo, January 22, 1949.
The Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters, Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers, presents its compliments to the
Philippine Mission in Japan and has the honor to refer to a
conversation on January 18, 1949, between Dr. Bernabe AFRICA,
Chief of the Mission, and representatives of this Headquarters.
Enclosed herewith is a map, as requested by Dr. Africa,
showing the present area in which the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers has authorized Japanese fishing, and showing that
portion of the proposed extension of the authorized area which
approaches nearest the islands off Luzon. The boundary of the
proposed extension would proceed from a point on the present
authorized boundary (123 degrees east longitude and 24 degrees
north latitude) south to a point at 123 degrees east longitude
and 20 degrees north latitude; thence east to a point at 130
degrees east longitude and 20 degrees north latitude; thence
south to a point at 130 degrees east longitude and 16 degrees
north latitude; and thence eastward.
The Mission's good offices in obtaining and forwarding the
views of the Philippine Government on the proposed extension would
be appreciated.

existing Japanese fishing grounds without regard to possible depletion.

disregard al' considerations of conservation. It is recommended that
the peragraph be changed by deleting the word "possible" in line 4 and

country to any expansion even though the country has exerted an interest
in an area far distant from its shores. It is recommended, therefore.

"asserted = direct concern in respect to fishing
or other commercial aquatic activities, SCAP will at-
tempt to secure consent of such country to an agree-

RESTRICTED
The United States agrees to the broad general
principle that each member nation of the Far Eastern
Commission may define (on a reasonable basis) an area
in the high seas within which it has a direct concern,
for security reasons or for the enforcement of legiti-
mate conservation measures, with respect to the entry
into that area of Japanese vessels for fishing or other
commercial aquatic activitiee.
F.W.
United States acceptance of this broad general
principle is, however, on the assumption that it will
be applied within the rule of reason, and that no state
will claim a direct concern over an area which would
not be generally accepted as reasonable. In most cases
of proposed expansion of fishing areas consultations
1
between SCAP and the interested nation would result in
9
the prior authorization provided for in the policy
paper for the expansion of authorized fishing areas.
-11
Obviously, however, it would be possible for a nation
to insist on a direct concern over en area which could
7
have no possible relationship to national security or
to legitimate conservation measures such as the entire
Pacific Ocean, for instance, or over an area up to
1,000 miles from its coasts. In such an unthinkable,
15
but yet possible, situation, if SCAP could not through
negotiation secure a modification of the position taken
by
RESTRICTED

=== Page 504 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
-2-
by the member nation, he would be forced to exeroise
the discretion implied in paragraph 2b of the polioy
decisions, with reference to the extension of Japanese
fiehing into such part of the area claimed as could
have no reasonable relation to that nation's national
security or to legitimate conservation interests. As
a practical matter such a situation would probably not
arise.
If the Sunreme Commander for the Allied Powers in
the exercise of such discretion prejudices what the FEC
member country continues to regard as its own direct
concern, it is recognized that each country has the right
in accordance with the terms of reference of the FEC to
request review of the action taken by the Supreme Commander
or that it may resort to diplomatic approaches to other
FEC member countries for a general consideration of the
question.

SUBJECT:
Discussion with Mr. West on FEC Japenese Fishing Paper.
The final results of the discussion with Mr. West and Col. Hendrick were:
1. The views of SCAP should be requested on paragraph 2b (area of
"direct concern" matter). State would draft the cable to be cleared
894.628/6-1049
with Army and which would present both sides of the argument as well as

2. Definite agreement was reached that paragraph 5 (reparations
matter) should be eliminated even if it should be necessary to veto
the paper.
I am instructing Lr. Blakeslee to press for the elimination of paragraph 5
in the Steering Committee indicating if necessary that we will veto the paper
if it is not removed.

Attached for your signature (Tab A) 1s a memorandum for

to SC-271/19, Fishing and Aquatic Industries 1n Japan. This
FW.
paper has had a long and controversial histony, and in its
amended form has the approval of the Department. It 1s now
894.628/6-1049
before the Steering Committee and it is expeeted that it will
soon go forward to the Commission.
After extensive consideration of thepaper by the FEC
Departmental group, which included discussions with the Army
regarding our pronosed position, we instructed our representa-
tive on the Steering Committee last April to support the paper
provided that the last sentence in paragraph 2b could beelimina-
ted. This sentence stated "In cases where no agreement is
reached within a reasonable period, SCAP shall refer the matter
to the FEC." We also instructed our representative on the
Steering Committee to enter into the record a carefully worded
statement of our understanding of the second sentence in
paragranh 2b which sentence reads "If such expansion will
involve an area in which any member country of the FEC has a
direct concern in respect to fishing or other commercial 6049
aquatic activities, prior authorization shall be obtained by
SCAP from such country." This statement (Tab B) was modified
extensively to meet the wishes of certain officers in/the
Civil Affairs Division. Our statement and position with
respect to the paper as a whole was accepted by the other
members of the Committee as a firm poeition and several of
the other members have received instructions from their govern-
ments to support the paper based upon our position.
6-1049
NA has now received a letter (Tab C) dated June 1o from
Colonel Porter, Chief of the Far Eastern Affairs Group, CAD,
X

approval of
the Army proposals for paragraph l, that we feel that we can-

2

The Army is particularly interested in deletion of paragraph

"Nothing in the foregoing should be interpreted to
prevent removal as reparations of Japanese vessels,
equipment or other facilities used for fishing or other
commercial aquatic activities, nor to predetermine the
ultimate constitution and size of the Japanese fishing
fleet."
Mr. West has personally indicated an interest in having this
paragraph deleted as the Army feels that its inclusion in a
policy decision could be construed as a modification of our
announced reparations policy and that it would serve to
alarm and discourage Japanese investors. I am inclined to
agree that some confusion might arise if the United States
were to approve this statement at this time, although from a
legal or technical standpoint the statement seems harmless.
Dr. Blakeslee is of the opinion that it would be undignified
for us to modify our original position which included support
of this paragraph. However, I feel that we are justified in
instructing our delegation to seek its deletion as our original

was mede prior to our announcement of our position regarding
reparations removals and level of industry. I do not feel
that we should go so far as to veto the paner if we are not
successful in obtaining deletion of this paragraph. Conse-
quently it is proposed that if the U.S. delegation is
uneuccessful in obtaining its deletion, General McCoy should
make a statement for the record before the FEC as indicated
in Tab A.

Colonel Forter and hold your instruction to General McCoy
until Colonel Porter informs us of the views of the Army
Department. I shall at the same time telephone Mr. West in
an effort to otain his agreement.

I have the lonor to refer to this Mission's airgram no, 116
of May 12, 1949, and to previous correspondence concerning authoriza-
tion and publicity of plans for a Japanese Antarctic whaling ex-
pedition for the coming season and to enclose a copy of a communica-
tion dated June 7, 1949, prepared by the Natural Resources Section,
General Headquarters, proposing that a 1949-1950 expedition be
formally authorized.
The enclosed comnunication is addressed to the Chief of Staff
and states that it is desirable to issue a formal directive to the
Japanese Governnent as soon as possible authorizing Antarctic whaling
for the coning season in order that preparations may be undertaken.
Included as a subenclosure is a draft SCAP directive to the Japanese
Government auth orizing an expedition of two fleets to operate on
substantially the same basis as last year's expedition.
This Mission, in its capacity as the Diplomatic Section of Gen-
eral Headquarters, has concurred in the enclosed proposal by the
Natural Resource: Section. It is anticipat ed that General Headquarters
will approve the proposal, but the late upon which the formal authoriza-
tion ill be issued to the Japanese Government remains uncertain.

imperative that extensive repairs be lade to three major units of

3,000,000,000 from commercial banks to be used as operating

fron SCAP. Commercial banks ani shipyards are reluctant to conclude
negotiations involving such large sums of money without such official
authorization. The issuing of a directive to the Japanese Government
will provide a sound ba is for these negotiations.

be issued at the earliest propitious moment, preferably not later
than 10 June 1949, in order that preparat ions for the 1949-50 ex-
pedition may be initiated.

RES TRICTED
WILDLIFE
JUN 24
ACTION
U/TW ENC
Subject: Report on Japanese Fishing Industry Prepared by Visiting
INFO
American Experts.
FR
DCR
894.628/6-2049
FE ENC
ITP
OLI
The Honorable
Secretary of State.
L
Washington.
Sir8
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch no. 317 of
May 16, 1949, forwarding a press report of an interview with three
American experts who visited Japan at the invitation of the Supreme
Commander to inspect the Japanese fisheries industry. In this con-
nection, there are enclosed three copies of the Natural Resources
Section Weekly Summary No. 189, on pages 8 to 19 of which appears a
report prepared by these experts and submitted to General Headquarters.
The report of the American experts strongly endorses the measures
being taken or proposed by the Natural Resources Section for control
of Japanese fishing operations. It recommends close liaison. between
Headquarters and the Japanese Fisheries Agency as well as increase of
Headquarters personnel engaged in this work and elevation of the Japa-
nese Fisheries Agency to the status of a ministry. The experts also
recommend that a fisheries patrol be operated by the Japanese Govern-
ment. They express their opposition to coastal fisheries operations
by the Japanese off the coast of any of the Allied Powers and they
recommend that at the time a treaty of peace is negotiated with Japan.
pernanent fishery treaties also be negotiated. The report endorses
Headquarterst efforts to democratize the Japanese fishing industry
and also criticises pre-war Japanese fishing methods and the present
low quality Japanese fisheries research. The experts state that the
Supreme Commander was justified in permitting Japan to resume whaling
operations.
The enolosed report praises highly the work of the Natural Resources
Section and indicates clearly that the visiting experts were thoroughly
impressed with the work of General Headquarters in the field of Japa-
nese fisheries.

Miyagi, Fukushima, Ibaraki, Chiba, Ishikawa, and Nagasaki.
b. The distribution to be made falls into three categories:

bonus for rice end rice straw deliveries in Hokkaido, Aomori, Iwate, Miyagi,
Fukushima, Akita, Yamagata, Niigata, Toyama, Ishikawe, Fukui, Shiga, and
Shizuoka prefectures.
(2) The Feed Distribution Koden will distribute fish meal
throughout Japan as a food for animals.
(3) Temporarily the Feed Distribution Kodan, through their
dealers, also will distributc fish meal to orchards growing fruit for ex-
port and to companies wishing to use it as a raw material for making mixed
fertilizor. Such fertilizcr-will contain a mixture of fish meal and bittern
obtained from the cvaporation of sea water.
c. Formerly, the distribution of fish meal as fertilizcr was con-

1. Annual Roport on Agricultural Resoarch and Extension System
a. In compliance with tho request of the Food and Agriculturo
Organization.of thc United Nations for data on agricultural research and
extension in Japan, Natural Resrurccs Section prepared the following state-
ment:
Logislation cnacted by the Japanose Diet in July 1948 providod
for (1) the logal basis for national government aid to the prefecturos
for support of a coordinatcd agricultural rescarch and extension systom,
and (2) the establishment of an Agricultural Improvoment Bureau in the
Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry to administer the national experiment
stations and to coordinato agricultural rescarch and extension programs
throughout Japan.
Pursuant to this legislation, the Agriculturel Improvement Bureeu
was cstablished 6 August 1948. The Bureau conpists of (1) the Technical
Research Division, (2) thc Economic Research Division, and (3) the Extension
Division.
Plans for consolidating the 378 national and prcfectural agricultural
experimont stations into approximatcly onc-half as meny cstablishmente were
completed by the Agricultural Improvement Bureau 27 December 1948
Porsonnel, essential equipment, and funds of the stations to be closed will
be used to improvc the remnining stations to provide facilities for an over-
all increase in offective agricultural research work. 'The Bureau is making
-5-

=== Page 539 === (confidence 0.98)
plans for a long-range, nationwide agricultural research program for Japan
into which the projects of each station will be designed to fit.
Since the establishment of the Agricultural Improvement Bureau in
August 1948, all but four of the 46 prefectures have formed prefectural
agricultural improvement committces. A director of agricultural improv-
ment has been appointed in 31 prefectures, while prefectural laws establish-
ing agricultural extcnsion systems have been enacted in 26 prefeotures. The
remaining prefecturcs, all of which have initiated oxtension systems under
governmental regulations, are expected to enact legislation incorporating
provisions suggcsted in a model law furnished by the Ministry of Agriculturo
and Forestry. Local cxtension committes have been formed in 2,709 rural
communitics throughout Japan.. Qualifying oxaminations for candidates for
positions in the new extereion eystem have been held in all profecturos, and
5,299 local farm adviscrs, including 1ll women as farm homo advisers, have
bcen selectcd and assigned to their areas. In addition, 682 prefectural
extension staff members have been appointed.
The new agricultural extension system in Japan is designed to
convert thc results of agricultural rcsearch into farm practices as rapidly
as possible, frcc from the regulatory controls and police powers formorly
associated with tho dutics of agricultural trchnicians. Although the agri-
cultural cxtcnsion staff is still limited, plans havo been made to reach
the maximum mumbor of farmers with usoful tcchnical information on agricultural
problems. Tho new Japanese agricultural extension system has outlined pro-
grams for (1) development of farmer discussion groups; (2) organization of
rural youth project clubs; (3) demonstratione on improvement of farm living
conditions; (4) demonstratione of improved crop cultural methods; (5) demon-
strations of improved crop varictics; (6) control of seed-borne diseascs of
all crops; (7) domonstration on control of insect pests; (8) selections of
diseaso-free white potatces for seed; (9) farm storage of sweet potatoes;
(10) utilization of limited fertilizer supplies; and (1l) demonstrations on.
control of soil crosion.

FISHERIES. DIVISION
GENERAL
1. Special Missiod Roviewe Japanose Fisheries Situation
a. A special fisheriés mission invited by Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powe-s to study the fisheries industry has completed its
assignment and returned to the United States. The mission was composed
of: Edward W. Allen of Seattle, United States Commissioner, International
Fisheries Commission; Frederick M. Bundy of Gloucester, Massachusetts,
and Donald P. Locker of Terminal Island, California, both of them execu-
tives of fishing and fish processing companies.
b. Prior to departure, the mission prepared and submitted the
following report based on its study and observation of Japanese fisheries
and fishing industry:
1. Preliminary
a. Because of the shortness of time allotted to the review, an
explanation would appear to be desirable for the assertion that the mem-
bers of the mission consider their opportunity to reach conclusions ade-
quate, except where the contrary is expressed for the following reasons:
(1) All members of the mission have had long experience in
fishery. matters.
(2)
All haye had contact with the international aspects of
fisheries.
(3)
All have had more cr less familiarity with the Japanese
situation in general and had given it some study before
departing for Japan.
(4)
The members were promptly well briefed by the Natural
Resources Section'executives.
(5)
Intensive field work was proviced which not only
afforded observation of various actual fishery opera-
tions and inspection of boats, harbors, markets, pro-
cessing and storage facilities, along a substantial
port on of the Japanese coast, but also numerous dis-
cussions with fishermen, boat captains, fishery associa-
tion officials, Military Government personnel, Japa-
nose Netional Government Fisheries Agency officials
and of course, representatives of the pertinent divi-
sions of SCAP.
(6) The Schedule was arranged so as to afford a final
period for the members to assemble and analyze their
data.

=== Page 542 === (confidence 0.98)
2. oojootives
a. The membere of the. mission have carefully considered the ob-
jectives pf SCAP to provide maximum immediate indigenous production and to
promote a sound pcrmanent Japanese oconomy. Thoy appreciate the hecossity
for securing the greatest production consistent with continuod maximum anmual
yicld. They also rocognize the necessity for SCAP to substitute a wholesome
democratic attitude on the part of the Japanese populace for the historical
feudal subservience. They are sympathetic toward these objectives and hope
that their comments and suggestions may be of such assistance that the steps
taken tovard them will be reconciled with major allied nation opinion.
b. The Potsdam Proclamation pormitted Japan "to maintain such
industrics as will sustain her economy----------. To this end, access to,
as distinguished from control of, raw materials shall be permitted."
c. 'SCAP, wc understand, has given this language tho rational con-
struction that it means, to permit the Japanese people to reinstato them-
selves in peaceful industrial activitics, but that it does not moan to pormit
the Japanese to reinstate the ruthless disregard for the rights and interosts
of others which for many ycars characterized the conduct of the industrial
and military despots, who soized arbitrary control over the economy of Japan.
d. There is probably no industry which impinges upon international
relations so directly as does that of fishcries. A can of fish upon a grocer!s
shelf is little different commeroially from a can of corn. But there is a
vast dif erencc betvcen seven vessols flying seven different national flags,
contending in the same ocean for the same fish, and that of two friendly
Illinois farmers with their adjcining ficlds of growing corn. . Fishery com-
petition was responsiblc for war after war betrcen the Dutch and British.
Wo know of no war over growing corn.
e. In thc attempt to restore Japanese ocean fisheries, therefore,
not only international law but international ethics must bo considered. Con-
tempt shown by tho Japanese toward other fishery nations contributed greatly
tovard the bitterness which still persists in many quarters. The endeavor
of SCAP to alleviate this bittcrness by directing the Japanese ocean fisherics
to be conducted upon sound oonservation lines and recognize the legitimate
interests and rights of other nations should be helpful to both the Japanese
and the Allicd Powers.
f. It appears desirable, thorcfore, to present a brief review of
world fishery conditions and trends before considering the Japanese fisherics
specifically.
g. Estimates of world fishery production vary from thirty to forty
billion pounds, consumed chiefly as wholesome protein food, rich in vitamin
and other essential food elements. The Japanese are above the average in
fish consumption, so that an adequate supply of fish, particularly in view

=== Page 543 === (confidence 0.98)
of their other food shortages is of vital importance to them. Nevertheless
Japan is only one of many fish consuming nations. With.population rapidly
incroasing in other parts of the world as weil as in Japan,  with the limita-
tions upon increased agricultural food production becoming daily more critloal,,
the problem of the maximum utilization and rational distribution of fish is
of accelerating importance to every nation. The consid. ration of Japanese..
fisherics, thercfore, can not be isolated from the world fishery piotire.
3. World Fishery Situation
a. Although fishermon have crossed the Atlantic to the Grand Banks
for centurics, fisheries were largely local. And, though there were local
fish shortages from time to time, there was no apprehension that the supply
of fish,could be exhausted:
b. With the advent of modern propulsion, processing and refrigera-
tion, a wholly different condition prevailed. It becamo physically possible
for any nation to posscss vessels able to fish at any distance and roturn
with an edible product. It became possible for the fishing vesscls of.one
cr scveral naticns to concentrate upon certain ocean fisheries with such in-
tensity'es to exhaust them commercinlly and cven to exterminate them.
c. Yet until the last few years the principles of law generally
considered applicable to ocean fisheries were those developed in the light
of fishery. conditions as they cxisted. three hundred yeara ago. A nation
"owned" its territorial waters whether the width was recognized as three
miles, four or.any other distance; therofore, it owned the fish in them as
Iong es thoy staycd in them. Anywhere clse in the ocenn anybody could
fish without limit and in any manner he saw fit.
d. Recent appreciation of the danger of such unrostricted fishing
resulted.in two movements, (1) for fishery ccnservation, (2) for reconsidera-
tion of rights and duties pertaining to ocean fisheries.
e.: Since the fishing industry has bccn subjected to auch a complete
revolution within recent decades, wholly new legal and operational concepts
are absolutely esscntial.
f. Fishery ccnservation is still in its infancy, but great progrcss
has been made. Greatest of all is the conclusive recogniticn that certain
fisheries. can be exhausted or extcrminated and that by proper managemont somo
depleted fisherias can bc restored end maintained at a permanently high level
of annual productivity. Many nations have successfully applied these prin-
ciples to domcstic fisheries. Canada and the United States have led in
successfully applying jcint international management to jrint fishorics
(halibut and salmon). International agrcements have bcen applicd, though
without joint managoment, to whaling and fur scals, and rapid progress ia
being made in the nogotiation.of bilateral and multilatcral conservation
treaties. A subsidiary of the United Nations, the Food and Agriculture Organ-
ization, is devoting much attention. to fishery utilization and oanservetion.
-10-

=== Page 544 === (confidence 0.99)
g. A corollary to thc recognition for the necessity of conserva-
tion is that fishery managemont in order to be successful must be based
upon factual knowledge. There are local cases in which & certain degree
of success is based upon generations of fishing experionce. Japaneso fishery
associations supply notable examples. But generally, certainly as to migra-
tory fish, accurate scientific research is a prerequisite to successful
management.
h. It should be noted, however, that all scientific fishery researgh
docs not promote conservation. Such research may be directed wholly toward
finding the most effective places and mcans to exploit fisheries or to locat
new fisherios to exploit. This may have merit as to unutilized stocks but
the Japancse Government was the most glaring example of its misuse, encoura-
ging maximum exploitation with no thought for the maintenance of a future
supply.
i. World War I stimulated interest in ocean fisheries as a source
for needed food elements. The Portugucse have long contended that the thre
mile rule" (that a naticn owns the strip of water three miles wide off its
coast, no more no less, and that its fishery control is limited to that
strip) was unsound. But it was the Japenesc invasion of the highly concen-
trated, valuable and casily depleted red salmon fishery of Bristol Bay,
Alaska, in the 1930's, which precipitated international reaction. Tho United
States Government had gone to great expense in research on this fishery. It
had imposed rigcrous ccntrol over its fishermen and operators in the attempt
to maintain a permanont maximum arnual yield. Japanese floating canneries
operating with floating gill nets two miles or more in length were effectively
cutting off tho strcam of salmon hecded for the Bristol Bay rivers to spawn.
Although the vessels operated ten, twenty or more miles offshore, the inter-
ception of these fish wes such that it threatencd the exterminetion of the
stock.
j. Tho United States made strong representations to Japan asserting
special interest in these fish which wore spawned inland, remained there part
of their lives, went to sea for a period, then returned to their parent stroams
to spawn and die. Without waiving its contentions based upon the antiquated
concepts of fnternational law, Japan agreed for the time being to-discontinue
this fishery practice. Although this opisode related to a single and remote
fishery it is believed to mark the beginning of a new epoch in concepts of
occan fishery rights and limitations.
k. United Statcs fiehermen generally wcre aroused. Pressure upon
their government, stimulated by the Japanese participetion in World War II,
clicited the sc-called Truman Prcclemation of September 28, 1945, which offi-
cially asserted a spccial interest by a naticn in its coastal fisherics re-
gardless of whether they cxtended beyond territorial waters, and also its
right to protect them. Althcugh the accompanying pross release disclosed
that Alaska salmon were dcfinitcly in mind, the proclamation showed full
appreciaticn of the tuna and other fisherics in which our fishcrmen operated
off the shores of other nations. The whole program was based upen the
ethical concept of reciprocal rights and the United States as a coastal nation
was proposing nothing which it was unwilling to grant to cther coastal netions
under similar conditions.

=== Page 545 === (confidence 0.98)
1j Moxico, Argentina, Peru and other nations were stirred into
action, some of it sound, some of it questionable. This review shows:that
the old idea that any nation has the right to deplete or destroy the coastal
fisheries:of another natirn, simply by keeping its operations three miles.
-off-shore, is now obsolete. It is imperative that tris be borne in mind ip
the consideration of present and future Japanese fishing.
4. Japanese Fisherics
a. The importance of fisheries as an industry, and fish as a food,
in Japan, is unchallenged. It is estimated that more than a million people
engage in fishing, that there are more than 400, 000 fishing boats of all
types and that the present annual production exceeds six billicn pounds.
b. Contrary to popular impressicn, the Jepanese coastal fishery
produces far more fish than the deep sea fishery and gives employment to the
great majority of the fishermen. When it comes to food supply, it is esson-
tial to appreciate the importance of these individual small operations. In
our opinion SCAP has done this and, with comprehensive assistance in provid-
ing gear and petroleum, has made a real contributien to the food supply of
Japan and has relieved the Amcrican taxpayer.
c.  The subject of fisher rights is almost too còmplicated even
for cxpcrts to explain, but it is sufficient to say that different groups
or communitics fish certain definitc areas and are exceedingly jealous of
their rights. There are said to bc some 3,O00 fishery associations. These
are mostly on a community basis although the Japanese military, when in con-
trol, forced thom into prefectural organizations and then again into a na-
tional organization for purposes of morc effectual regimentation. Under a
now law thesc associations are bcing changed into so-callcd cooperatives
which it is anticipated will reach 6,00o in number. An important featuro is
that in those groups, each fisherman retains his individuality. They are
coopcrative in a very practical sense, and inherently democratic.
d. With a few notable exccptions, such fishery conservation as
has existed in Japan has been confined to these local associations which
have placed practical limitation, seascnal and quantitative, upon thoir own
memborship in order to perpetuatc the strictly local fisheries.
c. It was in tho field of deep see fisheries, however, that the
Japanesc aroused international antagonisms by their ruthless mothods of ox-
ploitation and contempt for thc rights and interests of others. This an-
tegonism continues to exist. The mission hopes this fact will bo driven
homc to Japanese Government officials and to all fishermen and fish company
executives. Those few officials who appear to rcccgnize the situation de-
serve credit and encouragement, but numerous interviews disclosed either.
ignorance of the situation or a deceptive attempt to ignore it on the part
of many Japenese who were intelligont cncugh to knov better. Until the
Japanose fishing industry faces these cold facts, it can expect little
sympathy from the public of other nations.

f.In:this connection it is pertinont to discuss the Japanese
petitions for extension of the "MacArthur Line." The mission does not feel
it has sufficient information to chart biologically sound Paciric Ocean
fishery boundary lines, It has the impression that, ir these were the only
considerations, there might be historical and biological merit in 'Japan's re-
quest for extension of the westerly boundary line, particularly in the Chine
Sea. But when such requests are concurrent with contomptuous dierogard of
the line as now fixed, the mission feels that the position of Scap in de-

g. Immedietely after the war there was such pressure to increase
the food supply that the trawling fleet in the Shimonoseki-Nagasaki area was
rebuilt rapidly, and to a level beyond that necessary to fish the authorised
aroas. This overconstruction was unwisely approved by SCAP and tho cost
financed largely through Japanese Government loans. In the light of later
developments the overbuilding became clear but proposals to reduce the fleet
to rational proportions appear to be making slight progress. . The associations
proposal that it "may" reduce by 20% fails to indicate any gemuine offort to
meet the situation. In the event the Japanese Government does not take of-
fective action SCAP might meet the situation to some extont by control over
oil and other supplies. The Japanese Government might supplement the effec-
tivenoss of patrols by legislation limiting the number of boats or the poundage
of the catch. The legal price of fish might also be reduced, so less ef-
ficient operators will be gradually eliminated.
h. Real aggressive biological investigations in the Pagific are
now under way. Foremost is the United States, Fish and Wildlife Service pro-
gram under the Farrington Bill. Headquarters are being established at
Honolulu; thrce research ships are being put into operation; a staff is
being organized, headed by competent fishery scientists. An investigation
is already collecting informatiof in Japan. The Celifornia tuna industry
is cooperating with the State government in the study of albacore off the
California coast and has pledged industry funds for research on tuna. The
Pacific Marino Fishcrics Commission, under a state compact between Washington,
Oregon, and Galifornia vpted to give albacore its first attention. Canada is
undortaking simiier work off the British Columbia coast and the United States
Fish and Wildlife Service has been conducting tuna investigations around the
Philippine Islands. It is essential that Japanese investigations be coor-
dinated with the research work being done by these other countries.
i. With such research concentration it is reasonable to expect that
progress will be forthcoming. It seems unsound to expand:Japanese tima fishery
areas at this time with the possible exception of part of the albacore:grcunds
northwest of Midway. The Japanese were fishing in this area prior to the war
and it is not being fully utilized at present.
j. It is hardly conceivable that anyone would seriously consider
allowing the Japanese again to invade the Alaska salmon or halibut: fisheries.

p. These cbscrvations lead to the question of fishery research in

v.. Japanese fiahery people apparently hope that with a peace
treaty, all fishery limitations will be off. This does not follow. When
Japan made her two peece treaties with Russia she exacted very definite and
substantial fishery concessions. It is not expected that the United States
will take an equally selfish position, but it would seem to be misplaced
charity not to require in the peace treaty or in tresties thet are concurrent
and not subsequent that Japan bind itself permanently to sound fishery poli-
cies in the deep sea or international field, It is noteworthy that although
the fishery interests expect to be turned loose upon the world without re-
striction by the peace treaty, the members of the Diet with whom the mission
discussed the matter, appeared to take it for granted that tho treaty would
incorporate restrictions.
w. Just what these policies should be is a matter for careful con-
sideration by the United States fishery industry and Department of Stato.
They should be fair to Japan on the one hand and require Japan to be fair
to other nations on the other. Unlimited commitment to principles of the
"Truman Proclamaticn" has been suggested. Another suggestion is that an
arrangement be concluded whereby Japan keep its fishermen 100 miles from
the Amcrican coest and the. American nations keep their fishcrmcn a liko dis-
tance: from Japan. In any event there should be no peace treaty without a
permanent protection against Japan returning to her former unrestricted
methods of fishing off the shores of othor nations.
x.. In this same connecticn Japan should be compellod to become a
party of the. international whaling convention and to some form of fur seal
treaty. It was interesting to note that the officials of the large whaling
companies exprossod complcte willingness to have Japan become a party to the
convention. It should also be notcd in this connoction that these companies
vished to reacquire the same proportion of the antarctic whaling production
that they had when at the top level of their unrestricted whaling which was
a disporportionate share of the world catch.

a. Work of the mission falls within the scope of the Natural

to interview whomever it wished to intcrview. The facilities of the Division
have bcen frecly open for the sccuring of desired data.
b. Certain pclicies deserve special considcration. Tho mission
is thoroughly convinced of the scundncss of the position that the Japanese
should not be permitted to expand their deep sea fishing cperations, under
any circumstances, until the Japanese Government demonstratcs its ability
to control its fishermen and to respect international obligations. It is
felt to be of utmost importance to the entire world fishery future that the
sound position which SCAP has taken in this matter be maintained.

6. Conclusicns
In view of the foregoing review we conclude:
a. We commend SCAP upon the cxcellent leadership of the Natural
Resources Section and its Fisheries Division. We hope that this quality
will be maintained regardless of changes in personnel.

continued as long as they ere furnished by the United States.
d. We believe the policy of close liaison between SCAP and the
Japanese Fisheries Agency should be continued and thet SCAP should support

ECONOMICE
1. Remuneration System Used in Japanese Fishing Industry.
a. Some of the remuneration systems used in the Japanese fishing
industry date back more then 300 years. Five of the principal Japanese 
fisheries are used in Tables 1-3 and the figure on page 23 to show how the
owners of fishing boats and gear share profits and expenses with fishermen.
Some fisheries have no basic minimum vage, and fishermen' receive a portion'
of the net income on a percentage basis. In other fisheries they receive a
basic minimum vage in addition to a share of the net income.
b. In most cases the general expenses of the fishing trip are
deducted from the sale value of the fish catch. The remainder then is divided
between the management (owner or operator) and labor (fishermen). The owner,
from his share, pays such expenses as boat repairs or major repairs to the gear,
depreciation, and other standard maintenance expenses. Fishermen are paid
according to a "labor unit" system by which certain fisherics have a total
number of units based upon the number in the crew and unit allowances for
special qualifications and degrees of experience and/or schooling.
. Definitions of terms used within the tables illustrating the.
vage syetems follom:
TERMS USED IN WAGE SYSTEM TABLES
Term
Definition
Gross income
Income realized from sale of fish catch; ie,
total value of fish catch.
Net income
Total income minus general expenses only.
Expenses directly incurred on fishing trips;
General expenses
eg, fuel, provisions at sea, bait, ice,
fish crates, nets, etc.
(Cont'd)

necessarily equal to number of crew, be-
cause individuals such as fishing fore-
man, master, and engineer are each superior
to one unit and receive additional shares,
while those inferior to one unit receive
less than one share.

MINING AND GEOLOGY DIVISION
MINING AND MILLING
1. Mining Industry Monthly Reviev March 1949
a. Production data for March 1949 show an increase in 19 out of
25 commodities reported. Pyrites production increased nine percent. Among
the metals, lead, mercury, end tin made notevorthy gains. Among the non-
metals, limestone production increased from 554,531 metric tons in February
to 706,286 metric tons in Merch, a gain of 21 percent. Mineral and metal
mine production and stocks for March are shown in table on page 37.
METALI.URGY
1. Metal Smelting and Refining Industry Monthly Review. April 1949
a. Production: Iron production for April 1949 increased four
percent over that for March owing to the return to operation of a 3o0-ton
blast furnace at the Yawatn works, Japen Iron and Steel Co. April pro-
duction of all types of steel remained practically the same as for March.
Ferroalloy production irc eese? apprecjably during April owing to a peak
supply of electric power and the receipt of high-grade manganese ore im-
porte.
b. Stocks: Iron stocks for March 1949 increased slightly, and
steel stocks decreesed casiderably compared with those for February, while
ferroalloy stocks remained cbout the sane.
c. Production of the mejor nonferrous metals in April 1949 was
about the same as in March 1949. The percent of incrcase in April stoeks
of refined copper, refined lead, and zinc over those for March 1949 are
81.5, 48, and 5.5, res svivel.
d. Production and stocks of iron and steel, metal, and ferro-
alloys are shown in taclcs on pages 38-41.
CO4L
1. Report on Fire at Moiiri Coal Mine, Hokkaido
a. Mr K. Hiromatsu, managing director, Yubctsu Mining Co, re-
ported on the fire hich destroyed the tipple and coal preparation plant
at tho Mojiri coel mine, Hokkaidc, 25 April 1949. The fire was of un-
7.37.一

=== Page 565 === (confidence 0.99)
known origin and is still being investigated. Total loss is estimated at
¥117,000,000, including ¥60,000,000 owing to a loss of operation of dress-
ing plant until August 1949. Insurance covers ¥31,500,000.
b. Arrangements have been made with the Coal Kodan to ship the
unscreened coal as seventh-class boiler coal; formerly the Mojiri coal has
been sold as first-class coking coal. Daily production of 7o0 metric tons
per day is continuing under this arrangement. The Mojiri mine produced
more than 20,000 metric tons of coking coal in Mareh 1949. If necessary
funds and equipment can be obtained, the coal preparation plant should be
ready to resume operations in August.
2. New Mino Development at the Shimane Hokoku Coal Mine. Shimane
Prefecturo
a. A representative of the Shimane coal mine, Shimane Prefecture,
reported on the open-cut development at the mine. This mine is one of the
18 approved new mines which are being given special assistance by the Coal
Board. Owing to the curtailment of development funds, the amount of equip-
ment to be used has been reduced. Technical advice was given on the use of
a Bagloy-type scraper designed to remove surface overburden.
3. Organization of Mino Safety Bureau
a. Mr S. Kondo, chief of the Secretariat of tho former Ministry
of Commerce and Industry, reported on tho personnel of the newly created
Minc Safety Bureau. This bureau was crcated to carry out the provisions
of the Mine Safety Bill prcmulgated 16 May 1949 (Wcekly Summary No 188).
b. Mr H. Okada, presently chief of General Affairs Section,
Tokyo Chamber of Commerce and Industry, was selected to head the Mine Safety
Bureau. Mr Okeda has worked in government coal agencies for soven years and
at one time was a represcntative to the International Labor Orgenization.
Mr S. Araki, now essistent chief, Safety Soction, will head the Coal Mine
Safety Branch of the bureau, and Mr S. Koizumi will head the Metal Mine
Branch. These changes became effectivo 25 May 1949, on which date the
Ministry of International Trade and Industry roplaced the former Ministry of
Commerce and Industry.

2. Field Investigation of Oil Field Drilling and Production Problems
a. Natural Resources Section personnel inspected pil well drill-
ing, completion, and production problems in Yabase oil field, Akita Pre-
fecture.
b. Status of wells currontly drilling in Akita Prefecture is as
followss
(1) Sotoasahikawa R-7: Present depth of this well is 1,256
meters. Because there was no core recovery, the well will be Schlumbergered
to determine whether it has passed through the VIII horizon. Strong showings
of gas and oil were observed in the ditch while drilling in the basal part
of VIII horizon. A shortage of barite, which is needed to condition the gas-
cut mud, is delaying completion of this well.
(2) Koya R-34: A 13 3/8-inch surface casing was cemented at
125 meters, with 225 sacks of cement and 15 kilograms per square eentimeter
maximum pressure.
-34-

=== Page 568 === (confidence 0.99)
(3). Koya R-33: This well was listed as a produder on 7 Mays
the initial daily production rate was 20.4 kiloliters of oil and 2,548
cubic meters of gas through a 1/4-inch choke.
(4) Koya R-9 (workover): Operations have been suspended
until gas lift equipment can be procured.
(5) Omonogawa R-41: (Location A-2) The IV,horizon was gup-
perforated with 42 eight-millimeter bullets. The lon production rate of
0.35 kiloliter of oil daily from this well has necessitated reconsidering
the drilling of locations C-2, D-2, and E-2 which were planned to exploit
the same horizon.
(6) Yabase C-67: This well is being deepened from III horison
to b' and V horizons; 5-1/2-inch casing was set at 336 meters. Junk in the.
hole is now being milled up.

d. A water flood project of the III horizon in the Omonogawa-
Yebase district is under consideration. Plans originally suggested by
Natural Resources Section are being followed. 'Careful study is being made
of reservoir conditione vith the limited facilities available. It has been
assumed that the residual oil content of this sand is the highest of any in
-35-

=== Page 569 === (confidence 0.98)
the'field. The sand is at about 200 meters depth and consequently,: any
wells that may have to be drilled will, be cheap enough to complete. The
sand also is homogeneous, permeable, and porous and seems to be woll suited
for secondary recovory. The question of vater to be used for the flood must
be cerefully considered; the argillaceous and bentonitic character of the
sand at Yabase would seem to require a filtered:salt water. This question
is being studied, and laboratory analyses are being made by a water-flood:
committee that has been appointed for the undertaking. This projact is being
epproached comprehensively and no doubt will ultimately result in the recovery
of-a large quantity of oil that would otherwise be left in the ground. Natural.
Resources Section representatives recommended that salt water be used and a
filter system bo set up in order to have clean water for injection.
3. Qil Exploration in Tovama Prefecture
a. A representative of the Mining Bureau reported that the Toyama
Co's exploration well at Himi, Toyama Prefecture, which was located as a re-
sult of surveys instigated by Natural Resources Section, has struck a strong
gas sand and has blown out of control at 614 meters depth. The gas is said
to have e gaeolinc emell. ?is small independent company was reported to
have oxhausted its funds. They also have no barite or casing and have appealed
to the Geological Survey for aid. Natural Resources Section personnel ree-
ommended that a competent engineer be sent to Himi as soon as possible to dis-
cover the true facts. Becanse this well provides the first real indigation of
a potential gas field in Torama Prefecture, every effort should be made to
ensure that the operations are conducted properly.

Subject: Matters pertaining to the: Liquidation of the Local Nogyokai
Date: 21 May. 1949
The liouidatfon of Nogyokai, especially those Nogyokai which have re-
ceived government indemnities in accordance with the provision of Paragraph
1, Article 33 of Financial Institutions Reconstruction and Reorganization Law,
has been delayed due to the fear that problems may arise from the change in
the liquidation plan heretofore set forth. As it is intended that the govern-
ment shall solve such problems, it is requested that you will guide the liqui-
dator to expecite the business of liquidation at this time in accórdance with
the Cabinet Orders and instructicns regarding liquidation heretofore issued.
(sce annexed paper). With respect to the payment of retirement allowance to
the officials and employees of Nogyokai, there has been some change.. "Regard-
ing this matter the following principles should be followed. It is instructed
that policies, and laws and ordinances applicable to the liquidation be strictly
adhered to and that illegal actions be corrected immediately.
1. With respect to the payment of retirement allowance to officials and
employecs of the Nogyokai which cut off the total sum of capital in accordance
with provision of Item 8, Paregraph 1, Article 24 of Financial Institution
Reconstruction and Reorganization Law, there has been an amendmont to the
Notification (F. M. No. 174 8 June, 1948) based on tne 2 and 3 of Article 42
of tho same Law. The same shall bo handled as follows:
(a) Any retired official or employee of Nogyokai who has been em-
ployed by agricultural cooperative association or federation thereof which
succeeded a waole or a part, of the essets of Nogyokei up to the designated
dete by the am ndcd Notirication of the Ministry of Finance No. 271, 1949,
shall be handled as follews:
(1) The Nogyokai is not authorized to pay retirement allowance
of members in accordance with the provision of paragraph 2, item 1 of Article
42 of the Financial Institutions Reccnstruction end Reorganization Lew, re-
gerdloss of the provision, of laws and ordinances, constitution or clauses of
contract.
(2) The term of office of staffs of Nogyokai shall be regarded
as the term of office of staffs of Agricultural Cooperative Association or
Federation thereof. Retirement allowance of staffs shall be paid to them

ITEM 3. NATURAL RESOURCES SECTION WEEKLY SUMMARY NO 189
24 May 1949
No.. Nosei No. 1357
Cooperative Section
Tos
Prefectural Governors
From:
Vice-Minister pf-Agriculture &-Forestry
Subject: In Regard to:Enforcement of the Partly Revised Agricultural
Cooperative Association Law.
A law amending the. Agricultural Cooperative Association Law (Law No..72,
1949) was promulgated en May 16, 1949, effective on that date.
This legislation, being drafted. in line with the principle of the
directive issued by the Far Eastern Commission concerning farmors' organi-
zations in Japan, hes.great importance.
You are requested to take every possible step to enforce this law.
1. Matters regarding article 42-2
This article stipulates a provision prohibiting any one, who
personally carries on or engages in any activity which is in substantial
competition with a similar activity carried on by an agricultural coopera-
tive or its federation (hereinafter referred to as coop) from assuming the
post of director, auditor secretary or accountant of the coop. The follow-
ing interpretations are considered adequate;
(a) Definition of "activity carried on by a cooperative":
"Activity carried on by a cooperative" means an activity
actually run by a cooperative or the one materially decided to be carried
on'at its general membership meeting or directors' meeting; but does not
include all activities as generally provided in the articles of the coop as
activities to be carried on by the coop;
(b) Definition of "activity in substantial competition"
This means a similar activity that will or could actually
hurt economic advantage of the coop.
If the degree of disadvantago that effects or may affect
 coop is slight, it will not be considered as substantial competition.
(c) Even an activity carried on by any other coop or coop under
ir law will not make an exception to (b) for this cause.
(d) Between an activity carried on by a federation and that carried
 by a coop belonging to the federation, it is considered there exists no
competition.
4

=== Page 584 === (confidence 0.99)
ITEM 3 NATURAL RESOURCES SECTICN WEEKLY SUMMARY NO 189
(e) "Any one who personally carries on or engages in any aetivit
will be involved irrespectively of the individual or the organisation. As to
an organisation, its afficers will fall under this categary.
(r) "Any one who personally carries on or engages in any activity
means one who constantly carries on activity (not limited to physical labor)
under any form whatever, whether paid or unpaid.
(g) A shareholder, contributor of an organ or a person who is no
more than a mere organizer of an organization will not come under "a person
who carries on or engages in any activity.".
(h) In case a person who falls under the category of this article
has been elected at the general membership meeting, the election itself will
be valid. However, in case the election regulations provide that if an
elected person has not given notification of his declination within a. certain
period after he assumes the post of officer, said person will have to give
such notice.
2. Matters regarding article 95-2
This article, in place of the provisions of old article 95-2
so far specifying that the administrative agency may issue a dissolution
order, was amended to provide that the court issues the dissolution order
upon the application of administrative authorities. In this article, "In
case any coop has conducted the business other than specified in article 10"
will not involve the case where it carries on activity granted under other
law, for example agricultural warehouse activity.
A cooperative will be dissolved only on a dissolution order
by the court.
